13 Model Serving

Lecture slides for this chapter: Part 1 · Part 2

Purpose

Architecture stack focus for this chapter.

Why does serving change the optimization priorities that made training successful?

Training and serving pursue opposing operating objectives. Training maximizes aggregate throughput across large, scheduled batches and long runs, whereas serving must answer individual, heterogeneous requests under strict latency deadlines. Training amortizes hardware initialization and weight streaming across millions of examples; serving pays its full fixed cost on every query, and small inefficiencies accumulate into machine time that must be provisioned. A system handling one billion requests a day recovers more than eleven days of machine time every day from a single millisecond saved per request. Each request also runs inside a fixed end-to-end latency budget, in which network ingress, serialization, preprocessing, queueing, and postprocessing all spend time the model cannot use. When those stages consume most of the budget, kernel optimizations barely reach the user, and a prediction that arrives after the client has timed out has no value however accurate it is. Serving is therefore a capacity problem under uncontrolled traffic as much as a latency problem. Arrival rates swing between daily peaks and troughs, so capacity provisioned for the average collapses at the peak, while capacity provisioned for the peak sits idle and costly the rest of the day. The question a serving system answers is not whether the model computes correct outputs in isolation but whether it sustains reliable, low-latency execution under production variability. In D·A·M terms, serving is where the trained algorithm meets unpredictable live data inside the machine’s latency budget, and all three constraints bind on every request.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain serving inversion from training throughput to per-request latency, headroom, and tail behavior
  • Decompose request latency across serialization, preprocessing, inference, queuing, postprocessing, and network overhead
  • Apply queueing laws and simple queue models to plan capacity and admission control against percentile latency targets
  • Select batching windows and inference runtimes that trade formation delay and precision for throughput within a latency budget
  • Diagnose cold-start delays from weight transfer, runtime initialization, graph compilation, and warmup during model loading
  • Evaluate LLM serving bottlenecks using token latency, KV-cache memory, and continuous batching constraints
  • Calculate cost per inference from precision, hardware utilization, replica count, and runtime throughput
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

13.1 The Serving Inversion

The benchmarks of Benchmarking measured models under a load generator that decided when each request arrived, whereas production serving faces live clients that no scheduler controls. When a user uploads a photograph and waits for its label, or a chat interface sends a prompt and waits for the first word of the reply, the request arrives stochastically and must return within a service-level objective (SLO) of tens to hundreds of milliseconds. Moving from offline training to live traffic therefore triggers the serving inversion, a reversal of engineering priorities: offline training packs large batches to amortize weight transfers from high-bandwidth memory (HBM) and maximize work completed per hour, whereas online serving is judged by whether each individual request completes before its deadline while live traffic tests the operating envelope continuously.

That priority shift restructures the balance of terms across both levels of the iron law of ML systems. Shrinking a task from a large training batch to small batches or individual inputs (\(\BatchSize=1\)) slashes the Data factor (\(\text{Samples/Task}\) in equation 1.2) and inflates the Machine factor (\(\text{Seconds/FLOP}\)). The same shift appears across the unpacked terms of equation 1.3 (\(\Time = \frac{\DataVolume}{\Bandwidth} + \frac{\Operations}{\PeakRate \cdot \EfficiencyHw} + \Latency\)). For weight-dominated operations, small batches reduce arithmetic intensity and can shift execution from compute-bound matrix multiplications toward memory-bandwidth-bound transfers (\(\DataVolume/\Bandwidth\)). Simultaneously, the latency term \(\Latency\) expands beyond microsecond accelerator kernel launches to include network transit, request serialization, and host orchestration, often becoming the binding constraint on end-to-end response time.

↩︎ The multiplicative, additive, and pipelined formulations of the iron law of ML systems are established in Iron Law of ML Systems.

The inversion reshapes each D·A·M layer at once. In the data layer, the constraint shifts from total dataset volume to the arrival rate, payload freshness, and variable sequence dimensions of live queries. In the algorithm layer, execution becomes strictly forward-only: backpropagation state and optimizer memory vanish from the accelerator, even as dynamic input shapes introduce runtime execution variability into previously static operator graphs. In the machine layer, the operating target shifts from maximum hardware utilization to headroom. Whereas offline training treats an idle accelerator as wasted capital, online serving treats near-saturation as an operational hazard, because stochastic request arrivals make queue lengths and waiting times diverge nonlinearly as utilization approaches 100 percent. Maintaining adequate headroom preserves the spare capacity needed to absorb transient traffic spikes without violating tail-latency percentiles, so serving optimizes completed requests under a strict latency deadline rather than steady-state hardware occupancy.

Meeting that latency deadline governs how engineering teams partition the end-to-end serving stack. Every stage between the network socket and the response draws on the same single allowance, so any millisecond consumed by unoptimized feature retrieval, host serialization, or queueing delays directly subtracts from the execution time available to the neural network itself. Consequently, the central engineering task in model serving is deciding which operations must remain on the synchronous request path, which tasks can be offloaded to asynchronous background pipelines, and how much headroom the system must reserve before tail-latency guarantees collapse under load. Three conditions therefore separate serving from the forward pass that a benchmark times: a per-request deadline, arrivals that no scheduler controls, and a stack in which the model is one stage among several.

Definition 13.1: Model serving

Model serving is the operational phase that provides model predictions to end-users or downstream systems under latency, throughput, availability, and cost constraints.

  1. Significance: Online serving turns training’s priority on sustained hardware efficiency (\(\EfficiencyHw\)) into a request-latency constraint (\(\EndToEndRequestLatency\)), requiring a stack designed around the percentile named by its SLO.
  2. Distinction: Unlike model training, which commonly processes scheduled batches, serving may handle stochastic online requests, synchronized streams, or offline batches.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that serving is “just the forward pass.” In reality, it is a systems problem: model execution is only one component of a stack that may include request routing, load balancing, and data transformation.

The SLO1 turns those three conditions into a single time budget across the end-to-end request pipeline (figure 1). Host-side ingress and input preprocessing (network termination, payload deserialization, and tokenization or feature transformation), neural network evaluation on the accelerator, and output postprocessing with safety validation all draw from that allowance. Any of the three stages can become the binding latency bottleneck, and section 13.3.1 shows that host preprocessing alone can take longer than the forward pass.

1 SLOs in ML serving: An SLO is the internal target, as distinct from the contractual SLA. For ML serving, task quality and inference latency can both contribute to SLOs, creating multidimensional targets where improving one dimension (for example, deploying a larger model for accuracy) can violate another (latency).

↩︎ SLOs, service level agreements (SLAs), and the tail-latency percentiles they constrain are established in Latency and tail latency.

Two services carry the chapter, one per half, and each lighthouse arrives in the regime it represents. The first half follows a ResNet-50 image-classification service on one accelerator node. ResNet-50 is the book’s compute-bound lighthouse. Each forward pass performs about 8.2 GFLOP of arithmetic on 102.2 MB of FP32 weights, so a large training batch keeps the matrix units busy. A served request arrives alone, however, and at batch one the same forward pass spends most of its time on weight traffic and kernel launches rather than arithmetic. Recovering the efficiency that training enjoyed without breaking the deadline is the first half’s problem, and the reference request profile introduced with the latency budget carries the service through queuing, batching, runtime selection, and replica startup. Large language model (LLM) serving then follows a GPT-2 XL chat service, the bandwidth-bound lighthouse whose decoding step Autoregressive decoding traced. Each generated token streams all 3 GB of its FP16 weights from memory, about 0.9 ms on the book’s reference H100, so memory bandwidth sets token latency and the memory each conversation holds sets concurrency. Llama 3 8B, a larger member of the same transformer family, extends that case where the per-conversation cache grows large enough to bound how many requests share the accelerator. DLRM, the capacity-bound lighthouse, appears once as a contrast in which embedding lookups rather than arithmetic dominate a request, and MobileNetV2 appears where the target device cannot hold ResNet-50 at all.

The first four sections in table 1 follow a single ResNet-50 replica from its internal request path through single-request latency, multi-request queuing, and batching; the remaining five change the runtime engine, host-to-device execution path, replica startup, output modality, and fleet economics. Before a team designs any of those layers, however, two placement decisions fix the operating regime: a temporal choice of whether predictions are computed ahead of time or on demand, and a spatial choice of where the model physically runs.

13.1.1 Static vs. dynamic inference

A nightly job that precomputes product recommendations for existing users can pack thousands of items into each accelerator batch, whereas a live content-moderation API must score an uploaded image within milliseconds of arrival. Deciding whether to generate predictions before or during a user request sets the trade-off between offline hardware efficiency and online responsiveness (Google 2024). It determines whether an accelerator runs in an unconstrained throughput regime or under the strict tail-latency deadlines of an interactive serving loop.

Google. 2024. Static Vs. Dynamic Inference. Google Machine Learning Crash Course.

Static inference

Static inference (also called offline or batch inference) precomputes predictions for anticipated inputs and stores them in a retrieval store. Moving model execution entirely out of the user request path alters the underlying hardware constraints. Because batch jobs have no millisecond-level deadlines, accelerators can use large batches (\(\BatchSize\gg 1\)) selected for throughput within memory limits. Reusing weights across samples raises arithmetic intensity for weight-dominated operations. A batch-size sweep identifies where throughput stops improving. When a client issues a request (for example, a user opening nightly generated recommendation rankings), the serving system performs a low-latency key-value lookup rather than executing a forward pass on an accelerator. The primary operational constraint shifts from accelerator latency to storage capacity and data freshness. Precomputing predictions across an entire input domain scales combinatorially (\(\mathcal{O}(\RecommenderUserCount\times \RecommenderItemCount)\) for \(\RecommenderUserCount\) users and \(\RecommenderItemCount\) items in recommendation systems), and stale predictions persist until the next batch refresh.

Dynamic inference

Dynamic inference (also called online or real-time inference) evaluates inputs on demand as requests arrive. On-demand execution accommodates arbitrary, previously unseen inputs within the model schema and immediately reflects newly deployed model weights without requiring precomputation. However, executing the model directly within the user request path subjects inference to the strict end-to-end latency budget set by its SLO. Because user requests arrive stochastically, the serving runtime cannot delay execution indefinitely to accumulate large batches. Serving requests individually or in small batches (\(\BatchSize=1\) to \(\BatchSize=4\)) can expose weight traffic or launch overhead that larger batches amortize. When these costs bind execution, the device completes fewer queries per unit time, increasing provisioned cost per query. This tension between interactive response time and hardware efficiency can make dynamic serving more expensive per query than offline batching (example 13.1).

Example 13.1: The cost of latency
Latency constraints directly dictate infrastructure costs. The figures below come from the ResNet-50 reference profile that section 13.5.1 later sweeps across batch sizes. Consider a GPU server renting for $4/hour.

Scenario A (low latency): Batch size 1.

  • Latency: 3.5 ms.
  • Throughput: 283 req/s.
  • Cost per million queries: $3.92.

Scenario B (high throughput): Batch size 8.

  • Latency: 3.8 ms, barely above batch one, because the fixed launch cost dominates both.
  • Throughput: 2,129 req/s.
  • Cost per million queries: $0.52.

Systems insight: Serving one image at a time saves only the difference between 3.8 ms and 3.5 ms of latency, yet multiplies the hardware cost per million queries by 7.5×. Engineers must quantify whether that speedup generates enough business value to justify the 7.5× cost increase.

Consider two contrasting deployment scenarios for a ResNet-50 image classifier. A static batch approach suits a photo management application that preclassifies every image in a user library overnight. Classifying 10,000 photos one at a time, at 3.5 ms per batch-one forward pass on the reference H100, would take 35 s. At the 89,500 images/s that MLPerf Inference (Reddi et al. 2019) measured for a compiled INT8 engine at large batch on the same accelerator, the same library takes 0.11 s, about 320× faster. Clients then experience instantaneous retrieval upon browsing. Conversely, a dynamic online approach suits a content moderation service that must evaluate user-uploaded media in real time, where each image traverses the complete preprocessing, accelerator execution, and postprocessing pipeline within a 100 ms service-level objective.

These two modes optimize for different operating regimes. Static inference maximizes accelerator utilization and amortizes memory transfers across large, offline batches, trading persistent storage capacity for zero serving latency at query time. Dynamic inference minimizes tail latency under stochastic, concurrent traffic, accepting lower compute efficiency and higher marginal cost per query to evaluate arbitrary, unpredictable inputs. Production architectures frequently combine both paradigms in a tiered design. Static precomputation populates key-value caches with predictions for high-frequency queries, such as catalog items or viral media, while dynamic serving clusters evaluate the long tail of novel or personalized requests. Deciding when predictions are computed resolves only the temporal dimension of serving, however. Equally binding is the spatial dimension, where the model physically executes, because physical placement dictates memory hierarchy limits, network boundaries, and the hardware budget available to the serving stack.

13.1.2 The spectrum of serving architectures

A cloud server can pool accelerators behind an HTTP endpoint at the cost of a network round trip and multi-second cold starts, whereas a hearing aid or industrial sensor microcontroller cannot afford a network hop or an operating system at all. Although “serving” often implies a networked server processing API requests, the four deployment paradigms (Cloud, Edge, Mobile, and TinyML) and the physical constraints that give rise to them—the light barrier, the power wall, and the memory wall—shape the serving stack into different forms. Serving spans a continuum. At the cloud end, centralized networked microservices pool accelerators to optimize throughput and cost per query, at the expense of network round-trip overhead and cold-start latency. Further along, application-embedded edge serving runs models inside client processes via direct function calls, which eliminates network latency and preserves privacy at the expense of strict device memory and power limits. At the far end, bare-metal firmware execution runs on microcontrollers. Those hardware limits do not disappear at serving time; serving adds latency SLOs and cost pressure on top of limits that training could absorb through patience. The same model may therefore require different serving strategies depending on where it runs.

↩︎ The light barrier, power wall, and memory wall that give rise to the four deployment paradigms are established in Physical Constraints on Deployment.

The three architectures differ in what binds. Networked serving runs the model as a standalone service behind HTTP or gRPC, so network transfer and serialization join model execution as candidate bottlenecks. Data-center accelerators support batching and concurrency, but a new replica’s cold start can stretch from seconds to minutes because container startup, model loading, and warmup sit outside the steady-state request path. Application-embedded serving runs the model inside the user’s application process on a phone or embedded board, behind a function call rather than a network interface. With no shared server to batch for, optimization targets energy and responsiveness (the shape that benchmarking calls SingleStream), and a passively cooled envelope of a few watts throttles sustained inference once the chassis heats. Bare-metal serving compiles the model into microcontroller firmware, often without an operating system or a dynamic allocator, so weights execute in place from flash memory and activations fit a preallocated tensor arena of a few hundred kilobytes of SRAM. Batching is impossible on a single sensor stream.

↩︎ The MLPerf SingleStream, MultiStream, Server, and Offline scenarios are established in MLPerf execution scenarios.

Embedded execution enables zero-copy inference. When data moves through a system, each copy consumes CPU cycles and memory bandwidth. In cloud serving, separate physical memory spaces force repeated transfers. A camera frame passes from a network socket buffer to user space, through a preprocessing buffer into page-locked host DRAM, and across PCIe into accelerator memory. A mobile pipeline can eliminate these intermediate copies when its camera, image signal processor (ISP), CPU, and NPU exchange shared buffer descriptors in unified system memory. In unified-memory system-on-chip (SoC) architectures, such as Apple A- and M-series processors or Qualcomm Snapdragon, all compute engines share a single physical DRAM pool. When software passes buffer handles rather than duplicating bytes, inference latency drops and memory bus power decreases, although format conversions may still require CPU or ISP intervention.

The deployment tiers differ first in their operating envelopes (latency targets, memory, and power), which Physical Constraints on Deployment derived and tabulated for each paradigm. What serving adds is the model each tier can actually run. Applying those envelopes to one image-classification workload, table 2 shows why cloud and mobile can adapt ResNet-50 while TinyML must substitute MobileNetV2. The cloud column runs the model through TensorRT, NVIDIA’s ahead-of-time inference compiler,2 and the mobile column through the TensorFlow Lite runtime. The chapter uses these engines as given until section 13.6 examines what such runtimes do to a model.

2 TensorRT: TensorRT builds its engine ahead of time for one microarchitecture (such as Hopper or Blackwell) (NVIDIA 2024a). That target-specific build phase allows the compiler to generate fused kernels, select microarchitecture-specific Tensor Core execution paths, and optimize memory layouts (such as NC/32HW32). The resulting nonportable engine reduces per-request execution latency and minimizes the accelerator count needed to satisfy a target throughput.

NVIDIA. 2024a. NVIDIA TensorRT: Programmable Inference Accelerator.

Systems Perspective 13.1: ResNet-50 across the serving spectrum
Systems insight: It is tempting to read table 2 as one model deployed three ways. That reading is misleading: the cloud and mobile tiers share the ResNet-50 graph but use different precision and runtimes, while the TinyML tier cannot run ResNet-50 at all and substitutes an architecture designed for its constraints. Treating these as one model hides the work that makes each deployment possible.

Whichever tier hosts it, a networked deployment runs the model inside a server process, and each such replica must turn an irregular stream of requests into work its accelerator can execute efficiently. That conversion is the job of the inference server’s internal architecture.

Self-Check: Question

  1. When transitioning a deep learning model from training to an online serving environment, how does the system constraint in the D·A·M (Data-Algorithm-Machine) taxonomy fundamentally shift regarding machine utilization and algorithm state?

    1. Machine utilization must be maximized at 100% to amortize capital costs, while the algorithm continues parameter backpropagation during request serving.
    2. Machine capacity must maintain operational headroom below saturation to prevent tail-latency queueing collapse, while the algorithm’s weights remain fixed for a deployed version.
    3. Machine allocation switches from accelerators to general-purpose CPUs exclusively, while the algorithm dynamically modifies its architecture per incoming user payload.
    4. Machine throughput replaces latency as the primary operational constraint, while data volume shifts from live request streams to historical offline batches.

  2. True or False: In online model serving, the latency term (\(L_{\text{lat}}\)) in the iron law of ML systems must encompass external factors such as network round-trip time, request serialization, and system orchestration, rather than solely the accelerator kernel execution time.

  4. An e-commerce platform evaluates precomputing product recommendations offline (static inference) versus computing them on demand when a user visits the homepage (dynamic inference). Which trade-off correctly characterizes static inference compared to dynamic inference?

    1. Static inference eliminates database storage costs but increases real-time p99 latency by executing large batched matrix multiplications during user page loads.
    2. Static inference guarantees real-time contextual adaptation to intra-session user clicks but requires dedicated high-end GPUs on the critical request path.
    3. Static inference trades storage capacity and potential prediction staleness for retrieval latency that avoids per-request model execution, whereas dynamic inference provides fresh predictions from live context at the expense of computational latency and variable serving capacity.

13.2 Serving System Architecture

Inside each replica, user requests arrive in irregular bursts—milliseconds apart one moment and silent for seconds the next—whereas accelerator compute engines demand steady streams of uniform, dense batches to saturate memory bandwidth and arithmetic units. Reconciling these two incompatible rhythms at the system boundary requires a serving architecture that decouples network ingress from execution scheduling, absorbing arrival bursts and packing irregular inputs into dense batches without violating latency SLOs.

13.2.1 Internal architecture and request flow

If a web server invoked the accelerator synchronously every time a socket read completed, slow client uploads would stall the GPU and concurrent connections would contend for device memory. An inference server3 (such as NVIDIA Triton or TensorFlow Serving) avoids this by acting as a specialized runtime scheduler that manages concurrency, device memory, and data movement between host CPUs and accelerator devices. Rather than running the model directly on the network thread, the server pipelines incoming requests through dedicated subsystems that isolate client network handling from accelerator compute. Every request traverses a six-stage runtime pipeline (figure 2) from network ingress and request-buffering queues through the dynamic batcher to the inference runner and hardware accelerator.

3 Inference server: Google’s TensorFlow Serving (Olston et al. 2017) helped establish the separation of model logic from serving infrastructure; NVIDIA’s Triton (NVIDIA 2024b) extends this pattern across multiple model frameworks and backends. The critical design insight is that a scheduler and dynamic batcher turn irregular single-request traffic into accelerator-friendly execution, improving utilization when latency budgets allow batching. Exact utilization gains depend on model, hardware, arrival rate, and the configured batching window.

Olston, Christopher, Noah Fiedel, Kiril Gorovoy, Jeremiah Harmsen, Li Lao, Fangwei Li, Vinu Rajashekhar, Sukriti Ramesh, and Jordan Soyke. 2017. “TensorFlow-Serving: Flexible, High-Performance ML Serving.” CoRR abs/1712.06139. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.1712.06139.
NVIDIA. 2024b. NVIDIA Triton Inference Server.

Across the stages in figure 2, the server handles concurrency management, request transformation, model lifecycle management, and execution scheduling. At network ingress, the server manages concurrency through asynchronous event loops (such as epoll or kqueue) and worker thread pools that handle thousands of concurrent client TCP connections without stalling accelerator execution pipelines. Decoupling network I/O from compute ensures that slow client transfers or network stalls do not starve the accelerator of ready work.

Once a payload arrives, the server transforms requests into tensors, decoding serialized network bytes into contiguous, aligned memory buffers that match the tensor layout required by the underlying execution engine. For example, four-dimensional vision tensors typically adopt NCHW4 or NHWC formats. On modern accelerators, matrix multiplication units (such as Tensor Cores) load 8- or 16-element channel vectors using coalesced memory transactions that favor channels-last (NHWC) layout. If the execution kernel requires a different physical layout, the server must materialize that layout. Calling torch.permute merely creates a view with reordered strides; an explicit memory copy or transpose kernel must physically reorder the data in memory. Because such materialization performs zero arithmetic (\(\ArithmeticIntensity\approx 0\) FLOP/byte), memory traffic and launch overhead dominate its cost. A device-local conversion consumes device memory bandwidth, whereas conversions across the host-device boundary consume PCIe bandwidth.

4 NCHW and NHWC (tensor memory layouts): These acronyms encode the dimension order of 4D image tensors: N (batch), C (channels), H (height), and W (width). In a contiguous NCHW tensor, each channel plane is contiguous; in NHWC, channels for one spatial position are adjacent. Optimized kernels may favor either layout, and a semantic mismatch can scramble values even when the element count is correct. Serving code should therefore make both dimension meaning and any physical-layout conversion explicit.

For those tensors to execute without startup stalls, the server also manages the model’s local lifecycle. The runtime establishes the CUDA context before allocating device memory (VRAM) and streaming weights onto the accelerator. It also pins host memory buffers for direct memory access (DMA) transfers and executes representative warmup passes to trigger deferred compilation and memory allocation before admitting live client traffic. While enterprise model registries, canary rollouts, and rollback policies belong to cluster-level operations (ML Operations), the serving engine governs local state, validating that active model instances are warmed, resident in accelerator memory, and ready for dispatch.

Between request buffering and the warmed model sits the execution scheduler, which coordinates accelerator dispatch by implementing dynamic batching (section 13.5). For weight-dominated inference at batch size \(\BatchSize=1\), streaming parameters from HBM can limit execution before the arithmetic units reach peak throughput. Grouping \(\BatchSize\) requests into a concurrent batch amortizes the byte transfers of reading model weights across all \(\BatchSize\) inputs, increasing the arithmetic intensity (\(\ArithmeticIntensity= \Operations/\DataVolume\)).

To balance individual request latency against batch compute efficiency, the scheduler tunes queue residency using a batching window (or timeout threshold). Setting the batching window to zero removes deliberate batch formation delay, but requests can still wait for an available worker. An immediately dispatched small batch may also expose too little work to use the accelerator efficiently. Conversely, setting a bounded window (such as 2 to 5 ms) allows the scheduler to buffer arriving requests until either the queue reaches a target batch size or the timeout expires. The window bounds deliberate formation delay; waiting for a busy worker is a separate resource-queue delay. Batching trades that formation time for greater weight reuse and potentially higher service capacity, while total latency includes both waits and execution. Before a request ever reaches those internal queues, however, it must cross the network boundary into the API gateway.

13.2.2 Interface protocols and serialization

At that network boundary, a request’s wire representation can cost more CPU time than the model itself spends on the accelerator. As device kernels undergo aggressive compilation (via TensorRT, TorchDynamo, or custom kernels), forward-pass execution often drops below a single millisecond, leaving payload ingestion, transport, and deserialization as a major share of end-to-end latency (quantified stage by stage in section 13.3.2.1). Two interface choices, the wire serialization format and the network transport protocol, determine whether that host ingress path stays in microseconds or consumes the latency budget before inference begins.

The serialization bottleneck

Machine learning payloads differ from conventional web API payloads. They consist of multi-dimensional arrays of floating-point numbers (such as image tensors, dense embedding vectors, or token sequence logits). While text-based JSON is ubiquitous in web services, serializing dense numerical arrays into text incurs severe CPU and memory penalties. Serialization overhead manifests because JSON parsers must scan every byte sequentially, validate syntax, allocate heap nodes, and invoke string-to-float conversion routines (such as strtod). Each 32-bit float expands from 4 raw bytes into 7 to 15 ASCII characters including punctuation. Furthermore, raw binary data (such as compressed JPEG or raw image bytes) requires Base64 encoding to travel within JSON strings, imposing a 33 percent size inflation before parsing begins.

↩︎ Clipper’s decomposition of serving latency, in which serialization rivals model execution, is established in End-to-end vs. component latency.

5 Protocol Buffers (Protobuf): Protobuf uses a predefined schema (from a .proto file) to encode structured data into a compact binary format (Protocol Buffers Authors 2026). Because the schema carries field names and types, the wire payload need not repeat them as JSON does. Its wire format is still not identical to a C++ object’s in-memory layout, so it requires a parsing step and does not provide the same direct zero-copy access pattern that FlatBuffers targets.

Protocol Buffers Authors. 2026. Overview: Protocol Buffers.

6 FlatBuffers: The “flat” in the name describes the design: the binary buffer can serve as the serialized representation and the data structure being read, avoiding a separate parsing or unpacking phase for supported access patterns (FlatBuffers Authors 2026). For ML inference, this can enable zero-copy access to tensor metadata, because the serving system reads tensor shapes and offsets directly from the buffer rather than allocating a second object representation.

FlatBuffers Authors. 2026. FlatBuffers Documentation.

Binary serialization formats, such as Protocol Buffers5 and FlatBuffers,6 bypass this text conversion cost. Float arrays transmit directly as packed IEEE 754 byte streams with zero ASCII conversion and zero Base64 framing. Furthermore, FlatBuffers organizes serialized records with internal relative offsets, enabling zero-copy deserialization. The serving application reads tensor dimensions, data types, and byte offsets directly out of the network receive buffer without allocating an intermediate object graph on the heap.

REST vs. gRPC

Transport protocol selection governs connection life cycles and socket multiplexing. Conventional REST APIs typically deploy HTTP/1.1 with JSON payloads. HTTP/1.1 clients commonly use multiple persistent TCP connections for concurrency. Establishing each connection incurs handshake and, for HTTPS, TLS costs, but keep-alive reuse amortizes setup across requests. HTTP/1.1 also suffers from request-level head-of-line blocking, because responses on a single connection must return in request order. While REST APIs can adopt HTTP/2 or binary encodings, the combination of HTTP/1.1 and JSON creates significant socket overhead and CPU parsing contention when serving high-throughput tensor workloads.

In contrast, gRPC (gRPC Remote Procedure Call)7 pairs HTTP/2 transport with Protocol Buffers serialization. HTTP/2 establishes a single long-lived TCP connection and multiplexes hundreds of concurrent request and response streams simultaneously using binary framing. This architecture multiplexes concurrent streams over a persistent connection and removes HTTP-level response-order blocking (a lost TCP packet can still stall every stream on the connection, which HTTP/3 addresses by moving to QUIC). Protobuf, meanwhile, enforces strongly typed contracts and serializes binary tensor payloads efficiently. Consequently, gRPC is the standard protocol for internal microservice communication in model serving clusters, where low latency, compact wire payloads, and bidirectional token streaming are primary design criteria. Even a modest embedding payload shows the gap in wire footprint and host parsing time.

7 gRPC (gRPC remote procedure call): The gRPC framework pairs HTTP/2 transport with an interface definition language and message format, most commonly Protocol Buffers (gRPC Authors 2026). The size and latency benefit over REST/JSON depends on payload shape, client/server implementation, and whether serialization is a meaningful share of the end-to-end latency budget.

gRPC Authors. 2026. Introduction to gRPC.

Napkin Math 13.1: JSON vs. Protobuf serialization
Consider a request payload containing 1,000 floating point numbers (for example, an embedding vector).

  • JSON: Uses ~9 KB on the wire. Requires ~50 μs to parse.
  • Protobuf: Uses ~4 KB on the wire. Requires ~5 μs to parse.

Math: Switching one request to the binary payload saves 50 μs − 5 μs = 45 μs of parse time. For this illustrative system processing 10,000 requests per second, the savings compound to 10,000 \(\times\) 45 μs = 0.45 s of CPU time reclaimed every wall-clock second, which is 45 percent of one core freed from serialization overhead alone.

Systems insight: This 10× scenario gain makes gRPC/Protobuf, FlatBuffers, or another binary protocol a strong candidate for high-throughput internal microservices when serialization is a visible part of the latency budget.

Architectural selection is governed by system constraints. REST over HTTP/1.1 remains practical for public-facing edge gateways where broad client compatibility and human-readable debugging take precedence over raw wire efficiency. Within cluster boundaries, gRPC over HTTP/2 with binary serialization can reduce overhead when high query volume or tensor serialization consumes a significant share of the host CPU budget; its streaming support also suits incremental token responses.

13.2.3 The load balancer layer

When traffic exceeds what a single machine can handle, adding model replicas does not prevent tail-latency spikes if requests still land on a replica that is mid-batch or still loading weights into GPU memory. Cloud and data center deployments therefore place a load balancer between clients and model servers to route around busy or unready replicas. A serving load balancer protects the fleet by coupling request distribution with readiness checks and traffic shifting. It routes incoming requests across replicas using policies such as round-robin or least-connections, so that traffic steers around slow or saturated workers before queues build. It also backs that routing with health monitoring that inspects both process liveness and model initialization state, confirming that weights are loaded and warmup passes are complete before a replica receives live queries. The same routing control also supports progressive rollouts, shifting traffic gradually between model versions rather than cutting over abruptly.

↪ Shadow, canary, and blue-green release strategies that build on this traffic shifting are developed in Model deployment.

On a single machine running several model instances, such as several Open Neural Network Exchange (ONNX) Runtime sessions, the serving framework and the operating system queue requests without a separate balancer. A dedicated balancer becomes necessary once replicas span machines, and its distribution algorithms belong to that distributed setting. This chapter therefore plans capacity one node at a time. The unit of capacity is the sustained model throughput of one node. The queueing analysis in section 13.4 determines the utilization at which that node can no longer meet its latency target and a second replica must be added behind the balancer. Protocol selection, process architecture, and the balancer in front of the replicas establish the structural skeleton of a serving deployment. Evaluating actual operational performance requires tracing how latency accumulates as one request traverses each stage of a replica’s runtime pipeline.

Self-Check: Question
  1. Order the sequential stages of a request passing through a high-performance decoupled inference serving pipeline from initial arrival to client response:
  1. Hardware Accelerator Execution (tensor kernel execution on GPU/NPU)
  2. Dynamic Batcher (aggregating incoming items up to max batch size or timeout)
  3. Network Ingress & Gateway (TLS termination, authentication, protocol deserialization)
  4. Postprocessing & Response Dispatch (logit normalization, top-\(k\) filtering, response serialization)
  5. Request Queue (absorbing traffic bursts and buffering pending requests)
  6. Inference Runner / Engine (tensor memory management, CUDA stream scheduling)

  1. In a microservices architecture where measurements show JSON serialization and parsing are a visible bottleneck in an internal fraud detection service processing \(10^5\) queries per second under a 5 ms SLA, why is gRPC with Protocol Buffers preferred over HTTP/1.1 with JSON?

    1. JSON text parsing is performed directly inside GPU Tensor Cores, whereas Protocol Buffers require host CPU preprocessing.
    2. HTTP/1.1 provides automatic iteration-level continuous batching, whereas gRPC only supports static batching.
    3. Protocol Buffers eliminate the need for schema definitions, enabling zero-copy dynamic typing across heterogeneous languages.
    4. Protocol Buffers use compact binary encoding with pre-compiled schema parsers that can reduce CPU serialization overhead, and gRPC multiplexes requests over persistent HTTP/2 connections.

  2. What is the ‘serialization bottleneck’ in model serving, and under what operational conditions (model size, request payload, query rate) does it become the dominant factor in the end-to-end latency budget?

  3. True or False: Replacing HTTP/1.1 REST endpoints with gRPC automatically accelerates the GPU kernel execution time of deep learning models by \(2\times\) to \(4\times\).

  4. In an inference server architecture, what is the primary role of decoupling the request queue from the dynamic batcher?

    1. It allows the server to permanently cache all intermediate GPU activations across independent client requests.
    2. It allows the system to buffer irregular arrivals and present the batcher with pending work so it can form batches within a timeout budget.
    3. It eliminates the need for GPU kernel compilation by converting dynamic input shapes into fixed static tensors in the queue.
    4. It bypasses host RAM by streaming network packets directly from the NIC into the accelerator’s L2 cache via PCIe peer-to-peer transfers.

  5. A high-throughput model serving cluster sits behind a Layer 7 load balancer. Which routing approach is most appropriate for reducing p99 tail latency when inference times vary significantly across requests due to variable input sequence lengths?

    1. A load-aware policy such as least-connections or least-loaded routing, which steers new requests away from replicas that already appear busy or backlogged.
    2. Static round-robin routing, which deterministically rotates incoming connections across all healthy replicas regardless of current queue depths.
    3. Random routing with hash-based sticky IP assignment, which pins client IP addresses permanently to specific backends without monitoring load.
    4. Maximum-throughput greedy allocation, which concentrates all incoming traffic onto a single primary replica until its memory is completely saturated before spilling over.

13.3 Request Lifecycle

When a single HTTP request carrying a \(224{\times}224\) JPEG image arrives at an inference server, the clock starts on the client’s deadline as soon as the first byte enters the network stack and stops only when the classification payload departs. Between those two boundaries, the request must be decoded on the host, copied across the interconnect, evaluated on the accelerator, and formatted into a response, with every stage drawing from the same millisecond allowance.

13.3.1 The latency budget

A vision service that completes thousands of classifications per second still fails its users if individual requests take two seconds to return and miss their interactive deadlines. For dynamic inference systems, the serving inversion established in section 13.1 therefore imposes a strict latency budget across the entire request path (Gujarati et al. 2020).

8 Tail latency: Unlike averages, percentile latencies reveal the performance impact of system outliers common in ML serving, such as model cache misses or garbage collection pauses. These rare, high-latency requests can disproportionately harm user satisfaction and business outcomes. Because the magnitude is workload-specific, services validate percentile targets (p95, p99) against their own users and SLOs.

Relevant metrics expand from aggregate throughput and mean latency to the latency distribution. Mean and p50 describe the center, while p95 and p99 expose the slow tail. If mean latency is 50 ms but p99 is two seconds, the p99 threshold is 40× the mean, so about 1 percent of requests are at or beyond two seconds. Whether that tail matters depends on the service and its users, which is why percentile targets should come from the SLO rather than convention.8 Managing these percentile constraints requires decomposing the total allowed response time into a latency budget that allocates time across each processing phase, the serial accounting that the latency budget principle (12) states formally.

Definition 13.2: Latency budget

Latency budget is the time allocated to request stages under an end-to-end deadline; the SLO also specifies the required completion quantile.

  1. Significance: It acts as a zero-sum constraint system where any milliseconds consumed by serialization or network overhead directly reduce the latency budget (\(\EndToEndRequestLatency\)) available for model inference.
  2. Distinction: The budget allocates time across stages, while the latency SLO of Latency and tail latency couples the deadline (for example, \(<50\text{ ms}\)) with a completion quantile (\(\text{p99}\) or \(\text{p99.9}\)). A service-level indicator (SLI) is the measured quantity used to evaluate that objective. Tail latency spikes can stem from queueing, batch formation, cache state, interference, or data movement.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that the “model” has the entire budget. In the illustrative breakdown in table 3, the remainder is consumed by host preprocessing and data movement (JPEG decode, resize, normalization, and PCIe transfer), while upstream network and serialization overheads (table 5) consume further margin.

Inference is one slice of the latency budget; preprocessing rivals it.

Every serving request traverses three sequential phases that share this latency budget. Preprocessing converts raw input such as image bytes or text strings into model-ready tensors, inference executes the model computation over those tensors, and postprocessing turns the resulting device outputs into user-facing responses. Accelerating model execution alone yields diminishing returns when host-side preprocessing and response formatting consume the majority of the latency budget, an immediate manifestation of Amdahl’s law. As hardware compilers compress accelerator kernel runtimes into sub-millisecond durations, host-device coordination and memory transfers emerge as the primary latency bottlenecks.

13.3.2 Stage-by-stage latency breakdown

An engineer who measures only total request duration in a vision service sees a single millisecond number that lumps together CPU-bound JPEG decompression, host-to-device PCIe transfers, accelerator kernel execution, and response formatting. It is then easy to spend weeks optimizing GPU kernels that account for less than half of total response time. Timing each pipeline boundary independently isolates the actual bottleneck before optimization begins.

Example 13.2: ResNet-50: Latency budget breakdown
Table 3 details the per-phase latency breakdown for one ResNet-50 serving request on the reference H100, with the forward pass taken from the chapter’s modeled profile and the host phases as stated assumptions.

Systems insight: In this ResNet-50 serving budget, preprocessing consumes 52 percent of latency even though the forward pass performs nearly all of the arithmetic. If a compiled TensorRT engine reduces the forward pass to the modeled 0.33 ms, preprocessing would dominate at 83 percent.

Laid on the node’s hardware, the same budget occupies three resources in turn, and the accelerator is busy for less than half of the request (figure 3). While a host CPU core decompresses the JPEG, resizes the image, and normalizes pixel values before copying the tensor across PCIe, the GPU waits completely idle for work to arrive.

The 3.5 ms batch-one forward pass in table 3 is the chapter’s reference profile for ResNet-50: an eager framework running FP16 on the reference H100, modeled from the iron law’s terms rather than measured, and the later ResNet-50 examples derive from it. The framework issues 174 kernels, one per operation, and each pays 10 μs of Python dispatch and 5 μs of driver launch, the per-operation costs of Dispatch overhead in eager execution. Each kernel also occupies the accelerator for at least an assumed 5 μs, and the arithmetic itself, at the 25 percent of peak that ResNet-50 sustains in Data pipeline, takes about 33 μs per image. A compiled INT8 engine anchors the other end of the range: MLPerf Inference measured 89,500 images/s on one H100 at large batch (Reddi et al. 2019). The capacity plan in section 13.4.3 uses the reference profile as the service time, the batching sweep in section 13.5.1 starts from it at batch one, and the cold-start timeline in section 13.8.1 assumes its warm steady state. The runtime and precision comparisons in section 13.6 change one term of the same model at a time, so the effect of each change is visible.

In the ResNet-50 scenario, dense convolutional arithmetic dominates the model stage’s work. That does not make the stage compute bound at every batch size. At batch size one on the reference H100, issuing the kernels takes 2.6 ms, 74 percent of the forward pass, so the stage is overhead-bound and runs far below peak arithmetic throughput. Batching amortizes that fixed cost and fills the machine (section 13.5.1). Changing the model architecture can move the dominant cost out of dense arithmetic entirely, and recommendation systems make this shift concrete.

Lighthouse 13.1: Lighthouse example: DLRM serving

Scenario: Serving DLRM with a 10 ms p99 latency budget.

Analysis: While ResNet-50’s model stage is dominated by convolutional neural network compute, DLRM’s dominant model-stage cost is embedding-table memory access. End-to-end serving bottlenecks still require measuring the full path: preprocessing, inference, postprocessing, and data movement. Table 4 breaks the recommendation request down by phase.

Systems insight: In DLRM, the “Inference” multilayer perceptron stage is only ~17 percent of the latency. The majority of time is spent in embedding lookups, retrieving 128-dimensional embedding vectors from tables that hold about 100 GB in the reference model and reach terabytes in production. This is a memory-bandwidth and capacity-bound workload where adding more compute does not help unless the embedding tables can be served faster.

Together, table 3 and table 4 expose the same general failure mode. Optimization effort goes where ML expertise applies (model quantization, pruning) while the binding constraint sits elsewhere (image decoding on CPU for ResNet-50, embedding-table memory bandwidth for DLRM). For an isolated serial request with other stage times held fixed, model-only optimization is bounded by the time spent outside the model, the Amdahl ceiling that End-to-end vs. component latency derives for a request path. Serving modifies that familiar result in two ways. Accelerator progress tightens the ceiling, because each hardware generation shrinks the model term while the host stages stay where they were; the perspective below quantifies this for the reference request. Under load, a faster model also shortens the queue, an effect the fixed-fraction calculation cannot see and the first fallacy in section 13.11 develops.

Systems Perspective 13.2: The quantitative approach to serving
Analysis: Amdahl’s law (principle 8) sets this ceiling (Strong scaling (Amdahl's Law) provides the formal derivation). Preprocessing (4.5 ms) and data transfer (0.5 ms) consume 58 percent of total latency. Optimizing the model 10\(\times\) faster (3.5 ms → 0.35 ms) yields only a 1.6× end-to-end speedup (reducing latency from 8.6 ms to 5.5 ms). Focusing exclusively on model optimizations like quantization or pruning yields diminishing returns when non-model stages dominate execution time.

Mechanism: Domain-specific accelerators, such as the Tensor Processing Unit (TPU) and Tensor Cores, replace complex control logic with dense multiply-accumulate arrays, which shrinks the model term. The host-side stages stay on general-purpose CPUs and I/O buses, so the faster the accelerator, the larger their share of end-to-end latency.

Systems insight: Profile before optimizing. If preprocessing dominates, GPU-accelerated pipelines (NVIDIA DALI) that move preprocessing next to the accelerator may outperform model quantization, although the end-to-end gain is pipeline-specific.

Per-request overheads outside the model

Beyond model forward-pass execution, every request pays fixed overheads to the host system and network stack. When an inference query arrives, it traverses the network interface, moves across kernel and user space, deserializes from wire protocols into memory buffers, queues for batch assembly, and copies across the PCIe interconnect to accelerator device memory. These host-side operations run on general-purpose CPUs and I/O buses, which is why their share grows as the model term shrinks. Each overhead has a representative cost, a scaling behavior, and a design choice that reduces it (table 5).

The killer microseconds problem

Barroso, Patterson, and colleagues identified a gap in how computer systems handle latency across time scales (Barroso et al. 2017). Nanosecond-scale stalls (such as cache misses) are hidden by hardware instruction-level parallelism, while millisecond-scale delays (such as disk I/O or network RPCs) are hidden by operating system thread context switching. Operations in the microsecond range (1–100 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\)) fall into the gap between these two mechanisms. They are too long to spin-wait on a CPU core without wasting compute cycles and power, yet too short for the operating system to context-switch to another thread without paying scheduling overheads that exceed the wait time itself.

Barroso, Luiz, Mike Marty, David Patterson, and Parthasarathy Ranganathan. 2017. “Attack of the Killer Microseconds.” Communications of the ACM 60 (4): 48–54. https://doi.org/10.1145/3015146.

Serving infrastructure operates directly in this killer microseconds regime. Using the representative ranges in table 5, serialization at 50–500 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\), dispatch at 10–50 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\), and data copy at 100–500 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) are each individually small. For the 3.5 ms reference forward pass, however, these named microsecond-scale overheads collectively add about 5 percent to 30 percent of the latency budget before network and queuing delays are counted. No single overhead justifies optimization in isolation, yet together they determine whether the system meets its SLO.

Reducing this compound overhead requires changing the architecture across the software-hardware interface. When microsecond delays accumulate across serialization, kernel dispatch, and memory copies, isolated optimizations to individual functions yield negligible improvements. Instead, high-performance serving runtimes remove the boundaries themselves. They use zero-copy memory buffers between networking and execution runtimes, maintain persistent CUDA streams to bypass driver dispatch latencies, and migrate preprocessing kernels directly onto the accelerator to avoid intermediate host-device transfers. Eliminating those fixed boundary taxes leaves the portion of preprocessing and execution that scales with the dimensions of the input payload itself.

13.3.3 Resolution and input size trade-offs

Doubling an image’s side length from \(224{\times}224\) to \(448{\times}448\) quadruples the pixel count that the host must decode and that the convolutional layers must process, making input resolution a primary serving control as well as a model property. Model architectures are often evaluated at fixed resolutions, but the serving runtime must ingest variable client inputs and rescale them before kernel execution. In convolution-dominated vision networks, floating-point operations scale quadratically with spatial dimensions (\(\mathcal{O}(\ImageHeight \cdot \ImageWidth)\)), whereas static costs (such as weight streaming from accelerator memory and kernel launch overheads) remain invariant. Varying input resolution therefore shifts the operational arithmetic intensity across the roofline boundary. Compute-bound stages slow down quadratically as pixel count expands, whereas memory-bound stages scale with activation memory footprint.

↩︎ The roofline boundary and arithmetic intensity ridge points that separate compute-bound from memory-bound stages are established in Performance Bounds.

For convolution-dominated, compute-bound models, equation 1 gives a first-order approximation in which throughput scales inversely with resolution squared, comparing two input resolutions \(\InputResolution_1\) and \(\InputResolution_2\) (image side lengths in pixels): \[\frac{\text{Throughput}(\InputResolution_2)}{\text{Throughput}(\InputResolution_1)} = \left(\frac{\InputResolution_1}{\InputResolution_2}\right)^2 \tag{1}\]

Doubling resolution from 224 to 448 gives a 4× compute-scaling estimate, whereas the illustrative 3.6× scenario includes fixed overhead. Table 6 shows the mechanism behind that gap. Each step up in resolution reuses the same weights across more pixels, so the modeled arithmetic intensity rises and the fixed weight traffic becomes a smaller share of the work. That amortization is why the illustrative slowdown falls short of the pure compute estimate. The model counts only weights and one pass over a scaled activation term, so it establishes the direction of the effect. A profiler decides whether the executed kernels are compute or memory bound.

Resolution strategies in production

Different deployment contexts choose resolution according to which constraint binds first. A mobile application may accept \(224{\times}224\) inputs because latency and battery life outweigh marginal accuracy gains, whereas medical-imaging workloads often step up to \(512{\times}512\) or higher because diagnostic detail justifies the extra compute. Between those fixed choices, a perception pipeline may run a wide field at low resolution and reserve high-resolution crops for fine-grained recognition. A public API that receives arbitrary user uploads can either normalize every image to a single input size or route each request to one of several validated resolution profiles.

Adaptive resolution

Adaptive resolution lets production systems select resolution dynamically based on content. One approach runs a lightweight classifier at \(128{\times}128\) to categorize content type, then selects task-appropriate resolution with documents at \(512{\times}512\), landscapes at \(224{\times}224\), and faces at \(384{\times}384\). In this illustrative scenario, the policy provides 1.4× throughput improvement with 99.2 percent accuracy retention vs. fixed high resolution. This pattern trades preprocessing cost from running the lightweight classifier for inference savings on the main model. While resolution sets the cost of the stages before the model, the stage after it is usually far cheaper, though it still crosses the device boundary and spends host CPU time and therefore belongs on the same budget.

13.3.4 Postprocessing

When a model finishes a forward pass, its raw output logits sit in accelerator memory that client applications cannot read directly; copying thousands of unreduced floats across the PCIe bus and formatting strings on the CPU can stall the worker thread between batches. Postprocessing turns those device tensors into structured application responses across the physical boundary between the accelerator and the host CPU. How the serving engine splits computation across that boundary determines whether the final stage finishes in microseconds or blocks the pipeline.

From logits to predictions

Classification models emit unnormalized logits across the label space, and turning those raw scores into application-ready predictions requires a chain of reduction, filtering, and calibration steps. When only the single most likely label matters, argmax selection extracts the index of the highest logit, whereas applications that present ranked alternatives use top-\(\TopClassCount\) extraction to keep the \(\TopClassCount\) largest scores alongside their class indices. Before returning those candidates, probability calibration (such as temperature scaling) rescales raw logits so that output confidences reflect empirical accuracy, allowing thresholding to discard predictions that fall below a required certainty cutoff.

Executing reduction operations on the accelerator can reduce host-device transfer volume when the saved transfer cost outweighs the device execution and launch overhead. Performing softmax, argmax, or top-\(\TopClassCount\) selection in device memory prunes the payload before it crosses the PCIe bus. In ResNet-50 classification, for example, running top-\(\TopClassCount\) on the GPU reduces the transfer payload from 1,000 float32 logits (4,000 bytes) to five values and five integer indices. For ResNet-50 image classification, listing 1 traces this execution path from GPU tensor operations to host CPU label lookup and response formatting.

In listing 1, total postprocessing time is approximately 0.1 ms, negligible compared with preprocessing and inference. The GPU handles the normalization and top-5 reduction, so the subsequent .tolist() call transfers only tens of bytes across PCIe. However, .tolist() forces a synchronous device-to-host copy, blocking the host thread until the GPU stream finishes. In high-throughput pipelined servers, such synchronization barriers must be scheduled carefully to avoid stalling the CPU thread while it feeds subsequent requests. Fitting a calibration mapping is an offline task; applying a simple fitted mapping online is usually inexpensive, but it is valuable only when downstream decisions require confidence scores whose empirical meaning has been validated.

Output formatting

Production systems package predictions to satisfy application-level API contracts. These contracts govern JSON serialization schemas, floating-point formatting, error codes, and fallback defaults when thresholding rejects all candidates. In Python-based runtimes, repetitive JSON serialization and string allocation can consume unexpected host CPU cycles under heavy concurrency, turning a microsecond-scale formatting step into a CPU bottleneck. High-throughput servers address this by delegating serialization to native C++ runtimes or binary protocols like Protocol Buffers. Response metadata, including model version and inference execution time, completes the payload to enable downstream observability. Characterizing postprocessing closes the individual request’s latency budget, though analyzing these stages strictly in isolation assumes sequential evaluation, where each transaction completes its entire lifecycle before subsequent work begins.

13.3.5 Hardware utilization and request pipelining

Executing each request in lockstep isolation leaves specialized accelerators heavily underutilized. Because network termination, payload preprocessing, neural network evaluation, and response serialization bind on distinct physical subsystems (host network interfaces, CPU cores, PCIe interconnects, and accelerator matrix units), strict serial processing forces the accelerator into idle bubbles while the host prepares incoming tensors. The central systems objective is to maximize the accelerator’s duty cycle, the proportion of wall-clock time spent actively executing arithmetic kernels, without violating request tail-latency deadlines. Serving runtimes achieve this through request pipelining, overlapping asynchronous host preprocessing of forthcoming queries with concurrent accelerator execution on previously staged batches.

↩︎ Asynchronous host-accelerator transfer and double buffering mechanisms are established in Transfer optimization.

Overlapping I/O and compute

Figure 4 sequences execution across CPU preprocessing and GPU inference to demonstrate how request pipelining recovers lost compute capacity. In serial execution (A), each request must complete its entire lifecycle (Network \(\rightarrow\) CPU Preprocessing \(\rightarrow\) GPU Inference \(\rightarrow\) Postprocessing) before the next request begins, leaving the GPU idle for more than 50 percent of wall-clock time. Pipelining concurrent requests (B) overlaps CPU preprocessing of upcoming requests with active GPU inference on earlier inputs, eliminating the idle bubbles and maximizing accelerator duty cycle.

Pipelining relies on non-blocking hardware queues and asynchronous execution streams. Instead of blocking until a GPU kernel completes, the host CPU thread enqueues memory transfers and kernel launches into an accelerator stream and immediately begins preprocessing the next incoming request. The accelerator’s DMA engine and compute cores execute those queued operations independently, overlapping host preprocessing with device execution.

The systems metric: Hardware duty cycle

System efficiency here measures accelerator duty cycle rather than the fraction of peak arithmetic throughput or bandwidth achieved. In equation 2, compute time is the union of useful kernel-active intervals on each resource, summed across resources; overlapping kernels on one GPU are not counted twice. \[\text{System Efficiency} = \frac{\sum \KernelActiveTime}{\text{Wall Clock Time} \times \text{Resource Count}} \tag{2}\]

The reference ResNet-50 request spends 4.5 ms preprocessing on a host CPU core and 3.5 ms on the accelerator, so a serial system keeps the accelerator busy only 41 percent of the time. With at least two requests in flight in a sustained stream, the accelerator works on one request while the host prepares the next, and duty cycle rises to 78 percent after pipeline fill. It cannot reach 100 percent with one preprocessing worker, because the host stage is the slower of the two; a second worker removes that cap. A finite pair still pays pipeline fill and drain time, and stochastic service must retain enough capacity headroom to meet its tail-latency objective. If the CPU is too slow to feed the GPU, the system becomes CPU-bound, and further model optimization provides zero throughput gain. This is the Amdahl ceiling of section 13.3.2 seen from the throughput side. Hardware upgrades tighten it, because accelerator compute throughput has grown far faster than CPU single-thread performance across successive generations. A system that was compute-bound on an older accelerator may become CPU-bound after a hardware upgrade, not because preprocessing got slower but because the model got dramatically faster while the CPU did not.

Checkpoint 13.1: The latency budget

A request’s latency is spent across every stage of its path, not only in the model. Use this checkpoint to locate where the budget goes before choosing an optimization.

Tracing an isolated request reveals the intrinsic execution time of each pipeline phase. In production, however, multiple requests arrive concurrently and contend for shared accelerators, CPU cores, and memory buses, introducing queuing delays that dwarf raw computation time.

Self-Check: Question

  1. An image classification service has an end-to-end SLA of 30 ms. Assume the request path is a serial pipeline with unchanged non-GPU stages, no introduced overhead from the optimization itself, and no queueing feedback effects. The request path consists of: network ingress/egress (8 ms), CPU image decoding and normalization (10 ms), GPU forward pass (8 ms), and postprocessing/top-\(k\) filtering (2 ms). If an engineer optimizes the GPU model forward pass to run in 4 ms (a \(2\times\) speedup), what is the new total latency and what architectural principle explains the resulting overall speedup?

    1. Total latency decreases from 28 ms to 24 ms (a 14.3% latency reduction, about a \(1.17\times\) speedup), demonstrating Amdahl’s law where unchanged serial stages bound the system gains.
    2. Total latency decreases from 28 ms to 14 ms (a 50% overall improvement), because GPU acceleration propagates linearly across all pipeline stages.
    3. Total latency remains 28 ms because dynamic batching automatically inserts artificial delay to fill GPU occupancy.
    4. Total latency increases to 32 ms due to host-device synchronization overhead incurred by faster kernel launches.

  2. What is the ‘killer microseconds’ problem in low-latency ML serving systems, and why do standard operating system scheduling and hardware primitives struggle to handle it efficiently?

  3. True or False: For computer vision models utilizing standard 2D convolutions or self-attention mechanisms, increasing the input image resolution from \(224 \times 224\) to \(448 \times 448\) quadruples the number of input pixels (\(4\times\)), which results in an approximately \(4\times\) increase in FLOPs for standard convolutional layers and up to a \(16\times\) increase for unwindowed full self-attention layers, assuming fixed channels, patch size, and architecture.

  4. A classification serving pipeline returns only the top-5 predictions to the client. Why is it often better to run top-\(k\) selection on the GPU before copying results back to the host?

    1. It retrains the model online so fewer logits must be stored in GPU memory.
    2. It guarantees lower latency in every deployment because GPUs always execute small reductions faster than CPUs.
    3. It reduces the payload that crosses PCIe from the full logit vector to just a few scores and indices, though the host must still budget the synchronization and copy needed to materialize those results.
    4. It avoids the need for any host-side postprocessing or response formatting after inference finishes.

  5. In a high-throughput vision serving pipeline, engineers overlap CPU image preprocessing of request \(N+1\) with GPU inference of request \(N\) using multiple CUDA streams and pinned host memory buffers, thereby increasing accelerator ____ without modifying the underlying model architecture.

See Answers →

13.4 Queuing Theory

An inference service that responds in a few milliseconds at moderate load can suffer sudden, tenfold latency spikes when traffic climbs toward capacity, even though no hardware has failed and the model’s execution time has not changed. Because requests arrive in irregular bursts and contend for shared accelerator queues, host-to-device PCIe links, and memory buses, queuing theory governs how waiting times grow and why latency explodes nonlinearly as utilization approaches capacity.

13.4.1 Little’s Law

Before deciding how many accelerators to provision, an engineer must know how many requests will sit inside the server at the same time. Underestimating that in-flight population exhausts host request buffers, worker threads, and device activation memory under load. Given an expected arrival rate and mean response time, Little’s Law determines this average concurrency directly from throughput, while queuing models predict how response times climb as utilization nears saturation.

↪ The derivation of Little’s Law from conservation of requests is collected for reference in Little's Law.

Systems Perspective 13.3: Notation alert: L vs. latency
Classical queueing texts write Little’s law with single letters for the in-system count and the time in system. This book writes \(\RequestConcurrency\) for the count and \(\RequestTimeInSystem\) for the time, because the classical count letter would collide with the loss \(\LossFunction\) and with the iron law’s fixed-latency term \(\Latency\). In queuing theory, \(\RequestTimeInSystem\) denotes the response time or time in system per request; queue-only waiting time is \(\QueueWaitTime\). Here, \(\RequestConcurrency\) denotes the average in-system request count, \(\ArrivalRate\) the arrival rate, \(\ServiceRate\) the service rate of a server that handles one request at a time, and \(\ServingUtilization=\ArrivalRate/\ServiceRate\) the serving utilization. The subscripts distinguish queueing notation from the degradation equation’s \(\FittedSensitivity\) sensitivity parameter and keep serving utilization from occupying bare \(\Glyph{\rho}\). Section 13.5.2 adds the few symbols that batching needs.

Formulated by John D. C. Little (Little 1961), Little’s law establishes an exact relationship among three macroscopic metrics in any stable queuing system: \[\RequestConcurrency = \ArrivalRate \cdot \RequestTimeInSystem \tag{3}\] where \(\RequestConcurrency\) is the average number of requests residing in the system, \(\ArrivalRate\) is the arrival rate (requests per second), and \(\RequestTimeInSystem\) is the average time each request spends from arrival to completion.

Little, John D. C. 1961. “A Proof for the Queuing Formula: \(L = \lambda W\).” Operations Research 9 (3): 383–87. https://doi.org/10.1287/opre.9.3.383.

Little’s law holds regardless of the arrival distribution, service-time distribution, or scheduling discipline; it requires only that the system is stable and that requests are neither created nor destroyed internally. For an inference service targeting 1000 QPS with a mean response time of 50 ms, Little’s law dictates the average in-flight request population that host memory buffers and worker threads must sustain.

Napkin Math 13.2: Little's Law capacity sizing
Problem: How many requests are in the system on average at 1,000 QPS?

Math: Little’s Law gives \(\RequestConcurrency = \ArrivalRate \RequestTimeInSystem\), so average population equals throughput multiplied by mean time in system.

Given:

  • Arrival rate \((\ArrivalRate)\), equal to throughput in a stable system: 1,000 QPS.
  • Mean time in system \((\RequestTimeInSystem)\): 50 ms (0.05 s).

Math: \(\RequestConcurrency\) = 1,000 QPS \(\times\) 0.05 s = 50 concurrent requests on average

Systems insight: The system holds 50 requests on average across batch and queue state. That population is a memory and concurrency requirement, not a limit on the accelerator. A server whose GPU executes at most 32 requests per batch can still sustain this load, because the requests not currently executing wait in host memory. Service rate, queueing behavior, and the tail distribution, not the population itself, determine whether the load is feasible.

Because equation 3 binds these three variables, constraining any two dictates the third. If an inference service averages 10 ms per request \((\RequestTimeInSystem = 0.01 \text{ s})\) and holds 50 concurrent requests on average \((\RequestConcurrency = 50)\), the sustainable arrival rate must be \(\ArrivalRate = \RequestConcurrency / \RequestTimeInSystem = 5000\) requests per second. Conversely, if host memory limits concurrency to an average of 10 requests while average time in system remains 10 ms, the system cannot exceed a throughput of 1,000 requests per second without dropping traffic.

13.4.2 The utilization-latency relationship

Little’s Law describes average system inventory under steady-state conditions, but it does not reveal how latency behaves as traffic approaches system capacity. The M/M/1 queuing model (in Kendall’s notation: Markovian, that is Poisson, arrivals; Markovian, that is exponential, service times; and one server) quantifies the headroom required to prevent latency collapse (Harchol-Balter 2013).9 For a system with Poisson arrivals10 and exponential service times, equation 4 gives the average time in system: \[\RequestTimeInSystem = \frac{1}{\ServiceRate- \ArrivalRate} = \frac{\text{service time}}{1 - \ServingUtilization} \tag{4}\] where \(\ArrivalRate\) is the arrival rate, \(\ServiceRate\) is the service rate (requests per second the server can handle), and \(\ServingUtilization = \ArrivalRate/\ServiceRate\) is the utilization (the fraction of time the server is busy).

9 M/M/1 queue: Queuing theory originated with Agner Krarup Erlang’s 1909 analysis of the Copenhagen Telephone Exchange, where call arrivals were modeled as memoryless (Poisson).

10 Poisson process: Named after French mathematician Simeon Denis Poisson (1781–1840), this stochastic model describes events occurring continuously and independently at a constant average rate, so counts in disjoint intervals are independent.

11 Kendall notation: Kendall’s A/S/c (arrival/service/servers) shorthand (Kendall 1953) is the one already used for \(\KendallMarkovianCode/\KendallMarkovianCode/1\), where “M” signifies a Markovian (memoryless) process; the “D” in M/D/1 denotes deterministic service. Neither model replaces measurement of a production workload’s arrival process, service-time variance, or tail behavior.

Kendall, David G. 1953. “Stochastic Processes Occurring in the Theory of Queues and Their Analysis by the Method of the Imbedded Markov Chain.” The Annals of Mathematical Statistics 24 (3): 338–54. https://doi.org/10.1214/aoms/1177728975.

Because the denominator contains \(1 - \ServingUtilization\), latency grows nonlinearly as utilization approaches unity, and small incremental increases in traffic near saturation trigger severe backpressure and queuing delays. The standard M/M/1 formulation assumes exponentially distributed service times. For compiled neural networks processing fixed-size batches and static tensor dimensions, execution times are nearly constant, making an M/D/1 queue a tighter model of mean queuing delay. The analysis here retains the M/M/1 model because it yields closed-form solutions and predicts greater mean queueing delay than M/D/1 under matched arrival rate and mean service time. In production environments, variable input lengths, dynamic batch formation, CPU preprocessing, and key-value cache lookups introduce runtime variance that quickly breaks deterministic assumptions.11 Under this model, average time in system multiplies rapidly as utilization approaches 100 percent (table 7).

Plotted against the serving utilization \(\ServingUtilization\), the fraction of time the server is busy, the same \(\KendallMarkovianCode/\KendallMarkovianCode/1\) model shows why the knee matters (figure 5). Latency stays nearly flat at low utilization and then climbs without bound as the server approaches 100 percent busy, because each new arrival is increasingly likely to find work already waiting. The 99th percentile curve rises even faster in absolute terms, staying a fixed multiple above the mean that section 13.4.3 derives next. The green zone below 50 percent utilization and the red zone above 70 percent illustrate a planning policy, not universal operating limits. This rapid compounding near saturation is why latency-sensitive systems reserve headroom rather than planning for permanently high accelerator occupancy.

↪ Why long-tailed latency distributions make high percentiles, rather than the mean, govern what users experience is collected for reference in Distributions and the long tail.

13.4.3 Tail latency

A serving cluster running at 70 percent utilization can report a comfortable mean latency while its slowest 1 percent of requests wait more than four times as long in the queue. Because averages hide this tail contention, production SLOs specify percentile targets (p95, p99) that bound experience for the slowest requests (Dean and Barroso 2013).
