The M/M/1 model that produced the mean in equation 4 also yields the full latency distribution, and the tail follows from it. Total request latency (queue wait plus service) is exponentially distributed with cumulative distribution function \(\ResponseTimeDistributionFunction(\RequestResponseTime) = 1 - e^{-\RequestResponseTime/ \RequestTimeInSystem}\), where \(\RequestTimeInSystem = \frac{\text{service time}}{1 - \ServingUtilization}\) is that mean response time. To derive the 99th percentile latency (p99), we solve for the time \(\RequestResponseTime_{99}\) where 99 percent of requests have completed (\(\ResponseTimeDistributionFunction(\RequestResponseTime_{99}) = 0.99\)): \[1 - e^{-\RequestResponseTime_{99}/\RequestTimeInSystem} = 0.99 \implies e^{-\RequestResponseTime_{99}/\RequestTimeInSystem} = 0.01 \implies -\frac{\RequestResponseTime_{99}}{\RequestTimeInSystem} = \ln(0.01) = -\ln(100) \approx -4.605\] Solving for \(\RequestResponseTime_{99}\) and substituting the mean latency \(\RequestTimeInSystem\) yields the governing expression for tail latency: \[\TailRequestLatency\approx \frac{\text{service time}}{1 - \ServingUtilization} \cdot \ln\left(\frac{1}{1 - 0.99}\right) \approx \frac{4.6 \cdot \text{service time}}{1 - \ServingUtilization} \tag{5}\]

At 70 percent utilization, average latency is only 3.3 times service time, while p99 latency reaches 15.3 times service time \((4.6/0.3 \approx 15.3)\). Evaluating a serving cluster by average latency therefore conceals severe tail degradation. Before exploring software mitigations like hedging, systems engineers must first provision sufficient accelerator capacity to satisfy tail-latency SLOs under peak load. For a ResNet-50 deployment, the M/M/1 tail-response-time model turns provisioning into a short sequence of steps.

Napkin Math 13.3: ResNet-50 capacity planning
Consider designing a ResNet-50 serving system with these requirements:

  • Queue-plus-GPU p99 subbudget: 50 ms
  • Peak expected traffic: 5,000 QPS
  • GPU service time (batch one, the chapter’s reference ResNet-50 profile): 3.5 ms

Step 0: Define the modeled request boundary. The worker model covers waiting for the GPU and the GPU forward pass only. This scenario allocates 50 ms to that portion of the request and separately assumes a combined 18 ms upper bound for network transit, serialization, preprocessing, transfer, and postprocessing. The end-to-end deadline must accommodate both allowances. A 50 ms end-to-end deadline would instead require subtracting the outside-worker allowance before applying the queue model; the arithmetic below would no longer apply. An outside-stage mean or p99 cannot substitute for the stipulated combined upper bound.

Step 1: Find safe utilization. From equation 5, \(\TailRequestLatency\approx\) 4.6 \(\times\) service time / \((1 - \ServingUtilization)\). Setting \(\TailRequestLatency\leq\) 50 ms with 3.5 ms service time gives \(\ServingUtilization \leq 1 - (4.6 \times 3.5\,\mathrm{ms})/50\,\mathrm{ms} = 0.68\) (68 percent maximum utilization). This is an M/M/1 p99 estimate under the stated arrival and service assumptions; a load test must validate the tail.

Step 2: Calculate required service rate. \(\RequiredServiceRate = 5,000\,\mathrm{QPS} / 0.68 = 7402.9\,\mathrm{req}/\mathrm{s}\)

Step 3: Determine GPU count. In this M/M/1 model, the single-GPU rate is 283 req/s.

GPUs needed = 7402.9 req/s / 283 req/s = 26.1 → 27 GPUs

This single-GPU rate is the batch-1 service rate. Each request holds the GPU for the full service time, which keeps the M/M/1 model closed-form. Dynamic batching (section 13.5) amortizes fixed overhead across requests and raises the per-GPU rate several-fold, so a batched deployment may need fewer GPUs after its own latency distribution is validated. Section 13.10.2 compares the two regimes.

Step 4: Add headroom for variance. This scenario adds 30 percent headroom for traffic spikes and variance: final count = 27 \(\times\) 1.3 = 35.1, rounded up to 36.

Step 5: Verify fault tolerance. The 30 percent headroom addresses traffic variance, but production systems also need fault tolerance. With 36 GPUs, losing one leaves 35 GPUs handling 5,000 QPS. The postfailure utilization is 5,000 QPS / (283 req/s \(\times\) 35) = \(50.4\%\).

This remains well below the 68 percent safe utilization threshold, confirming N+1 redundancy is satisfied. For stricter fault tolerance requirements, N+2 redundancy (tolerating two simultaneous failures) would require 29 GPUs under the same safe-utilization threshold, or about 38 GPUs if the 30 percent headroom must remain after two simultaneous failures.

Result: Under the batch-1 M/M/1 assumptions, 36 H100 GPUs supply the modeled queue-plus-GPU capacity for 5,000 QPS within the 50 ms p99 subbudget, including one failed replica. End-to-end acceptance still requires validating the tail under load and the separately reserved outside-worker allowance.

The queuing analysis explains the capacity planning approach detailed in section 13.10.2 and connects directly to the MLPerf Server scenario. The Server scenario (Server) is defined as Poisson arrivals scored by throughput against a tail-latency target. A Server result is therefore valid only when the run satisfies its percentile-latency constraint; otherwise, the target QPS must be reduced and the run repeated.

13.4.4 Tail-tolerant techniques

A capacity plan built on stationary Poisson arrivals holds only while traffic stays near the planned rate. Production traffic instead arrives in bursty, uncoordinated surges driven by user behavior, scheduled jobs, and daily cycles. When a surge pushes arrivals past the reserved headroom, accelerator service times cannot shrink to absorb the excess, so queries pile up in host and device queues and clients begin timing out.

Example 13.3: The 'Black Friday' traffic spike
Scenario: An e-commerce recommendation serving system experiences a 10× traffic surge (10,000 QPS) during Black Friday sales events.

Diagnosis: A fleet sized for normal traffic now receives more requests per second than it can complete. Utilization pins at 100 percent, and every second of the surge adds the excess arrivals to a queue that the servers cannot drain, so waiting time grows for as long as the surge lasts. In this scenario, latency reaches 10 s and clients time out. Section 13.4.2 showed why the queue starts growing sharply well before utilization reaches 100 percent.

Systems lesson: High average throughput does not prevent queueing collapse under traffic surges. Depending on the service, preserving a tail-latency objective may require load shedding, graceful degradation, or autoscaling before utilization reaches the workload’s queueing knee.

Eliminating all sources of latency variability is often impractical. Production systems instead use techniques that tolerate variability while still meeting SLOs (Dean and Barroso 2013; Dean 2012). For one serving node, the first distinction is between an isolated straggler and overload. Racing an isolated slow request can reduce its latency when spare capacity exists. When the node is overloaded, however, racing requests injects duplicate work and deepens the queue, so the system must instead protect the latency of admitted requests through graceful degradation or admission control. Graceful degradation returns lower-fidelity results to preserve throughput: classification pipelines fall back to cached approximations, generative models truncate decoding lengths, and model ensembles evaluate only a subset of members. Dynamically reducing active ensemble members shortens the service time \(\BatchServiceTime(\BatchSize)\) of each batch of \(\BatchSize\) requests. That increases the effective service rate \(\EffectiveServiceRate(\BatchSize) = \BatchSize/\BatchServiceTime(\BatchSize)\) and lowers utilization \(\ServingUtilization = \ArrivalRate / \EffectiveServiceRate(\BatchSize)\) toward a workload-appropriate operating point with more headroom, as illustrated by figure 5. The system trades a controlled accuracy reduction for SLO survival instead of an uncontrolled latency collapse. Admission control enforces an even stricter boundary. When the ingress queue exceeds a designated threshold, the server immediately rejects excess queries with HTTP 503 status codes rather than admitting work doomed to violate its latency deadline.

Dean, Jeffrey. 2012. Achieving Rapid Response Times in Large Online Services. Berkeley AMPLab Cloud Seminar.

Setting this rejection threshold requires comparing how long the arriving request can afford to wait against how long the work already ahead of it will take to drain. Consider a warmed, isolated worker that serves requests sequentially in first-in-first-out (FIFO) order, without batching or preemption, and assume known upper bounds on each request’s service time and on every remaining stage outside the worker, including the response path. An arriving request has already spent some of its end-to-end latency allowance on network ingress and preprocessing, and it must still reserve enough time for its own execution on the worker and for the remaining outside-worker stages after the worker finishes. Subtracting the time already spent, the remaining outside-worker bound, and the arriving request’s own service bound from the end-to-end deadline leaves the request’s waiting allowance, the maximum time it can sit in the queue and still finish on time. If those non-queue stages already exceed the deadline, the waiting allowance is negative and the request cannot meet its target even on an idle worker. Otherwise, the request must wait for the worker’s outstanding-work bound, which is the remaining service bound of the request currently executing plus the service bounds of all requests already waiting in the queue. Admission passes the deadline screen only when that outstanding-work bound fits within the waiting allowance. A simple limit on queue length approximates this comparison only when service costs are nearly uniform across requests. When they are, a practical starting point bounds the ingress queue at two or three requests per worker, the backlog a worker clears within two or three further service times, and the deadline screen and measured tails then tighten or relax that bound.

When the queued backlog exceeds the full model’s waiting allowance, the server can still check whether a smaller, faster fallback model that is already resident and warmed would finish in time. Switching the arriving request to a faster model does not shorten the backlog already queued ahead of it, because those earlier requests still have to finish first. Instead, it shrinks the service time that the arriving request must reserve for its own execution, which transfers that saved millisecond budget into a larger waiting allowance. If the existing backlog fits inside that expanded waiting allowance and the fallback model’s prediction quality meets the application’s minimum requirement, the server can admit the request on the fallback path. If neither acceptable model fits, rejection avoids spending accelerator time on a result that would arrive too late. Rejection reduces availability, while fallback can reduce prediction fidelity. Both outcomes must be counted alongside latency so that shedding difficult requests does not make the service appear successful merely by shrinking its measurement population.

The deadline screen is a deterministic bound only under the stated service, isolation, and scheduling assumptions. Mean service time or a per-stage p99 cannot replace those upper bounds. Variable input lengths, batch formation, and shared memory-bus contention require empirical calibration against the joint request-latency distribution. Completed-request tails must be reported together with rejection rates and fallback quality over offered traffic. Adaptive admission control can tighten the admissible backlog as measured tail latency approaches the SLO, but neither a fixed queue count nor the simplified screen certifies a production p99 target.

13.4.5 Scaling across multiple replicas

Once traffic outgrows a single node’s safe utilization knee, no single-machine tuning can prevent queue build-up. Higher aggregate volume requires horizontal replication, placing identical model replicas behind a load balancer so that each holds a complete model copy and serves disjoint queries without inter-node synchronization. (Model parallelism, which shards one large model across accelerators linked by a fabric such as NVLink, solves a different problem: fitting a model that exceeds one device’s memory or latency budget.) The \(\KendallMarkovianCode/\KendallMarkovianCode/\QueueServerCount\) queuing model extends the single-server analysis to \(\QueueServerCount\) parallel workers. Pooling requests in one shared queue absorbs arrival bursts better than isolated single-server queues, provided dispatch is balanced (least-loaded routing, for example) and each replica keeps its own headroom.

The tail at scale problem

Dean and Barroso’s analysis reveals why tail latency becomes critical as systems scale beyond single machines (Dean and Barroso 2013). When requests fan out to \(\FanoutBackendCount\) parallel backends (querying an ensemble of models, searching sharded embedding indices, or parallelizing pipeline stages), the overall user request completes only when the slowest sub-task finishes. If sub-task completion events are independent and each has a 99 percent probability of finishing within latency threshold \(\LatencySlo\), the probability that all \(\FanoutBackendCount\) backends finish within \(\LatencySlo\) is \((0.99)^\FanoutBackendCount\). For a fan-out of \(\FanoutBackendCount= 10\), only \(0.99^{10} \approx 90.4\%\) of requests meet the target; at \(\FanoutBackendCount= 100\), that probability drops to \(0.99^{100} \approx 36.6\%\), meaning nearly two-thirds of user requests experience a tail delay. This “tail at scale” effect makes controlling single-node tail variance essential for overall system performance.

Dean, Jeffrey, and Luiz André Barroso. 2013. “The Tail at Scale.” Communications of the ACM 56 (2): 74–80. https://doi.org/10.1145/2408776.2408794.

12 Hedging: The term is borrowed from finance, where an offsetting position reduces risk; here, the redundant request insures against a slow server. The insurance is not free. For ML systems, the losing hedged request may still occupy accelerator time if inference has already launched, because ordinary GPU kernels are not cheaply canceled mid-execution. Thus, a hedging policy must budget duplicate work as well as the latency benefit.

Hedging and tied requests limit the damage from a straggler replica. Under hedging, a request that has not completed within the expected time is duplicated to another server, and the client keeps whichever response arrives first.12 With a stable latency distribution, a threshold at the historical 95th percentile duplicates about 5 percent of requests; correlated slowdowns raise that fraction and can make hedging amplify overload instead of reducing the tail. Tied requests instead enter several server queues at once and cancel the other copies when one server begins processing, which avoids waiting to detect a slow response. Both techniques create duplicate work. Cancellation before dispatch prevents a losing copy from consuming accelerator time, whereas cancellation after dispatch only discards its result, so either needs spare capacity and an explicit duplicate-work budget. Canary requests apply the same idea to fan-out, sending the request first to one or two servers and committing to the full fan-out only if they answer within the expected time.

Replicas or streams on one machine can also race requests, but sharing an accelerator or memory bus can correlate their slowdowns and exhaust the same headroom. The local admission decision remains the foundation; fleet coordination is an extension of it. Retries across a fleet obey the same duplicate-work constraint, because load balancers that retry rejected requests at other overloaded replicas amplify the overload. Replicas should therefore share load information and shed the same fraction of requests rather than each rejecting independently. Production frameworks may use shortest-job-first or deadline-aware scheduling rather than FIFO, changing which queued work lies ahead of an arrival and requiring a corresponding admission analysis (Harchol-Balter 2013).

Checkpoint 13.2: Queuing and SLO headroom

Latency SLOs are not enforced by fast inference alone; they are enforced by headroom.

Headroom keeps the queue stable by leaving capacity idle, and adding replicas creates headroom by adding accelerators. The other way to keep utilization below the knee is to raise each replica’s service rate, and for an accelerator that executes one request at a time the largest lever is to execute several at once.

Self-Check: Question

  1. An online recommendation service handles an arrival rate of \(\lambda = 500\) queries per second. Instrumented telemetry reveals an average residency time (waiting time in queue + inference service time) of \(W = 40\text{ ms}\) (\(0.04\text{ s}\)). According to Little’s Law, what is the average number of concurrent requests (\(L\)) present in the serving system?

    1. \(L = 12.5\text{ requests}\)
    2. \(L = 200\text{ requests}\)
    3. \(L = 2\text{,}000\text{ requests}\)
    4. \(L = 20\text{ requests}\)

  2. In a distributed serving architecture, an aggregate user request fans out in parallel to 50 leaf model servers and must wait for all 50 responses before completing. Assume each leaf independently has a 1% probability of exceeding the same latency threshold, other latency sources are negligible, and all 50 requests are issued simultaneously. Explain the ‘tail at scale’ phenomenon and calculate the probability that the aggregate request suffers tail latency.

  3. True or False: Hedged requests (speculative backup requests) reduce p99 tail latency by sending identical duplicate requests to multiple servers simultaneously for every incoming query upon arrival, without incurring any additional cluster compute overhead.

  4. Order the following overload-handling workflow for a serving system from first diagnosis to final verification: (1) choose a mitigation that fits the current bottleneck and SLO trade-off, such as admission control, graceful degradation, or a batching change, (2) measure arrival rate, service time, queue depth, and observed tail latency, (3) identify the binding constraint from those measurements, such as overload, queue growth, or service-time inflation, (4) validate the chosen policy under representative load and confirm it improves SLO compliance.

  5. An inference node modeled as an \(M/M/1\) queue has an average execution service time of \(T_{\text{svc}} = 10\text{ ms}\). If the arrival rate increases such that system utilization \(\rho\) increases from \(50\%\) (\(\rho = 0.5\)) to \(90\%\) (\(\rho = 0.9\)), what happens to the mean total response time \(W\)?

    1. \(W\) increases linearly from 10 ms to 18 ms.
    2. \(W\) increases nonlinearly from 20 ms to 100 ms (\(5\times\) increase).
    3. \(W\) decreases from 20 ms to 11.1 ms due to batching efficiency.
    4. \(W\) remains fixed at 10 ms because service time is independent of arrival rate.

  6. True or False: Under a simple \(M/M/1\) queueing model approximation, when server utilization (\(\rho_{\text{serv}}\)) reaches 90%, the 99th percentile (p99) tail latency is approximately equal to 1.5 times the mean latency.

See Answers →

13.5 Throughput Optimization

Consider the reference ResNet-50 classifier on an H100 GPU operating at batch size one. Each forward pass reads the model’s weights from high-bandwidth memory to serve a single image and issues a long sequence of small kernels whose fixed launch cost is paid once per image, leaving neither weight traffic nor launch overhead shared across inputs. Processing requests continuously at 280 images/s, each pass spends 99 percent of its time on those fixed costs. Processing 32 images at once delivers 7,000 images/s, a 25× throughput improvement on identical hardware, because one kernel sequence and one weight read now serve thirty-two inputs. The fixed-cost share must not be confused with serving utilization. A single worker processing batch 1 at 280 requests/s is 100 percent busy in queueing terms (\(\ServingUtilization = 1.0\)) with zero spare serving capacity, yet nearly all of its time goes to work that a larger batch would share. Batching13 is the primary lever for improving serving economics, but its operational constraints differ sharply between training and serving (Crankshaw et al. 2017). Training batches maximize throughput by grouping hundreds or thousands of samples together with no constraint on individual sample latency. Serving batches must balance accelerator saturation against individual request deadlines, ensuring that no early-arriving input waits excessively for a batch to form. An inference server resolves this tension through dynamic batching, adjusting batch size at runtime to match incoming request arrivals.

13 Batch: From Old French bache (a quantity baked at one time), entering computing in the 1950s for jobs processed together without human interaction. The ML serving usage preserves the original trade-off: grouping requests amortizes fixed costs (kernel launch, weight movement) across multiple inputs, but an online request may wait for a batch to form.

Crankshaw, Daniel, Xin Wang, Guilio Zhou, Michael J. Franklin, Joseph E. Gonzalez, and Ion Stoica. 2017. “Clipper: A Low-Latency Online Prediction Serving System.” 14th USENIX Symposium on Networked Systems Design and Implementation (NSDI 17), 613–27.
Definition 13.3: Dynamic batching

Dynamic batching is the ML serving optimization that trades latency for throughput under stochastic arrival patterns.

  1. Significance: By buffering requests into a batching window, the scheduler amortizes fixed overheads \((\Latency)\) across multiple inputs, pushing the system away from the bandwidth-bound regime \((\Bandwidth)\) toward the compute-bound regime \((\PeakRate)\).
  2. Distinction: Unlike fixed-size serving batches, dynamic batching adjusts the realized batch size at inference time according to arriving traffic, a timeout, and a maximum-batch limit.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that batching “always helps.” In reality, there is a latency-throughput Pareto frontier: if the batching window is too large, the increased queuing delay may violate the system’s SLO before the throughput gains are realized.

13.5.1 Why batching helps

A single request often leaves an accelerator’s compute units idle because its operations expose too little parallel work to hide kernel launch overhead or reuse weights streamed from memory (though large spatial inputs or smaller edge accelerators may already saturate at batch one). For workloads that underuse the device at small batches, increasing the batch size improves hardware efficiency (Shen et al. 2019) by amortizing fixed costs across multiple requests and running inputs in parallel across the batch dimension.

Shen, Haichen, Lequn Chen, Yuchen Jin, Liangyu Zhao, Bingyu Kong, Matthai Philipose, Arvind Krishnamurthy, and Ravi Sundaram. 2019. “Nexus: A GPU Cluster Engine for Accelerating DNN-Based Video Analysis.” Proceedings of the 27th ACM Symposium on Operating Systems Principles, 322–37. https://doi.org/10.1145/3341301.3359658.

Two fixed costs dominate at small batch sizes. Kernel launch overhead is the time for the CPU to prepare and submit work to the GPU. The number of launches depends on graph lowering and fusion rather than the layer count. For each launch, the CPU must assemble kernel parameters, copy them to GPU-accessible memory, and signal the GPU to begin execution. The cost per kernel does not depend on batch size. The reference ResNet-50 profile issues 174 kernels at 15 μs of host time each, 2.6 ms per forward pass. At batch size 1, this overhead far exceeds the actual compute time; at batch size 32, the same overhead is amortized across thirty-two images. Weight loading reads model parameters from GPU memory (VRAM) to the compute units. In a weight-streaming approximation, batch size 1 reads the weights for one image; batch size 32 reuses that weight stream across thirty-two images, increasing weight-only operational intensity by 32\(\times\). Activation traffic, caches, and workspace determine the executed operation’s full intensity.

↩︎ Per-kernel launch overhead, and why fusion and graph execution attack it, are established in The dispatch overhead law.

Larger batches improve hardware efficiency but inflate per-request latency. The engineering objective is to identify the operating point that maximizes sustainable throughput while satisfying the request deadline. For the reference ResNet-50 profile quantified in table 8 below, serving batch size 8 with a 10 ms batching window requires a mean formation-plus-inference budget of approximately 8.8 ms. An arriving request waits on average half the window (5 ms, as derived in section 13.5.4.1) and requires 3.8 ms of inference. If the service must meet a 20 ms SLO, the remaining 11.2 ms provides headroom for worker backlog and auxiliary pipeline stages, while delivering 7.5× higher service throughput than batch 1 on identical hardware. On this accelerator the batching window, not the computation, is most of the batch-8 budget. Batch size cannot grow indefinitely, and figure 6 shows why by modeling this relationship across batch sizes on a logarithmic axis. To isolate the planning knee under worst-case scheduling conditions, the figure assumes a strictly serialized single worker that waits out the full batching window before executing each batch, without pipelined overlap (\(\text{Latency} = \BatchingWindow + \BatchServiceTime(\BatchSize)\)). Under this unpipelined assumption, batch-1 throughput is 74 requests/s rather than the 280 images/s sustained when inference runs back to back. Throughput rises almost in proportion to batch size while the fixed overhead dominates each batch, because each added image costs only 33 μs of device time. The planning knee sits where the batch’s total per-image work equals the fixed part of its latency, the window plus the fixed cost, near batch 410. There latency is approximately twice that of batch one and throughput is half its ceiling. Beyond it, each doubling of the batch roughly doubles latency while throughput gains less and less. In this model throughput never declines; it saturates, so latency, not throughput, sets the largest useful batch.

The aligned dashed markers near batch size 410 in figure 6 mark the point where per-image work equals the fixed part of a batch’s latency. The knee illustrates a general systems trade-off rather than a fixed operating setting. Under unpipelined serial dispatch, throughput is bounded by \(\BatchSize / (\BatchingWindow + \BatchServiceTime(\BatchSize))\). In contrast, when the serving runtime pipelines batch formation with accelerator execution, saturated throughput depends solely on accelerator service time (\(\EffectiveServiceRate(\BatchSize) = \BatchSize / \BatchServiceTime(\BatchSize)\)), achieving the higher capacities documented in table 8.

Napkin Math 13.4: ResNet-50 batching efficiency
Table 8 illustrates the throughput-latency trade-off for the reference ResNet-50 profile on the H100 across batch sizes one through thirty-two. The last column is the fixed-cost share, the fraction of each batch’s time spent on costs that do not grow with batch size: kernel issue, the per-kernel device floor, and the weight read. This share must be distinguished from the server’s queueing utilization (\(\ServingUtilization = \ArrivalRate/\ServiceRate\), section 13.4.2) and its wall-clock duty cycle (equation 2). For example, a dedicated worker executing batch size 1 continuously operates at 100 percent queueing utilization with zero idle time between requests, yet spends 99 percent of each forward pass on fixed costs that a larger batch would share.

Math: For the batch-8 row, dividing batch latency by batch size yields per-image compute: 3.76 ms ÷ 8 images ≈ 0.47 ms per image. Dividing batch size by batch latency yields throughput: 8 images ÷ 3.76 ms ≈ 2,130 img/s. The same two relations apply to every derived row.

The inference times exclude queue wait. The window-plus-inference column is the unpipelined worst case for a request that waits out the whole window, and section 13.5.4.1 derives the average wait a request actually sees.

Systems insight: Batch size 32 achieves 25× higher throughput than batch size 1. However, user-perceived latency includes both queue wait and inference time. With a 10 ms batching window and 4.5 ms inference, formation plus inference reaches 14.5 ms, excluding resource backlog and other request stages, vs. 3.5 ms at batch size 1.

The sweep reports seconds; the same batches look different in bytes and joules. At batch one each image carries the entire 102.2 MB FP32 weight read by itself, and at batch thirty-two it carries one thirty-second of it, while the 25 MB of activations each image writes and reads stays fixed (table 9). Arithmetic intensity rises from 64 FLOP/byte to 291 FLOP/byte. With the energy constants that accompanied the iron law in Iron Law of ML Systems, 160 pJ/byte of DRAM traffic and 2.3 pJ/FLOP of FP32 arithmetic, the energy of one classification falls from 39.2 mJ at batch one to 23.4 mJ at batch thirty-two. Data movement accounts for 52 percent of that energy at batch one and 19 percent at batch thirty-two. Past that point arithmetic dominates, and a larger batch saves almost nothing per image while still lengthening every request’s wait.

13.5.2 Formation delay and the latency-throughput frontier

The throughput gains demonstrated in table 8 incur a deliberate systems cost. Every request in a batch pays a latency penalty, an engineered queueing delay introduced to elevate hardware utilization. The performance gain arises from parameter reuse across inputs. Evaluating \(\BatchSize\) independent queries simultaneously converts memory-bound matrix-vector products into compute-dense general matrix multiplications (GEMM), raising arithmetic intensity toward the hardware roofline ceiling (Principle 6). Conversely, the latency penalty accumulates in the queue, because assembling \(\BatchSize\) distinct queries forces earlier arrivals to sit idle while waiting for subsequent requests to appear.

Formalizing this trade-off requires extending the single-server queuing notation. Let \(\BatchServiceTime(\BatchSize)\) denote the execution duration for a batch of size \(\BatchSize\). The unbatched service rate is \(\ServiceRate= \EffectiveServiceRate(1) = 1/\BatchServiceTime(1)\), while batching \(\BatchSize\) inputs yields an effective aggregate throughput of \(\EffectiveServiceRate(\BatchSize) = \BatchSize/\BatchServiceTime(\BatchSize)\). In a request-triggered fixed-batching system, end-to-end response time decomposes into two distinct penalties. Batch formation delay \((\BatchFormationDelay)\) is the time earlier requests wait while the batch accumulates to capacity. Inference inflation is the modest increase in kernel execution duration \(\BatchServiceTime(\BatchSize)\) as batch size expands from 1 to \(\BatchSize\). Under stationary Poisson arrivals at rate \(\ArrivalRate\), the first request in a batch waits an average of \((\BatchSize-1)/\ArrivalRate\) seconds while the final arrival waits zero, yielding an average formation delay of \((\BatchSize-1)/(2\ArrivalRate)\). Assuming an execution worker is immediately available upon batch assembly, equation 6 models the resulting latency-throughput Pareto frontier: \[ \BatchedRequestLatency\approx \underbrace{ \frac{\BatchSize-1}{2\ArrivalRate} }_{\text{Formation delay}} + \underbrace{ \BatchServiceTime(\BatchSize) }_{\text{Inference time}} \tag{6}\]

Equation 6 formalizes the component trade-off between accelerator throughput and individual request latency, assuming an idle worker is immediately available upon batch assembly. Increasing \(\BatchSize\) raises arithmetic intensity and amortizes memory bandwidth across inputs, but introduces formation delay for early arrivals. Evaluated as a formation-plus-service component comparison using the service times from table 8 at 500 QPS, moving from batch 1 to batch 32 increases formation wait from 0 ms to 31 ms. That is a 10× increase in isolated stage latency (3.5 ms to 36 ms). However, batch-one service cannot sustain 500 QPS on a single worker whose peak service rate is only 280 requests/s. Without batching, worker backlog diverges toward infinity, so 3.5 ms does not represent a viable steady-state response time at this arrival rate. Batching is what makes 500 QPS feasible on that hardware in the first place, demonstrating that grouping requests can lower loaded end-to-end response time even while adding formation delay. Sizing \(\BatchSize\) is therefore an operational stability screen before it is an economic optimization. Operators first enforce queue stability (\(\ArrivalRate < \EffectiveServiceRate(\BatchSize)\)) and then select the batch size that maximizes throughput within the memory ceiling and latency SLO \((\LatencySlo)\). Because equation 6 assumes the batcher waits for a full count of \(\BatchSize\) requests before launching, the two batching policies below differ in how they relax that count-only assumption when traffic fluctuates.

13.5.3 Static vs. dynamic batching

If an inference server holds execution until its queue accumulates a fixed batch of \(\BatchSize\) requests, a lull in traffic can leave early arrivals waiting indefinitely until enough subsequent queries arrive to fill the batch. Static batching (where static describes the batch dimension rather than the precomputed predictions of static inference) fixes the batch size at compile time so compilers can allocate static memory buffers and emit specialized compute kernels. That is a natural fit for offline processing and model training, where inputs are already staged, but a liability when interactive requests arrive stochastically. Conversely, setting a small fixed batch size to cap waiting time leaves accelerator compute units underutilized and fails to amortize weight transfers from high-bandwidth memory when traffic spikes.

Dynamic batching decouples batch formation from arrival determinism by buffering incoming requests behind a dual-trigger scheduler (Olston et al. 2017; NVIDIA 2024b). Rather than waiting indefinitely for a fixed tensor shape, the serving runtime makes a batch eligible for dispatch when either of two thresholds is satisfied. Either the queue accumulates a maximum batch size \(\MaxBatchSize\), or a timeout window \(\BatchingWindow\) expires relative to the oldest buffered request. When traffic bursts, the queue fills to \(\MaxBatchSize\) almost instantaneously, achieving high arithmetic intensity with minimal queuing delay. When arrival rates drop, the timeout window forces the dispatch of an undersized batch (\(\BatchSize< \MaxBatchSize\)), bounding formation time at the expense of per-request compute efficiency. If the accelerator is busy, the ready batch can still wait for service. The timeout window \(\BatchingWindow\) and maximum batch size \(\MaxBatchSize\) form the primary operational levers. Shorter windows limit formation delay, whereas longer windows allow larger batches when enough requests arrive. A longer window is useful only when the resulting throughput gain remains within the latency and memory constraints.

13.5.4 Dynamic batching latency-throughput trade-offs

Setting those two levers (\(\BatchingWindow\) and \(\MaxBatchSize\)) requires accounting for what happens to the latency budget while the batcher waits. A wide window can spend a large share of the SLO accumulating requests before a single kernel launches. Because latency under load grows in the queue before it grows on the accelerator (section 13.4.4), configuring a dynamic batcher starts by separating time spent waiting from time spent in service.

Equation 7 decomposes the modeled batching-and-service portion of request latency into two components. Its total excludes network transit and any host stages outside the service interval: \[\BatchedRequestLatency= \BatchingQueueWait+ \BatchServiceTime(\BatchSize) \tag{7}\] where \(\BatchingQueueWait\) is the time spent waiting in the batching queue (corresponding to \(\QueueingLatency\) in the latency budget of principle 12) and \(\BatchServiceTime(\BatchSize)\) is the service time for a batch of size \(\BatchSize\) (encompassing \(\InferenceLatency\) plus portions of \(\PreprocessingLatency\) and \(\PostprocessingLatency\)). This wait includes formation and any subsequent wait for a worker. The batching window \(\BatchingWindow\) bounds formation time alone; \(\BatchingQueueWait\leq \BatchingWindow\) holds only when no additional resource backlog delays dispatch. Batch size affects service time through weight reuse, parallel work, and execution overhead.

Quantitative analysis of batching

Configuring a dynamic batching policy requires balancing batch formation delay against accelerator execution time and pending queue backlog. In a pure count-triggered batcher (equation 6), average formation delay \((\BatchSize-1)/(2\ArrivalRate)\) contracts as arrival rate \(\ArrivalRate\) grows, but diverges under light traffic. Conversely, a pure timer-triggered batcher dispatches on a periodic schedule of length \(\BatchingWindow\), decoupling formation delay from instantaneous arrival fluctuations.

To evaluate how time-based batching impacts latency budgets, consider a baseline fixed calendar window where batches dispatch periodically every \(\BatchingWindow\) seconds and an idle execution worker is available at the boundary. Under stationary Poisson arrivals, query arrival times distribute uniformly across the interval \([0, \BatchingWindow]\). A request arriving at offset \(\WindowArrivalOffset\) within the window experiences a formation wait of \(\BatchingWindow - \WindowArrivalOffset\). Integrating across the uniform distribution yields an expected waiting time equal to exactly half the batching window (equation 8): \[\mathbb{E}[\BatchingQueueWait] = \frac{\BatchingWindow}{2} \tag{8}\]

A 20 ms batching window adds 10 ms average wait (up to 20 ms for the first arrival in a window; later arrivals wait less) regardless of batch size achieved. For a 50 ms mean latency SLO with 3.5 ms inference, the average wait consumes 20 percent of the latency budget before any computation begins, about 3× the forward pass itself; tail SLOs must budget the full window.

Batch size distribution

For fixed calendar windows, the number of requests collected during \(\BatchingWindow\) follows a Poisson distribution with mean \(\ArrivalRate \BatchingWindow\). Equation 9 formalizes this relationship: \[\Pr(\text{batch size} = \WindowArrivalCount) = \frac{(\ArrivalRate \BatchingWindow)^\WindowArrivalCount e^{-\ArrivalRate \BatchingWindow}}{\WindowArrivalCount!} \tag{9}\] where \(\WindowArrivalCount\) is a nonnegative integer count of arrivals in the window.

With a fixed 10 ms window, the count a window collects fluctuates most at low traffic. At 50 QPS the mean batch is 0.5 with a standard deviation of 0.7, and 61 percent of windows contain no request at all, so no GPU batch launches. At 200 QPS the mean is 2 and the standard deviation 1.4, so realized batches still swing widely around the mean. At 1000 QPS the mean reaches 10 and the spread narrows relative to it. The probability that a window collects at least twice the mean falls from 39 percent at the lowest traffic level to 0.3 percent at the highest, the law of large numbers at work.

Throughput maximization strategy

Throughput optimization requires separating per-request latency from saturated service capacity. A request waits for batch formation, then pays the service time of the formed batch. Under sustained load, however, batch formation can overlap with the previous batch’s execution, so capacity is governed by the ready-batch service time: \[\EffectiveServiceRate(\BatchSize) \approx \frac{\BatchSize}{\BatchServiceTime(\BatchSize)}, \qquad \text{throughput} = \min(\ArrivalRate, \EffectiveServiceRate(\BatchSize)) \tag{10}\]

In equation 10, \(\ArrivalRate\) is the offered arrival rate and \(\EffectiveServiceRate(\BatchSize)\) is the saturated service capacity for batch size \(\BatchSize\). The numerator increases linearly with batch size while service time often increases sublinearly over a useful range, because larger batches amortize fixed kernel-launch overheads and DRAM weight transfers. This shifts execution from memory-bandwidth-bound matrix-vector operations toward compute-bound matrix-matrix multiplication. The batching window still appears in request latency and in low-traffic regimes, where the expected batch size is limited by arrivals during the window, roughly \(\ArrivalRate \BatchingWindow\).

To analytically evaluate batching strategies across arbitrary batch sizes without interpolating discrete benchmark tables, the reference ResNet-50 profile is affine in batch size, \(\BatchServiceTime(\BatchSize) = 3.50\text{ ms} + (0.033\text{ ms})\,\BatchSize\). The model has 3.50 ms of fixed kernel issue, per-kernel device floor, and weight read, plus 0.033 ms of device work per image. It is the same model that produced the batching sweep (table 8), so the sweep and the model describe one service. This analytical service model unifies the queueing, window screening, and dispatch calculations throughout the remainder of this section. The sweep’s window-plus-inference column already evaluates it for a 10 ms batching window \(\BatchingWindow\), pairing formation-plus-service latency with saturated pipelined capacity. The sweep’s throughput gains trace directly back to the fixed-overhead term \(\Latency\) of the iron law (principle 3), which batching amortizes across requests.

The sweep, the formation wait, and the arrival statistics compose a calendar-window planning model. Average formation wait is half the window, Poisson arrivals make the window count fluctuate around \(\ArrivalRate \BatchingWindow\), and saturated service capacity \(\EffectiveServiceRate(\BatchSize)\) grows as batching amortizes fixed costs. An oldest-request timer, a maximum-batch trigger, or a busy worker changes the observed wait and batch distributions. Mean-latency screening can narrow candidate configurations; a percentile SLO requires their tail behavior under the intended dispatch policy and load.

Latency-constrained optimization

When latency is the binding constraint, a mean-latency approximation provides an initial screen of candidate batch sizes. For the portion of a mean latency SLO allocated to batching and service, \(\BatchingLatencyBudget\), and average wait time \(\BatchingWindow/2\), equation 11 defines the maximum allowable batch size \(\LatencyBoundedBatchSize\) using a first-order average latency approximation. Other request stages require separate budget allocations. This is a feasibility bound, not a throughput optimum, and it screens a mean budget rather than certifying a p95 or p99 target: \[\LatencyBoundedBatchSize= \max\left\{\BatchSize : \frac{\BatchingWindow}{2} + \BatchServiceTime(\BatchSize) \leq \BatchingLatencyBudget\right\} \tag{11}\]

Applying equation 11 to the affine service model for a 50 ms mean budget shows how the choice of batching window shapes the feasible configuration space. A conservative 5 ms window adds 2.5 ms of average formation wait (up to the full 5 ms for the first request in a window) and leaves 47.5 ms for inference, enough by equation 11 for a batch of about 1,340 images. The configured engine maximum batch of 128, an assumed setting, binds first, so the batch is capped at 128 images and the worker sustains about 16,600 img/s when saturated. An aggressive 25 ms window adds 12.5 ms of average wait and leaves 37.5 ms, still enough for about 1,030 images, so the same engine cap binds and saturated capacity does not change. A tail SLO, unlike this mean-latency plan, must budget the full window rather than its average.

This comparison quantifies the diminishing returns of expanding the batching window. Widening the window by 20 ms buys no saturated capacity while increasing average formation wait by 10 ms. On an accelerator this fast relative to its fixed cost, the latency budget stops being the binding constraint on batch size; the engine’s maximum batch and the arrival rate are. However, this initial screen relies on mean formation wait and deterministic batch sizing. In production, stochastic arrivals introduce batch-size variance that can breach tail deadlines even when the mean latency appears well within the budget, requiring the percentile envelope analysis developed next.

SLO violation analysis

Batch size variability can cause SLO violations even when mean latency appears safe. Earlier, equation 9 counted arrivals across all calendar windows, including empty windows in which no request arrived and no batch launched. Latency, however, is measured only over requests that actually arrive. Under a Poisson process, an arriving request lands in a window alongside a number of other arrivals that is itself distributed as \(\operatorname{Poisson}(\ArrivalRate \BatchingWindow)\). Including the arriving request itself gives the request-seen batch size \(\RequestSeenBatchSize=1+\operatorname{Poisson}(\ArrivalRate \BatchingWindow)\), whose mean is \(1 + \ArrivalRate \BatchingWindow\) rather than \(\ArrivalRate \BatchingWindow\). With an available worker at dispatch, combining the worst-case formation wait of a full window \(\BatchingWindow\) with the service time of the 99th-percentile request-seen batch size \(\TailRequestSeenBatchSize\) gives the conservative formation-plus-service envelope in equation 12: \[\BatchLatencyEnvelope= \BatchingWindow+ \BatchServiceTime(\TailRequestSeenBatchSize) \tag{12}\] For \(\ArrivalRate\) = 500 QPS and \(\BatchingWindow\) = 10 ms, an average window sees five other arrivals plus the request itself, so the mean request-seen batch size is 6 and its p99 is 12. The mean latency is 8.7 ms, whereas the conservative envelope reaches 13.9 ms.

The envelope is 1.6× the mean, showing why a configuration whose mean sits comfortably inside the SLO can still breach it when a long formation wait coincides with an unusually large batch. Under the available-worker calendar-window assumptions, equation 12 is a conservative upper envelope rather than the exact p99 latency because it assumes the same request suffers both extremes at once: arriving at the very start of the window (waiting the full \(\BatchingWindow\)) and landing in a 99th-percentile batch (\(\TailRequestSeenBatchSize\)). A passing configuration clears only this formation-plus-service screen, so acceptance still requires evaluating worker backlog and the other request stages under the intended traffic and dispatch policy. A failing configuration can be rejected conservatively or investigated with a more faithful model and load test. Neither outcome alone establishes compliance with the end-to-end SLO.

Adaptive batching windows

Static batching windows impose a rigid trade-off that penalizes both traffic extremes. Under heavy traffic surges, a static timeout window holds early arrivals unnecessarily long after sufficient queries have already accumulated, consuming latency budget without enlarging the batch. Conversely, during off-peak periods, a fixed window frequently dispatches tiny, inefficient batches, failing to exploit accelerator arithmetic intensity. To navigate this tension, serving runtimes use adaptive batching policies that dynamically adjust the window duration based on instantaneous queue backlog, observed arrival rates, and the residual latency margin before the service-level objective expires. An adaptive window contracts during surges and expands during lulls, so it can lower average latency and raise realized batch size at the same time, within the remaining latency budget. Listing 2 implements one such heuristic.

The heuristic selects a candidate formation timer. Its inputs omit time already spent by the oldest request and work queued ahead of the batch, so the returned window does not establish a remaining deadline allowance. Deadline-aware dispatch must account for both quantities and validate the service and outside-worker budgets under load.

The nondominated batching configurations form a Pareto frontier where improving throughput requires accepting higher latency. Across five window and batch-cap configurations on the reference profile, from a 2 ms window capped at batch 8 to a 50 ms window capped at batch 128, modeled throughput rises from 2,100 img/s to 16,600 img/s, a gain of 680 percent. The modeled p99 envelope rises from 5.8 ms to 57.7 ms, 10× its initial value. Average latency over the same range grows from 4.8 ms to 32.7 ms.

Practical configuration guidelines

The frontier, like the knee of figure 6, shows why configuration must work backward from the latency budget. Past the knee, widening the window buys diminishing throughput for sharply rising tail latency. In the worked scenario, reserving 30 percent of the SLO for batching wait gives \(\BatchingWindow\leq 0.3 \times \LatencySlo\); another workload may allocate a different fraction after accounting for network, preprocessing, inference, and postprocessing. The traffic estimate should include peaks and burst duration rather than rely only on the mean. GPU memory supplies a separate ceiling on batch size through activations and runtime workspace. Finally, monitoring the realized batch-size and wait-time distributions reveals whether the assumed arrival model holds and whether a fixed or adaptive window is appropriate.

For an illustrative ResNet-50 serving system with a 50 ms SLO and 500 QPS traffic, the calculation provides starting estimates for two configuration knobs: the batching window and maximum batch size. Table 10 summarizes the resulting configuration and modeled operating point. The batch statistics and tail envelope use the calendar-window model without worker backlog; an oldest-request timer and maximum-batch trigger require a load test of their actual distributions. The saturated-capacity row of that table derates the ideal batch-32 rate by an assumed efficiency factor of 0.89, standing in for scheduling gaps and batches that dispatch before they fill.

Example 13.4: From a batching window to a dispatch decision
Problem: In a hypothetical trace of the selected dual-trigger configuration, four requests arrive at 0, 2, 5, and 9 ms. No further requests arrive before dispatch. The worker becomes available at 18 ms, and the remaining response path takes 4 ms. Does the oldest request meet the 50 ms deadline under the planning service-time model?

Analysis: The batch remains below the maximum count, so the oldest-request timer makes it eligible after 12 ms. The worker is still busy, adding 6 ms of resource-queue wait. The four-request batch then takes 3.6 ms under the same linear service model used for the window analysis. Adding the remaining response path gives 25.6 ms from the oldest arrival to completion.

Result: Dispatching the four-request batch meets the oldest request’s deadline with 24.4 ms of slack; the later arrivals finish with more slack. Waiting for the maximum batch would leave these requests stalled because the trace supplies no further arrivals. The timeout therefore determines eligibility, while worker availability determines actual dispatch.

Systems insight: The selected window passes this complete trace, including backlog and the response path. Establishing a percentile SLO requires repeating the accounting over the intended traffic, service-time variation, and input mix, with rejected requests and fallback quality reported alongside completed-request latency.

Working backward from the budget can also conclude that the right window is zero, because schedulers and dynamic memory allocators add coordination and synchronization overhead that hurts latency-critical or resource-constrained deployments. Under an ultra-low p99 target, even a brief formation delay consumes an unacceptable fraction of the latency budget. In runtimes without ragged (variable-length) tensor support, batching variable-length inputs pads every sequence to the longest item in the batch, which wastes work that single-request execution avoids completely. Memory capacity can rule out batching as well: when model weights already occupy most of the accelerator’s physical memory, concurrent batch activations and dynamic KV-cache allocations risk out-of-memory aborts, forcing the runtime to serve one request at a time.

13.5.5 Traffic patterns and batching strategy

A timeout window tuned for independent web API calls will stall a multi-camera robotics rig whose frames arrive in synchronized bursts, and it is useless on a smartphone that processes one user photo at a time. Because the right batching policy depends on how requests arrive, the MLPerf inference benchmark supplies four standardized scenarios that approximate common production arrival patterns.

Server traffic (Poisson arrivals)

The MLPerf Server scenario models cloud/API-like inference traffic with Poisson arrivals (Reddi et al. 2019), the arrival model behind the window statistics of section 13.5.4.2. A fixed calendar window collects \(\ArrivalRate \BatchingWindow\) requests on average, and that count fluctuates most, relative to its mean, at low traffic. Higher traffic can fill a desired batch sooner, permitting a shorter formation window. An oldest-request timer also includes the request that starts the window. Increasing the arrival rate therefore allows the same expected batch count with less deliberate waiting. The scheduler may instead target a larger batch if the latency and memory budgets permit it. Neither choice creates service capacity. The formed batches must complete fast enough to keep up with sustained arrivals, or resource backlog grows despite the shorter window. Window tuning must jointly check the arrival count, the service rate at the realized batch sizes, and the full request-latency distribution.

Reddi, Vijay Janapa, Christine Cheng, David Kanter, Peter Mattson, Guenther Schmuelling, Carole-Jean Wu, Brian Anderson, et al. 2019. “MLPerf Inference Benchmark.” 2020 ACM/IEEE 47th Annual International Symposium on Computer Architecture (ISCA), 446–59. https://doi.org/10.1109/isca45697.2020.00045.

Streaming traffic (correlated arrivals)

Video analytics, robotics, and other multi-sensor systems receive inputs from several synchronized sensors, so arrivals are correlated rather than independent. The batch size equals the number of sensors and the deadline equals the frame period, both fixed outside the server, and the binding quantity becomes arrival jitter. Inference cannot start until the last frame of a set lands, so jitter consumes the frame budget before any computation runs. Such pipelines bound latency with an explicit synchronization deadline, the latest batch start that still meets the frame deadline, and a fallback, such as reusing a sensor’s previous frame, when a frame misses it. The MultiStream scenario (MultiStream) approximates this shape for benchmarking.

Single-user traffic (sequential arrivals)

Synchronized sensors fix batch size and deadline outside the server. At the opposite end of the spectrum, interactive mobile and edge applications process one request stream at a time, leaving no opportunity to batch independent queries. The MLPerf SingleStream scenario captures this sequential-serving shape. Here, the optimization target shifts from a synchronization budget under a frame deadline to per-request latency and energy consumption under a rigid thermal envelope. For the smartphone vision pipeline modeled below, the primary engineering objective is not batch formation, but minimizing per-phase latency and energy dissipation.

Mobile serving constraints

Cloud services often emphasize fleet cost and latency; mobile serving adds three closely coupled constraints. The first is an energy budget that throughput alone does not capture, because each inference consumes battery. In the modeled pipeline, 2.98 mJ at 22 FPS draws about 66 mW for the inference path alone, before camera, display, ISP, and OS overhead add to that total in a full photo app. Thermal throttling can compound this limit when sustained system power reaches a device-specific thermal boundary. Memory constraints close the set because the model shares RAM with the application and operating system. Reload time depends on device storage, runtime, and model state; quantization and memory-mapped loading (section 13.8.2) can reduce footprint and startup work but do not eliminate those dependencies.

Example 13.5: ResNet-50: Mobile serving

Table 11 decomposes per-phase latency and energy for a single-user mobile vision inference:

The mobile serving node is governed by four metrics:

  • Energy per inference: 2.98 mJ enables ~12.1M inferences per 10 Wh battery (typical smartphone)
  • Modeled inference power: 2.98 mJ / 45 ms = 66 mW, excluding the rest of the device and therefore not a guarantee of indefinite operation
  • NPU vs. CPU trade-off: CPU fallback replaces the 12 ms, 0.8 mJ NPU inference stage with a 45 ms, 4.2 mJ CPU stage; the full pipeline would rise from 45 ms and 2.98 mJ to about 78 ms and 6.4 mJ before additional system overhead.
  • Memory footprint: 150 MB peak (model + activations), competing with app memory

Systems insight: In this illustrative pipeline, the mobile NPU reaches 82 percent utilization at batch size one while the chapter’s batch-one ResNet-50 request keeps the H100 busy only 41 percent of the time even before its tiny per-image arithmetic is counted. The contrast is workload- and hardware-specific, but it shows why a mobile accelerator designed for single-stream execution can operate efficiently without the batches used to amortize a large server GPU.

These physical constraints make edge serving optimization different from data-center optimization. Rather than maximizing aggregate batch throughput, the objective is to sustain real-time execution within a tight thermal and battery envelope without triggering hardware throttling or starving foreground applications of system memory.

Traffic pattern summary

Traffic-adaptive batching adjusts the batching window as queue depth and request rate change. The four MLPerf inference scenarios that MLPerf execution scenarios defines map onto these traffic shapes and the batching strategy each favors (table 12). These scenarios are benchmark abstractions rather than deployment prescriptions, so the operative batching policy must still follow the service’s current concurrency, deadline, and power constraints.

The examples developed here (cloud API traffic under the Server scenario, grouped sensor inputs motivated by MultiStream, and throughput-dominated pipelines under Offline inference) all decide how requests share the accelerator. Together with the latency budget and the queueing model, they complete one replica of the ResNet-50 service, which is where the chapter’s first half ends. None of them decides how efficiently the accelerator executes each batch once it forms. The same batch of the same model can run several times faster or slower depending on the engine that compiles and schedules its kernels and on the number format it computes in, and those choices are the next lever.

Checkpoint 13.3: Batching and traffic patterns

Batching is the primary lever for serving economics, but the optimal strategy depends on context.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Why can increasing batch size during deep learning inference improve hardware throughput on modern GPUs for weight-streaming workloads, and when does that improvement stop paying off on a Roofline model?

    1. Batching converts all floating-point operations into integer bitshifts, bypassing GPU ALUs entirely.
    2. Batching reduces the total parameter count of the neural network by sharing weights across batch samples.
    3. Batching eliminates PCIe bus data transfer overhead by generating synthetic input tensors on the device.
    4. Batching increases arithmetic intensity (FLOPs per byte of memory accessed) by reusing loaded weight matrices across multiple input samples, shifting the workload toward the compute ceiling until another limit or saturation is reached.

  2. A video-analytics node receives frames from 6 synchronized cameras that capture simultaneously at 30 Hz. Because these camera frames arrive in synchronized periodic bursts, this represents a ____ arrival process in the traffic taxonomy.

  3. A cloud serving system with an end-to-end latency budget of 50 ms configures a dynamic batcher with max_batch_size = 32 and max_batch_delay_ms = 20. Under low traffic, requests arrive deterministically 100 ms apart, the worker is idle when each request arrives, and no other requests arrive during any 20 ms batching window. What is the operational impact on latency and batch efficiency?

    1. Every request waits the full 20 ms timeout in the queue and executes as a batch of size 1, incurring the maximum batch-formation delay penalty with zero throughput amortization gain.
    2. Requests are automatically dropped because the queue fails to reach the maximum batch size of 32 within 50 ms.
    3. The server achieves peak GPU utilization because small batches execute with sub-microsecond latency.
    4. The dynamic batcher disables timeouts and waits indefinitely until 32 requests accumulate, preserving throughput at the cost of infinite latency.
