13.6 Inference Runtime Selection

Running an unchanged ResNet-50 model through a compiled C++ engine rather than an eager Python interpreter cuts batch-one latency on the reference H100 by more than fivefold, without altering a single weight. Dynamic batching and request scheduling govern how requests are grouped and queued, but an execution engine still has to map the resulting tensor operations onto physical execution units and memory hierarchies. The inference runtime, the software layer that schedules tensor operations and manages device resources, therefore changes both latency and throughput for a fixed model architecture. Runtime engineering proceeds in two steps: selection chooses the execution engine that fits the deployment target, and configuration tunes that engine for the model, hardware, input shapes, and latency budget.

13.6.1 Runtime constraint map

Attempting to deploy a Python-based server runtime onto a 256 KB microcontroller fails before the first request arrives, and exporting a model with custom operators to a mobile neural processing unit (NPU) delegate can silently push unsupported layers back onto the CPU. The deployment target narrows the viable runtime family before any speed comparison matters, and each tier in table 2 rules out entire classes of software. The keyword-spotting microcontroller in Static runtimes for microcontrollers requires a fixed memory arena and a compact C/C++ runtime. A smartphone, by contrast, can host an application runtime that routes supported operators to accelerator delegates and falls back to another path when an operator is missing. Within each target envelope, table 13 lists the candidate runtime families and the conversion or execution condition each candidate must pass before latency or memory numbers can be compared.

A runtime can fit the device and still fail to execute the exported model. Specialized inference runtimes such as TensorRT and Apache TVM require compatible operators and conversion paths; a mobile delegate may support only part of a graph. Unsupported regions can introduce fallback execution and extra transfers. Portability therefore requires an agreement about the graph and operator semantics on both sides of the export boundary.

13.6.2 Runtime ecosystem and configuration

Serving a model directly from the Python interpreter used to train it is the fastest path to a working endpoint, but under production load the interpreter itself becomes the bottleneck. Threads contend for the Global Interpreter Lock (GIL), operators launch one by one without whole-graph fusion, and dynamic allocations fragment memory. Engine selection therefore starts with the binding system constraint rather than the training framework. When rapid iteration and full support for dynamic Python control flow or custom operators matter most, PyTorch and TensorFlow models can serve directly through their native runtimes. When interpreter overhead limits throughput, serialized graph formats such as torch.export and TensorFlow SavedModel (with TorchScript supporting legacy deployments) capture a static operator graph for ahead-of-time optimization and multi-threaded C++ execution. Framework-native runtimes prioritize developer flexibility and broad operator coverage, whereas specialized inference engines trade that generality for whole-graph rewrites and lower launch overhead.

General-purpose optimization

The development framework and deployment runtime need not be the same system, but they must exchange a representation both can interpret. The ONNX format addresses this fragmentation by standardizing model exchange across runtimes (ONNX Contributors 2019). A pipeline can train an architecture in PyTorch, export the computation graph to an ONNX intermediate representation, and execute the serialized graph in ONNX Runtime or compile it for a target accelerator. In contrast, TensorFlow Lite relies on its own conversion path, translating TensorFlow SavedModels into FlatBuffers rather than routing through ONNX. Although standard interchange formats reduce manual model conversion between development and production environments, they do not eliminate compatibility testing, operator coverage gaps, or custom-kernel fallback overhead.

The exchange is hub-and-spoke around the common ONNX intermediate representation (figure 7). Training frameworks such as PyTorch, TensorFlow, Keras, Caffe2, MXNet, and CNTK export inward to the shared representation, and ONNX Runtime and other target frameworks load the serialized computation graph outward without reimplementing model code.

Interchange formats mitigate framework fragmentation, but an export boundary cannot salvage an architecture that violates the target’s physical limits. Converting a graph to ONNX or an embedded runtime after development completes cannot resolve an excessive memory footprint, missing hardware operators, or unsupported dynamic control flow. Treating deployment as an afterthought guarantees costly rewrites, which is why hardware and runtime trade-offs need evaluating before the first line of model code is written.

When cross-hardware portability is the binding constraint, ONNX Runtime14 provides an intermediate, hardware-agnostic execution layer (Microsoft 2024). Models export to the ONNX format, after which ONNX Runtime applies target-independent graph optimizations and delegates subgraphs to hardware-specific execution providers. This architecture enables single-format deployment across CPUs, GPUs, and custom accelerator application-specific integrated circuits (ASICs).

14 ONNX Runtime: Microsoft’s inference engine applies graph optimizations such as constant folding, redundant-node elimination, and kernel fusion before handing subgraphs to execution providers. Performance relative to a tuned target-specific engine such as TensorRT depends on the workload, execution provider, and hardware.

Microsoft. 2024. ONNX Runtime: Cross-Platform Inference and Training Machine-Learning Accelerator. GitHub.

Portability still demands profiling operator placement across execution boundaries. If an operation is unsupported by the preferred execution provider, placement depends on the configured provider order and available implementations. A CPU fallback can introduce host-accelerator transfers and synchronization that erode the expected latency advantage; if no provider supports the operation, execution may fail. Profiling must therefore verify provider assignment and operator coverage across the entire graph, not merely confirm that the exported artifact executes without errors.

Specialized inference engines

When latency budgets or serving costs bind more tightly than portability, specialized engines such as TensorRT for NVIDIA GPUs and OpenVINO15 for Intel hardware optimize graphs specifically for target microarchitectures (NVIDIA 2024a; Intel Corporation 2026; Chen et al. 2018). Rather than maintaining portable abstractions, these compilers specialize kernels directly to the hardware’s register files, cache hierarchies, and matrix execution units. A specialized engine such as TensorRT or TVM offers no advantage unless its backend supports the target and its conversion path supports the model (NVIDIA 2024a; Chen et al. 2018), and ONNX reduces the exchange burden without proving either condition (ONNX Contributors 2019).

15 OpenVINO (open visual inference and neural network optimization): An Intel-oriented inference toolkit that converts, optimizes, and runs models across Intel CPU, GPU, and NPU targets (Intel Corporation 2026). Specializing for direct hardware execution departs from the portable execution guarantees of general-purpose frameworks, enabling the compiler to emit architecture-tuned vector instructions (such as AVX-512 or AMX) and exploit low-precision integer execution units. While speedups depend on the specific operator mix and target processor, this specialization can make CPU or edge serving economically viable for latency-sensitive models.

Intel Corporation. 2026. OpenVINO Documentation.
Chen, Tianqi, Thierry Moreau, Ziheng Jiang, Lianmin Zheng, Eddie Q. Yan, Haichen Shen, Meghan Cowan, et al. 2018. “TVM: An Automated End-to-End Optimizing Compiler for Deep Learning.” Proceedings of the 13th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation (OSDI ’18), 578–94.
ONNX Contributors. 2019. ONNX: Open Neural Network Exchange.

16 Layer fusion: The term parallels loop fusion in compiler optimization, where adjacent loops over the same array are combined into one pass to reduce memory traffic.

Layer fusion16 is kernel fusion (also called operator fusion) applied when the engine is built. For the convolution \(\to\) batch normalization \(\to\) ReLU block, whose folding Compression-specific fusion derives, the serving compiler performs the fold once, at engine build time, because batch normalization is a fixed affine transform once training ends. It absorbs the normalization into the convolution weights and bias, then fuses the ReLU into the same kernel, so the input feature map is read from HBM once and only the activated result is written back.

↩︎ Kernel fusion, which merges adjacent operations so intermediates stay in registers and on-chip memory instead of round-tripping through HBM, is established in The memory wall and kernel fusion.

Kernel auto-tuning benchmarks and selects the optimal algorithm for each operation on the target microarchitecture. A single 2D convolution can be evaluated through direct convolution, GEMM transformation (im2col), Winograd minimal filtering (efficient for small \(3 \times 3\) filters), or Fast Fourier Transforms (efficient for large spatial filters), alongside dozens of tile-size variations. Because the winning strategy depends on filter dimensions, batch size, and the accelerator’s register and shared memory capacity, the compiler benchmarks candidate implementations against target hardware and caches the lowest-latency kernel, trading ahead-of-time compilation time for predictable serving latency. Together with operator fusion, these optimizations produce substantial speedups over an untuned framework-native baseline, though they require explicit graph compilation and may restrict dynamic tensor dimensions.

Example 13.6: ResNet-50: Runtime comparison
Table 14 applies the chapter’s profile to each engine at batch one on the reference H100, changing one term at a time. The eager row is the reference profile. TorchScript runs the same kernels from a graph executor, removing the per-operation Python dispatch. ONNX Runtime also fuses each convolution with its normalization, activation, and residual add, cutting 174 kernels to 56. TensorRT replays the whole fused pass as one captured graph and then varies only the number format.

Systems insight: Removing per-operation overhead, not lowering precision, produces most of the batch-one speedup. TensorRT FP32 is already 9.3× faster than eager execution, and INT8 adds only 1.3× over FP32, because once host overhead is gone the per-kernel floor dominates a single image. Precision pays at large batch, where per-image work dominates; INT8 also requires calibration data and NVIDIA-specific deployment.

This optimization-compatibility trade-off governs engine selection. Higher specialization can improve hardware efficiency for supported operations and shapes but increases deployment complexity, restricts dynamic control flow, and may introduce numerical deviations from the training baseline. The engineering choice depends on latency budgets, operational constraints, and how frequently model architectures evolve.

Once an execution engine is selected, configuration tunes its resource allocation to the hardware envelope. For CPU inference, thread pool sizes must match physical core counts: too few threads leave execution units idle, while too many cause CPU cache thrashing, OS scheduling delays, and lock contention across non-uniform memory access (NUMA) domains. For accelerators, static memory preallocation reserves fixed-size activation workspaces during initialization, removing runtime memory allocations (cudaMalloc) and page-table faults from the latency-critical request path. Execution provider priority determines which hardware backend handles each operator subgraph, while graph optimization levels balance build times against constant folding and algebraic simplification. Production deployments must benchmark these configurations across full latency distributions (p50, p99) rather than relying on framework defaults.

13.6.3 Precision selection for serving

On the reference H100, Tensor Cores execute FP16, FP8, and INT8 matrix operations, and each halving of the bytes per value doubles the dense peak rate. For ResNet-50, moving from FP32 to INT8 multiplies large-batch throughput several times over while sacrificing less than 0.4 percentage points of top-1 accuracy. Numerical precision is therefore coupled directly to infrastructure economics. At the serving layer, precision engineering introduces operational constraints: calibration data fidelity, layer sensitivity under live inputs, and dynamic precision dispatch.

↩︎ The quantization techniques this selection draws on, from numerical formats through calibration and quantization-aware training, are established in Numerical Precision: Changing How Values Are Stored.

Precision-throughput relationship

For memory-bandwidth-bound operations, reducing precision raises throughput by cutting the byte volume transferred across the memory bus. Equation 13 gives the bandwidth-only upper bound from precision reduction: \[ \frac{\text{Throughput}_{\text{INT8}}}{\text{Throughput}_{\text{FP32}}} = \frac{32}{8} = 4\times \text{ (theoretical maximum)} \tag{13}\]

Actual speedup depends on the compute-to-memory ratio, operator mix, hardware instruction throughput, and matrix alignment, falling below the bandwidth-only bound whenever arithmetic throughput or memory alignment binds. Low-precision kernels achieve peak arithmetic efficiency only when matrix dimensions align with the accelerator’s warp-level execution tiles (for example, multiples of 8 or 16 elements). When dimensions do not align with hardware tiles, libraries such as cuBLAS and cuDNN pad tensors or fall back to slower execution paths.

↩︎ Tensor Core tile architectures and their matrix-dimension alignment constraints are established in Tensor Cores.

Example 13.7: ResNet-50: Precision trade-offs on the H100
Table 15 compares FP32, FP16, and two INT8 paths for ResNet-50 on the reference H100: batch-one latency for each TensorRT engine of the chapter’s profile, throughput at large batch, weight bytes, and assumed accuracy:

Systems insight: At batch one, INT8 is only 1.3× faster than FP32, because the fixed per-kernel cost dominates a single image. At large batch it sustains about 6× FP32’s throughput and loses 0.33 percentage points of accuracy with PTQ. QAT recovers most of that loss but requires retraining.

Integer formats are not the only 8-bit option. On the reference H100, an 8-bit floating-point format (FP8, whose E4M3 layout for weights and activations Numerical Precision: Changing How Values Are Stored introduced) is the natural serving default for transformer workloads. The H100’s dense FP8 peak of 1,979 TFLOP/s is 2× its FP16 peak of 989 TFLOP/s, and each value occupies one byte instead of two. For the chapter’s GPT-2 XL service, FP8 weights shrink from 3 GB to 1.5 GB, which lowers the bandwidth floor on a batch-one decode step from 0.9 ms to 0.45 ms. Because the format keeps a floating-point exponent, it tolerates the wide activation ranges of transformers better than INT8, though it still needs per-tensor scale factors and an accuracy check before deployment.

Precision selection constraints

Quantizing every layer of a network to INT8 indiscriminately, or calibrating scale factors on daytime studio photos before serving nighttime camera feeds, can cost several percentage points of accuracy. Precision selection is constrained by layer sensitivity, calibration data, and runtime policy. Not all layers tolerate reduced precision equally. The error bound of the uniform quantizer, half a quantization step inside the calibrated clipping range plus saturation error outside it (Numerical Precision: Changing How Values Are Stored, with the step from equation 10.1), limits what each layer must absorb. Layer-level output or task error depends on activation distributions and downstream sensitivity, so it must be measured rather than inferred from a universal norm law. This mechanism explains why first convolutional and final classification layers are commonly retained at FP16, while intermediate layers tolerate INT8. Initial layers process low-dimensional raw inputs (such as 3 RGB channels) before representations expand into overcomplete feature spaces that can absorb quantization noise. Final classification layers, by contrast, produce raw logits where small numerical perturbations directly shift decision boundaries and alter top-\(1\) predictions.

Post-training quantization adds a data dependency. The calibration dataset determines the clipping thresholds \([\ClippingRangeLowerBound, \ClippingRangeUpperBound]\) and scale factors \(\QuantizationScale\) used for INT8 conversion, requiring it to match the statistical profile of live serving traffic rather than arbitrary training or validation splits. A calibration-serving distribution mismatch causes excessive clipping or underflow, degrading end-to-end task accuracy, an operational pitfall analyzed in section 13.11.

Production serving architectures can also turn precision from a static compilation setting into a dynamic runtime policy. When request queues are shallow and the system operates with ample latency headroom against its service level objective (SLO), the engine can dispatch requests at FP16 to maximize task accuracy. During traffic spikes that threaten queue saturation, the engine can dynamically switch execution to INT8 to preserve latency bounds. Similarly, requests that yield low classification confidence under INT8 can be selectively re-evaluated at FP16, trading a small accuracy margin for lower latency when traffic spikes.

Precision selection carries direct infrastructure sizing consequences. Under the large-batch throughput gain of table 15, 10 GPUs at INT8 offer the same aggregate saturated capacity as about 59 GPUs at FP32. Equal capacity does not establish equal queueing latency; service-time distributions, batching policy, and failure reserve must still satisfy the same SLO. Precision engineering is therefore an infrastructure sizing discipline, directly coupling numerical representation to cluster capital expense and energy consumption. Yet configuring the runtime and numerical bit width addresses only the model level; achieving low-latency serving in production requires optimizing the host-to-device boundaries within the physical server node itself.

Self-Check: Question

  1. An ML systems team is deploying a transformer-based ranking model on NVIDIA data center GPUs. They consider running the raw PyTorch model in eager Python mode versus compiling the model into an optimized NVIDIA TensorRT engine. Which graph-level serving optimization is TensorRT designed to apply more aggressively than an unoptimized eager execution path?

    1. TensorRT automatically converts the supervised classification task into an unsupervised reinforcement learning policy.
    2. TensorRT trains additional adapter layers during live request serving to adapt to data drift.
    3. TensorRT eliminates all GPU memory usage by running inferences exclusively in the CPU instruction cache.
    4. TensorRT performs aggressive vertical and horizontal operator fusion (e.g., combining Conv/MatMul, BiasAdd, and Activation into a single kernel), reduces redundant memory round-trips, and selects hardware-tuned kernels.

  2. Why can quantizing a model’s weights and activations from FP16 to INT8 for GPU serving increase throughput, what limits the gain, and what role does Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) calibration play in preserving accuracy?

  3. True or False: In deep neural network quantization, when sensitivity evaluation identifies particular layers as unusually vulnerable to precision loss, preserving those layers in FP16/FP32 while quantizing the remaining bulk layers to INT8/INT4 can preserve model accuracy in mixed-precision serving.

  4. When deploying models to edge devices with Apple Silicon (such as iPhones and iPads), developers export models to the ____ framework to take full advantage of the dedicated Apple Neural Engine (ANE) hardware accelerator.

  5. When calibrating an INT8 quantized model using Post-Training Quantization (PTQ), what is the primary danger of using a synthetic or unrepresentative calibration dataset (e.g., ImageNet validation images for a medical endoscopy model)?

    1. The activation dynamic ranges and outlier distributions in production may be misrepresented by the calibration dataset, causing clipping of real-world features or overly coarse quantization scales that degrade serving accuracy.
    2. The model compiler will fail to generate valid GPU assembly code and reject the weights.
    3. The server’s physical power consumption will double due to misaligned integer registers.
    4. The dynamic batcher will permanently lock its batch size to 1.

13.7 Node-Level Optimization

Runtime selection and precision tuning operate at the model level, determining what computation runs and what numerical format it uses. In the reference ResNet-50 service, however, the forward pass accounts for 3.5 ms out of 8.6 ms end to end, and once a compiled engine shrinks that forward pass to 0.33 ms, host preprocessing alone consumes 83 percent of the remaining time. An isolated model benchmark hides those host-side costs: JPEG decoding on CPU cores, copies across the PCIe bus, and the driver work that launches each kernel.

Between the model graph and the silicon lies a steady-state execution layer that governs how smoothly a warmed node feeds its accelerator (while the storage and deserialization path that initializes a new replica is developed in section 13.8 and section 13.8.2). Node-level optimization targets the active request path between the network socket and the execution units across three physical boundaries:

  • Graph-to-kernel boundary: Translating computation graphs into numerous fine-grained kernel launches incurs driver serialization overhead and forces intermediate tensors through device HBM. Graph compilers fuse adjacent operations into composite kernels that preserve intermediate activations in on-chip SRAM or register files.
  • Host-accelerator boundary: Accelerators sit idle when the host processor fails to enqueue work ahead of execution. Unpipelined copies across host-device interconnects (such as PCIe) and blocking synchronization calls stall accelerator compute pipelines.
  • CPU execution boundary: Host preprocessing pipelines and CPU-based inference degrade when executed through interpreted scalar code rather than vectorized single instruction, multiple data (SIMD) instruction units and NUMA-local memory allocations, and when operating-system noise preempts the threads that feed the accelerator.

Each boundary represents a distinct physical constraint where a live request stalls on memory bandwidth, bus transit, or OS scheduling noise rather than peak arithmetic throughput. Crossing them from the accelerator outward moves from runtime graph capture and host resource isolation to CPU-native execution and end-to-end timeline profiling.

13.7.1 Runtime graph compilation

Crossing the first of those boundaries, the graph-to-kernel interface, without compilation leaves the host CPU submitting tiny kernels one operator at a time across a 32-layer transformer or 50-layer convnet, often spending more time in the driver than the GPU spends on the math. Because serving workloads run fixed operator graphs over bounded input shapes (section 13.6), the specialized engines of section 13.6.2.2 apply kernel fusion and compile-time constant folding (such as folding batch normalization into preceding convolution weights) ahead of deployment, while static memory planning (Memory planning) preallocates a single arena for non-overlapping tensor lifetimes so allocator calls stay off the request path.

Beyond memory planning and kernel fusion, static execution paths can use CUDA Graphs, the replay of a captured sequence of kernel launches that Graph capture and JIT compilation developed. Each eager submission pays the 5–20 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) of host launch work quantified in section 13.5.1, and total submission cost depends on the executed kernel count, not the number of layers. A decode step launching 8 to 10 sub-kernels across each of 32 transformer layers can accumulate several milliseconds of host submission work, longer than the accelerator needs for the tensor math at small batch sizes. Serving systems therefore capture the graph during warmup, once per supported batch size because replay requires static shapes and fixed buffer addresses. They dispatch it during live inference with a single driver invocation (illustratively \(\sim 5\,\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\), depending on the graph, driver, and hardware). Replay removes repeated host submission work without eliminating device execution costs.

Systems Perspective 13.4: Compilation timing trade-off
Just-in-time compilation defers graph optimization until the runtime engine observes concrete input shapes and execution paths. This runtime visibility enables aggressive kernel specialization for dynamic control flow and variable sequence lengths, but it injects compilation overhead directly into the serving path, generating multi-second latency spikes on early requests.

Ahead-of-time compilation executes graph analysis, kernel fusion, and hardware-specific auto-tuning prior to deployment. Pre-building removes the covered compilation work from the request path, but weights, device contexts, allocations, and representative execution paths still require loading and warmup. The compiled engine remains constrained to its supported shape profiles and may require padding or multi-profile compilation to accommodate dynamic input sizes.

13.7.2 Deterministic latency and resource isolation

Noisy neighbors stretch identical forward passes unevenly, widening the tail.

Compilation fixes what the accelerator executes; it does not control how steadily the host feeds it. The server shares its host with other work. On a single host, the operating system multiplexes hardware across background telemetry daemons, logging agents, and kernel worker threads. When these unisolated processes contend for execution resources, they intermittently preempt the inference pipeline. These noisy neighbors are a primary source of latency jitter, where the physical time required to execute identical forward passes fluctuates unpredictably. This contention inflates 99th percentile (p99) tail latency even when average hardware utilization remains low. Resource contention thus widens tail latency through service-time variance, a physical degradation distinct from the queuing-delay accumulation along the saturation knee plotted in figure 5.

Keeping latency predictable requires isolating the serving process from the operating system’s default time-sharing scheduler. Serving systems such as Clockwork demonstrate that deep neural network (DNN) inference execution times can be predictable down to sub-millisecond tolerances under their controlled workload and hardware conditions (Gujarati et al. 2020). CPU affinity (thread pinning) establishes the baseline compute boundary by binding inference worker threads to designated physical cores. Without pinning, the OS scheduler periodically migrates threads between cores, losing access to warm private L1 and L2 cache contents and requiring lines to be refetched from shared L3 cache or DRAM. On multi-socket architectures, thread migration across NUMA boundaries is even more penalizing. Accesses to memory left on the previous socket cross inter-socket interconnects, adding tens of nanoseconds of latency per transaction. Pinning threads to specific cores and binding their memory allocations to local NUMA nodes reduces migration-related cache misses and remote-socket memory traffic.

Gujarati, Arpan, Reza Karimi, Safya Alzayat, Wei Hao, Antoine Kaufmann, Ymir Vigfusson, and Jonathan Mace. 2020. “Clockwork: Predictable and Scalable DNN Inference in the Cloud.” USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation (OSDI), 443–62.

Even with pinned CPU cores, memory access latency remains vulnerable to the virtual memory subsystem. Under host memory pressure, the kernel can page inactive address ranges out to disk. When the CPU touches a paged-out address, execution can stall for milliseconds while the page returns to DRAM. Host buffers used for PCIe DMA also need resident, registered backing pages during the transfer. Memory locking (mlock) instructs the kernel to pin specified virtual address ranges into physical host RAM, preventing page eviction for host-resident weights, input staging buffers, and offloaded key-value (KV) cache blocks. This removes eviction-related page faults at the cost of host memory flexibility; locked pages remain resident until unlocked, while cache misses and memory contention can still delay access. Because mlock operates strictly on host page tables, it does not apply to accelerator HBM, which is allocated directly by the accelerator driver.

Beyond scheduled processes, asynchronous hardware interrupts introduce another source of sub-millisecond preemption. When a network interface card (NIC) receives packet bursts, it triggers hardware interrupt requests (IRQs) that force the handling CPU core to immediately suspend user-space execution and process kernel interrupt handlers. If the OS routes these interrupts to cores running the inference engine’s submission loop, the CPU pauses command dispatch to the accelerator. Because GPUs process short inference layers in microseconds, even brief CPU submission stalls drain the accelerator’s hardware command ring buffer, causing pipeline bubbles where compute units sit idle waiting for the next kernel descriptor. Interrupt shielding restricts IRQ affinities to dedicated housekeeping cores, reducing preemption of the GPU dispatch thread by those device interrupts.

Isolating physical cores, locking host memory, and shielding interrupt lines reduce specific sources of operating system noise, though the resulting service-time distribution still needs measurement under the intended load. Thus far, isolation has treated the host as a predictable partner that feeds a discrete accelerator; when deployment constraints or workload profiles dispense with the accelerator altogether, the host CPU becomes the executor itself.

13.7.3 CPU inference optimization

For a small fraud-detection or ranking model evaluated at batch size one, copying a single request across PCIe and launching GPU kernels through the driver can take longer than running the forward pass directly on the host processor. Deploying inference on CPUs therefore remains practical for lightweight models, sparse or bursty request streams that cannot saturate an accelerator, and services where host-to-device transfer time exceeds the math time. Yet an untuned Python CPU path leaves most of the processor’s silicon idle. Modern server CPUs17 (such as Intel Xeon and AMD EPYC) include wide SIMD vector registers and matrix units (AVX-512 and AMX), which specialized runtimes such as OpenVINO target by tiling matrix operations across L1/L2 caches and emitting packed fused multiply-add (FMA) instructions (Intel Corporation 2026).

17 SIMD: From Michael Flynn’s 1966 taxonomy of computer architectures, SIMD enables one instruction to operate on multiple data elements simultaneously. Intel’s AVX-512 can process sixteen FP32 values in a 512-bit vector, while AMX adds matrix tile operations. The attainable fraction of peak depends on data type, matrix shape, memory behavior, and implementation, but optimized vector kernels provide multiple-fold speedups over scalar execution.

The NUMA boundaries that thread pinning respects matter more when the CPU itself runs the model. Memory controllers are physically distributed across CPU sockets and linked by coherent interconnects (such as Intel UPI or AMD Infinity Fabric). Accessing memory attached to a remote socket incurs substantially higher latency and lower bandwidth than local channel access. An inference server must maintain NUMA awareness. Worker threads must be pinned to dedicated physical cores, and the weight tensors and request buffers those threads touch must reside in memory nodes local to that socket. Because model weights, at hundreds of megabytes to tens of gigabytes, routinely exceed per-socket L3 cache capacities, every forward pass streams weights from main RAM. Mismatched socket allocations force continuous weight streaming across the slower inter-socket interconnect, creating severe tail-latency degradation.

For lightweight models evaluated at batch size one, a CPU frequently outperforms a discrete GPU. When an accelerator processes an isolated request, the fixed overhead of transferring inputs across the host PCIe bus (several microseconds), driver queue serialization, and kernel launch overhead (5–20 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\) per kernel) can easily surpass the arithmetic execution time. The CPU processes the request directly in local cache without bus transfers or driver queue delays. Determining the operational crossover requires empirical measurement across model architecture, arithmetic intensity, batch size, and latency constraints.

13.7.4 Profiling the serving node

Finding that crossover—or diagnosing why an accelerator-backed node misses its p99 target while average GPU utilization sits below 30 percent—requires inspecting where each request actually waits across the host and device. The top-down profiling method of Identifying Bottlenecks applies unchanged to a serving node, where the objective is useful throughput that meets the latency SLO. The duty-cycle metric defined in equation 2 helps locate idle intervals, but active time alone does not measure arithmetic efficiency or prove adequate queueing headroom. Timeline profilers, such as PyTorch Profiler and NVIDIA Nsight Systems (nsys), record concurrent CPU threads, OS runtime calls, memory transfers, and GPU hardware queues along a synchronized timeline.

Serving traces show three recurring signatures:

  • Idle compute bubbles: White space on the GPU execution track indicates hardware starvation. Compute warps idle while waiting for the host CPU to complete request preprocessing (e.g., tokenization or image decoding), page faults during on-demand weight paging, or blocking inter-thread synchronization.
  • Kernel launch fragmentation: A sequence of micro-kernels, each executing in under 5–10 \(\MicroUnitPrefix\text{s}\), suggests launch overhead as a candidate. Host launch timing and gaps between device kernels reveal whether submission starves the GPU; if so, graph capture or legal fusion may help.
  • Interconnect transfer stalls: Synchronous MemcpyHtoD blocks on the primary CUDA stream indicate unpipelined host-to-device data movement. Overcoming this stall requires allocating page-locked (pinned) host memory and executing asynchronous transfers on dedicated copy streams to overlap bus transit with concurrent accelerator execution on preceding requests.

Example 13.8: The profiling loop
Scenario: An inference serving node reports high p99 tail latency despite low average GPU compute utilization.

Diagnosis: Timeline profiling (Nsight Systems/PyTorch Profiler) reveals large idle gaps between tiny GPU kernels caused by CPU host-side preprocessing bottlenecks and kernel launch overhead.

Systems lesson: Serving node optimization requires empirical timeline profiling. Iteratively identifying trace bottlenecks (kernel launch overhead, host-device copy stalls) guides targeted optimizations like kernel fusion and pinned memory.

Table 16 summarizes representative node-level optimization techniques alongside their primary target metrics and engineering costs. The table serves as a decision matrix rather than an exhaustive checklist. Engineering effort should be directed exclusively to the row that addresses the binding bottleneck identified on the measured execution trace.

This diagnostic hierarchy ensures engineering resources address structural bottlenecks along the entire request path rather than applying isolated micro-optimizations. A four-level audit checkpoint structures this prioritization from external service boundaries down to device kernels.

Checkpoint 13.4: The optimization hierarchy

Optimizing inference follows the request path from the outside in across four structural levels:

A node tuned this way runs each request near its physical limit, but only once it is running. The compiled engine that makes the steady state fast took tens of seconds to build, its weights must cross storage, host memory, and the PCIe bus before the first request arrives, and its preprocessing must match what training used. How a replica reaches that steady state, and whether it computes what training computed once there, decides whether the fast node is also a correct and available one.

Self-Check: Question

  1. In a CPU-based model serving deployment, how do vector and matrix extensions such as AVX-512 vector registers and Advanced Matrix Extensions (AMX) tiles accelerate inference throughput compared to standard scalar x86 execution?

  2. To reduce OS scheduler thread migration and cross-socket memory traffic on multi-socket CPU serving nodes, engineers pin worker threads to dedicated physical cores and place each worker’s weights in the memory attached to the same socket, making the server ____-aware.

  3. Order the iterative steps of an end-to-end performance profiling workflow for diagnosing an inference serving bottleneck:

  1. Inspect timeline traces (e.g., via Nsight Systems or PyTorch Profiler) to identify gaps, CUDA stream stalls, CPU-GPU synchronization, and memory bandwidth utilization
  2. Implement targeted optimization (e.g., kernel fusion, precision reduction, or CPU pipelining) on the identified bottleneck stage
  3. Establish a reproducible baseline by driving realistic synthetic load with an open-loop load generator and measuring latency percentiles (p50, p99)
  4. Re-benchmark under identical load to verify latency reduction and ensure model prediction accuracy remains intact

    1. nvidia-smi measures fan speed and ambient temperature rather than compute kernel activity.
    2. The GPU memory clock is automatically halved during Nsight Systems tracing.
    3. The CPU is running at 100% duty cycle, which forces nvidia-smi to report false GPU metrics.
    4. nvidia-smi reports the fraction of sampled time with one or more kernels active on the GPU, not how efficiently those kernels used the compute units.

13.8 Model Loading and Versioning

Every optimization so far assumed a replica that is already warmed up in device memory and a host pipeline that reproduces the exact transformations used during training. Both assumptions fail at the edges of a replica’s lifecycle: provisioning a new container or swapping an active model stalls traffic until weights and compiled engines are resident on the device, and any drift between training and serving preprocessing silently corrupts accuracy even when every latency target is met.

13.8.1 Cold start and initialization dynamics

When an autoscaler spins up a new serving container during a traffic surge, that replica can sit dark for 35 seconds before answering its first request, most of them spent compiling an execution engine after fetching weights from remote storage (Romero et al. 2021). That initialization delay, known as cold start, governs how quickly a service recovers from node failures or scales out into a spike.

Romero, Francisco, Qian Li, Neeraja J. Yadwadkar, and Christos Kozyrakis. 2021. “INFaaS: Automated Model-Less Inference Serving.” 2021 USENIX Annual Technical Conference (USENIX ATC 21), 397–411.
Definition 13.4: Cold start

Cold start is the initialization latency incurred when starting a new model replica.

  1. Significance: It represents the fixed startup cost of loading weights, compiling graphs, and allocating buffers, which can take seconds or minutes and block elastic scaling during traffic bursts.
  2. Distinction: Unlike inference latency \((\InferenceLatency)\), which is a per-request cost, cold start is an initialization cost paid when a replica or model instance is created, including deployment, scale-out, recovery, and on-demand model loading.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that cold start is “just loading weights.” In reality, it includes graph compilation and memory allocation, which can often take longer than the bandwidth-limited data transfer itself \((\DataVolume/\Bandwidth)\).

Total cold-start latency compounds across four physical operations along the initialization path. Weight loading streams serialized parameters from persistent storage across the network or host I/O subsystem into host memory and then across the PCIe bus into GPU HBM. Graph compilation then lowers the framework computational graph into device-specific machine code by fusing operators and auto-tuning matrix multiplication kernels for the target accelerator. Once the compiled graph is ready, memory allocation reserves device arenas for activation scratchpads and intermediate buffers. Warmup18 execution then runs synthetic inferences to populate kernel caches, prime framework memory allocators, and complete any remaining lazy initialization before live traffic arrives.

18 Warmup: Borrowed from just-in-time (JIT) compilation, where initial executions compile hot paths into optimized machine code; for ML serving, warmup inferences trigger CUDA kernel compilation, cuDNN algorithm auto-tuning, and memory pool allocation that frameworks defer until first use.

NVIDIA. 2026a. Lazy Loading: CUDA Programming Guide.

Establishing a CUDA context is the first fixed cost on the device initialization timeline. Before launching any GPU kernel or allocating device memory, the runtime must instantiate a driver-level context: an internal control structure that manages virtual address spaces, loaded kernel binaries, and hardware event queues. Creating this context requires synchronous communication with the kernel-mode driver and allocates tens to hundreds of megabytes of device memory for bookkeeping. The 0.3–0.5 s range in table 17 reflects a typical data center environment. While CUDA lazy loading defers kernel module loading to shorten this initial pause, it merely defers that latency penalty to the first executed inference (NVIDIA 2026a).

In multi-process serving deployments where multiple worker processes share a single accelerator, replicating individual CUDA contexts wastes both memory capacity and initialization time. CUDA Multi-Process Service (MPS) coordinates concurrent work from multiple client processes, reducing scheduling overhead when individual processes underuse the device (NVIDIA 2026d). Its isolation behavior depends on the architecture. Pre-Volta clients shared a GPU address space; Volta clients retain separate GPU address spaces and contexts while their kernels can execute concurrently. Separate address spaces do not provide hard fault containment. A fatal GPU fault can affect other clients sharing the affected GPU, so the serving design must budget for correlated replica failures.

NVIDIA. 2026d. NVIDIA CUDA Multi-Process Service.

Example 13.9: ResNet-50: Cold start timeline
Table 17 traces the per-phase duration of a ResNet-50 cold start:

Systems insight: Precompiling models and storing the optimized engine eliminates the 30-second compilation phase on subsequent deployments.

Without warmup, the first live request absorbs deferred JIT compilation, driver memory allocation, and kernel launch overhead. A request whose steady-state forward pass takes 3.5 ms can stall for hundreds of milliseconds during an unmitigated cold start, violating strict SLOs. Controlling this initialization overhead requires systematic selection among storage, memory, and runtime loading strategies.

13.8.2 Loading strategies and serialization formats

Once precompilation removes the 30-second compiler step from table 17, weight loading becomes the largest remaining term in the startup budget, and how the server schedules that read trades cold-start duration against first-request latency and memory footprint. The simplest approach, full loading, reads the entire model into memory before the server marks itself ready. This maximizes steady-state inference speed because all weights are resident in memory, but it stretches cold-start time and restricts model size to available memory. A second strategy, lazy initialization, defers compilation and allocation until first use. This minimizes startup time but shifts latency to the first request, so production systems often combine lazy initialization with synthetic warmup requests to trigger initialization before real traffic arrives.

Under either loading schedule, reading a multi-gigabyte checkpoint from persistent storage into host memory dominates the remaining initialization time once compilation is moved offline. Whether that read runs at NVMe bandwidth or stalls on single-threaded CPU parsing depends on the checkpoint’s serialization format.

Legacy PyTorch checkpoints loaded via torch.load() package weights within a zip archive containing pickle-encoded object metadata. Deserializing a pickle file forces the CPU to interpret a stream of serialization opcodes sequentially, allocating individual Python objects (dictionaries, metadata wrappers, and tensor headers) and potentially copying tensor bytes into newly allocated memory. Restricted loading modes reduce arbitrary code execution risks, while memory-mapped options can avoid some eager copies. Whether parsing or storage dominates depends on the checkpoint, loading path, and hardware.

A tensor-oriented format19 bypasses interpreter-driven object construction. Safetensors organizes checkpoints with a lightweight header containing tensor metadata followed by contiguous, uncompressed tensor byte buffers aligned to memory boundaries. Because the on-disk binary layout mirrors the in-memory array layout, the runtime can use memory mapping (mmap(2)), which maps file blocks directly into the process’s virtual address space. Virtual memory pages fault into host RAM on access or are prefetched sequentially by the operating system, reducing Python object reconstruction and avoiding eager user-space tensor copies on the mapped path. Mapping shortens apparent startup, but a dense model eventually touches nearly all of its weights, so deferred page faults can move loading latency into early requests. The benefit is greater for sparsely accessed components or model repositories that load subsets on demand; latency-sensitive services still prefault or warm the pages needed on the request path. On systems with discrete accelerators, transferring these tensors across PCIe or NVLink is still required before computation begins, so memory-mapped host loading reduces CPU reconstruction costs without eliminating page faults, storage reads, or device initialization.

19 Safetensors: The name emphasizes safety: unlike unrestricted pickle deserialization, the format does not encode executable Python objects (Hugging Face 2026). It stores tensors as contiguous raw bytes with a small header and supports memory-mapped loading. In the local benchmark example, that path is 10× faster than the compared PyTorch checkpoint path; the result is workload- and storage-specific and does not include transfer to a discrete accelerator.

Hugging Face. 2026. Safetensors Documentation.

Example 13.10: Loading speed: Safetensors vs. Pickle
Scenario: A serverless inference replica loads a 5 GB model checkpoint during cold-start scaling events.

Diagnosis: PyTorch torch.load() (Pickle) requires CPU object reconstruction, taking 15 s to initialize. Safetensors memory-mapping bypasses CPU parsing, loading weights in 1.5 s (10× faster).

Systems lesson: Tensor-oriented, memory-mapped formats can reduce CPU reconstruction and eager-copy overhead during cold start. Storage throughput, page faults, runtime initialization, and accelerator transfer still remain in the scale-out budget.

13.8.3 Model caching infrastructure

A zero-copy format like Safetensors can only load as fast as the underlying storage tier delivers bytes, so production systems pair fast checkpoint formats with infrastructure-level weight caching matched to how often the model changes. Embedding the weights in the container image yields a single deployment artifact with no network fetch at startup, but images of 10–50 GB slow every container pull, so the approach suits models that rarely update. A shared filesystem of versioned weights lets many replicas read one copy without rebuilding images, at the price of network latency in every cold start and a new availability dependency. It still requires an explicit, atomic version switch so that files never change underneath a live replica. Node-local SSD caching prepopulates Non-Volatile Memory Express (NVMe) drives on inference nodes with frequently used models and loads at several GB/s without the network, but it needs its own refresh policy as models update. Which versions those caches hold, and how a new version replaces an old one in production, belongs to the release process of Model deployment; once those cached weights reach a node, the next question is how multiple models or versions share its GPU memory.

13.8.4 Multi-model serving

Dedicating an entire GPU to each regional model, A/B candidate, or ensemble stage leaves most of the fleet idle whenever traffic to individual models is uneven, yet packing several models onto one GPU without coordination risks out-of-memory crashes and noisy-neighbor latency spikes. Production systems therefore share a single machine across multiple models, making GPU memory capacity and bandwidth isolation the primary constraints. How a server manages that shared memory depends on whether models fit in VRAM simultaneously. When the combined footprint exceeds device capacity, time-multiplexing loads one model at a time and swaps weights on demand as routing changes, keeping implementation simple at the cost of swap latency. When several models fit together, memory sharing partitions GPU memory among them so that more models remain resident without swapping, even though each model receives a smaller concurrency budget. Across both regimes, model virtualization—as implemented by frameworks like Triton—decouples model lifecycle from application code by exposing repository and control APIs that load, unload, and version models dynamically (NVIDIA 2024b, 2026c, 2026b). Which policy wins depends on the arrival mix. Steady traffic across models favors keeping them concurrently resident, whereas bursty, model-specific traffic favors time-multiplexing with explicit preloading to balance average latency against GPU utilization.

NVIDIA. 2026c. Model Repository: NVIDIA Triton Inference Server.
NVIDIA. 2026b. Model Management: NVIDIA Triton Inference Server.

Partitioning one GPU across models

When multiple models or multiple instances of the same model must run concurrently on a single GPU, the hardware must partition resources between them. NVIDIA’s Multi-Instance GPU20 technology enables hardware-level isolation, dividing a data-center GPU such as the H100 into up to seven independent GPU instances, each with dedicated memory and compute resources (NVIDIA 2026e). On GPUs without MIG, CUDA stream scheduling permits overlapping or time-multiplexed work without hardware partitioning. The choice depends on whether consistent latency with MIG or maximum utilization with shared streams is the priority.

20 MIG (multi-instance GPU): Introduced with NVIDIA’s A100 (NVIDIA Corporation 2020) and documented across supported data-center GPUs (NVIDIA 2026e), MIG partitions a single physical GPU into independent instances, each with dedicated compute and memory resources; unlike software sharing (MPS or time-slicing), MIG provides hardware-level isolation between partitions. The trade-off is granularity, because partitions must follow fixed profiles, so resources cannot be divided arbitrarily. For multi-model serving, MIG reduces noisy-neighbor risk on shared hardware, while per-model SLO guarantees still depend on scheduler policy, load, and the selected partition profile.

NVIDIA Corporation. 2020. NVIDIA A100 Tensor Core GPU Architecture. NVIDIA Whitepaper, V1.0.
NVIDIA. 2026e. NVIDIA Multi-Instance GPU User Guide.

Model swapping and host memory

When the aggregate size of all models exceeds GPU memory capacity, the serving system must swap models between host dynamic random-access memory (DRAM) and device memory (VRAM) on demand. This introduces a new latency component determined by the PCIe bus bandwidth. For a 10 GB model on PCIe Gen4 x16 (32 GB/s theoretical bandwidth), loading takes at least 312.5 ms before deserialization, graph setup, or warmup. This raw transfer latency dwarfs a tight service-level objective such as a 50 ms deadline by more than \(6\times\), rendering on-demand model swapping unviable on the interactive request path.

Model loading lands far outside a tight serving SLO.

To accelerate this transfer path, systems keep the weights in pinned memory (page-locked host RAM). The GPU’s DMA copy engines require the source pages to stay at fixed physical addresses for the whole transfer, but ordinary heap memory is pageable, and the operating system may relocate or swap its pages at any time. A transfer from a pageable buffer therefore takes two stages: the CPU first copies the data into a pinned staging buffer, and the DMA engine then moves that buffer across PCIe. Allocating model weights with cudaHostAlloc removes the CPU staging copy; achieved bandwidth still depends on transfer size, memory locality, and contention. What serving adds is a capacity trade-off. Pinned memory cannot be paged out or reclaimed by the OS under memory pressure, so every pinned model holds its share of physical host RAM until it is released. High-throughput serving systems therefore pin active model weights and I/O ring buffers while leaving standby model checkpoints in pageable memory.

↩︎ Pinned memory and the DMA staging copy it avoids are established in Frameworks as the operating system interface.

13.8.5 Training-serving skew

↩︎ Training-serving skew and the consistency imperative it violates are established in Training-serving consistency.

Loading the exact checkpoint that training produced still does not guarantee that the server computes the same function if the host pipeline transforms raw inputs differently before those weights ever run. A model that performed well during validation degrades silently in production whenever training-serving skew causes the two paths to compute different features from the same raw input while every latency and error-rate dashboard stays green. The consistency requirement belongs to the data pipeline; serving is where a violation surfaces, and because the divergence is measurable on identical inputs without labels (principle 11), it is also one of the most preventable failures. Training-serving skew: Diagnosis and prevention catalogs the skew categories and the method that detects each. The serving-specific manifestation of skew is preprocessing divergence: the real-time inference pipeline processes raw data differently than the batch training pipeline, a common failure mode when training uses Python/Pandas while serving uses C++/Java or optimized inference servers.

Example 13.11: ResNet-50: Image preprocessing skew
Scenario: ResNet-50 vision serving models experience silent prediction accuracy degradation between offline training and online serving pipelines.

Diagnosis: The serving pipeline implemented C++/OpenCV preprocessing (cv2.INTER_LINEAR, BGR order) while training used Python/PIL (PIL.BILINEAR, RGB order, different normalization constants).

Systems lesson: Preprocessing divergence causes silent training-serving skew without raising runtime exceptions. Standardizing data transformation pipelines across training and serving environments prevents input distribution corruption.

A replica that loads quickly, runs predictably, and computes what training computed completes fixed-output serving. Every workload so far, however, has treated a request as one completed prediction. The fastest-growing category of serving workloads violates this assumption entirely. Large language models generate outputs token by token, with each token depending on every previous one. A single request may produce hundreds or thousands of tokens over seconds of elapsed time, yet must feel responsive from the first token onward. The shift from fixed-output to variable-length, streaming-output serving reopens each tool developed so far, from the latency budget to the runtime.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A computer vision team trains a ResNet-50 model in PyTorch using torchvision’s PIL-based bilinear image resizing. In production, high-throughput C++ inference servers use OpenCV’s cv::resize with default bilinear interpolation. On matched input images, the team observes different preprocessed tensors; when they rerun evaluation with the training preprocessing path, the original accuracy returns. What phenomenon is causing this degradation, and what is the proper engineering solution?

    1. Training-serving skew caused by subtle implementation differences in resize anti-aliasing and pixel coordinate rounding between PIL and OpenCV; the solution is using shared or parity-verified preprocessing across training and serving, such as a common library or exportable graph when supported.
    2. Accelerator thermal throttling caused by high frame rates; the solution is downclocking the GPU Tensor Cores.
    3. Floating-point precision drift between Python and C++; the solution is switching all production inference to 64-bit double precision.
    4. Catastrophic forgetting in the weights caused by static batching; the solution is retraining the model with dynamic dropout.

  2. Why does simply copying model weights into GPU HBM upon container startup fail to prevent latency spikes on the very first incoming user requests, and how does executing a ‘warmup pass’ resolve this issue?

  3. Sequence the stages of an illustrative serial cold-start initialization workflow when autoscaling an inference server pod from an idle state to serving live production traffic:

  1. Initialize device context or runtime state needed before host-to-device copies, then transfer model weights from host staging memory to GPU High-Bandwidth Memory (HBM) over PCIe
  2. Fetch model checkpoint artifacts and configuration from remote object storage (e.g., S3) to local NVMe SSD cache
  3. Initialize remaining execution-engine state, allocate memory pools, and run synthetic dummy warmup passes
  4. Container initialization, environment bootstrap, and runtime dependency loading
  5. Register pod as healthy with load balancer gateway to begin receiving live traffic
  6. Memory-map (mmap) or deserialize model weights from local SSD into host memory, with explicit pinned staging buffers if the transfer path uses them

  1. True or False: In multi-model serving on a shared GPU, Volta-era NVIDIA Multi-Process Service (MPS) provides hard physical hardware partitioning of high-bandwidth memory (HBM) channels and compute cores, completely preventing memory out-of-memory (OOM) faults caused by co-located tenant models.

  2. A multi-model serving platform hosts 50 distinct fine-tuned vision models on a single GPU node with 24 GB of VRAM. Each model requires 2 GB of memory, exceeding total VRAM capacity. Traffic to individual models is sporadic. What loading and memory management architecture enables serving all 50 models while minimizing request latency?

    1. Redeploy the cluster on 50 dedicated GPU nodes running continuous batching 24/7.
    2. Quantize all models to 1-bit weights to fit all 50 models into GPU L2 cache simultaneously.
    3. Maintain a tiered memory cache (storing active models in GPU VRAM and inactive models in host pinned DRAM), using asynchronous PCIe DMA transfers to swap weights into VRAM on demand with an LRU eviction policy.
    4. Compress all models into a single shared zip archive on remote S3 and download the entire archive over HTTP on each incoming request.

  3. Why can loading model weights from modern safetensors format provide faster startup times than some legacy PyTorch checkpoint paths?

    1. safetensors files compress weights using gzip, reducing disk storage by \(10\times\).
    2. safetensors files store raw tensor buffers in a simple deterministic layout that supports memory mapping (mmap) without Python object deserialization or arbitrary code execution.
    3. safetensors files compile PyTorch code directly into binary x86 machine instructions on disk.
    4. safetensors files automatically quantize FP32 weights into 4-bit integers during read operations.

See Answers →

13.9 LLM Serving

An image classifier returns a single class vector in a few milliseconds and immediately frees its activation buffers, whereas a conversational LLM streams hundreds of tokens over several seconds while holding a growing key-value cache in GPU memory. LLMs make three properties far more prominent than in fixed-output serving. Autoregressive generation21 means that each token depends on all previous tokens, which makes output inherently sequential. Variable-length output means that response length is unknown at request time, which complicates fixed-batch assumptions. Stateful memory means that the key-value cache grows with each generated token, which creates dynamic memory pressure. Each property strains a different tool from the preceding sections: sequential generation splits the latency budget into phases, unknown output length breaks fixed-batch scheduling, and the growing cache makes memory capacity, not arithmetic, the limit on concurrency. The p50, p95, and p99 metrics used for classification serving still matter, but they apply separately to the initial prompt processing and to the subsequent token generation. The same principles of queuing theory, batching trade-offs, and latency budgets remain applicable; LLM serving adds domain-specific techniques atop them.

21 Autoregressive: From Greek auto- (self) and Latin regressus (a going back), since the output “regresses” on itself; George Udny Yule introduced autoregressive models in 1927 for analyzing sunspot cycles. In language modeling, each output token conditions on all previously generated tokens, creating a serial dependency that prevents the parallelism exploited during training. Decode is often weight-bandwidth-bound at small batches; larger batches amortize the weight stream and can shift the bottleneck toward compute or KV-cache traffic (Pope et al. 2023).

Pope, Reiner, Sholto Douglas, Aakanksha Chowdhery, Jacob Devlin, James Bradbury, Jonathan Heek, Kefan Xiao, Shivani Agrawal, and Jeff Dean. 2023. “Efficiently Scaling Transformer Inference.” Proceedings of Machine Learning and Systems (MLSys) 5: 606–24.

↩︎ Prefill, small-batch autoregressive decode, and the KV cache that every decode step reads are established in Autoregressive decoding.

The chapter’s second service carries the section. GPT-2 XL, the bandwidth-bound lighthouse, anchors the metrics, the decode floor, speculative decoding, and the energy per generated token, because its weight stream sets each of them. Llama 3 8B, introduced with the running cases, carries the cache budget, paging, and prefix reuse, because its longer contexts make memory capacity rather than bandwidth the constraint that binds first.

13.9.1 Performance metrics: TTFT and TPOT

Prefill and low-batch decode often face different resource limits.

A single end-to-end latency number cannot distinguish a chat response that begins streaming in 100 ms and flows at reading speed from one that leaves the user staring at a blank screen for two and a half seconds before dumping the entire paragraph at once. Because generative models emit a stream of tokens rather than a single output tensor, serving systems split latency into time to first token (TTFT) and time per output token (TPOT). TPOT is the average of a request’s inter-token latencies (ITL), as defined in Token latency: TTFT and TPOT. In serving, each metric belongs to one phase. Prefill (processing the prompt and populating its KV cache) sets TTFT and is often compute-bound, while decode (generating output) sets TPOT and is often bandwidth-bound at small batches. A low TTFT therefore does not mean a fast model. A model can have a fast TTFT but a sluggish TPOT (if the memory wall \((\Bandwidth)\) is the bottleneck), leading to a frustrating user experience where the answer starts quickly but “stutters” thereafter. Because these metrics capture distinct aspects of the user experience, interactive services set separate targets for TTFT, TPOT or ITL, and aggregate throughput.

Systems Perspective 13.5: LLM serving latency targets
Interactive LLM services usually need separate SLOs for responsiveness, generation fluidity, and fleet throughput. The following values are illustrative targets rather than universal requirements:

  • TTFT: \(<\) 500 ms (for a 1000-token prompt)
  • TPOT: \(<\) 50 ms (equivalent to ~20 tokens/s, faster than human reading speed)
  • Throughput: \(>\) 1000 tokens/s aggregate across active serving replicas

A single “latency” number therefore hides the prefill/decode split. TTFT is the blank-screen budget, TPOT is the reading-flow budget, and aggregate service throughput, measured as tokens/s summed across active serving replicas, determines whether those targets hold under shared load.

13.9.2 The decode floor

The chapter’s bandwidth-bound lighthouse puts numbers on both phases. A GPT-2 XL chat request with a 512-token prompt performs about 1.5 TFLOP of prefill arithmetic, which an H100 sustaining 40 percent of its FP16 peak completes in about 3.9 ms, the model’s share of TTFT. Each decode step then reads all 3 GB of FP16 weights to produce one token per active sequence. At batch one that read takes 0.9 ms, so the model-side TPOT can be no shorter, however many arithmetic units the accelerator adds. The floor assumes the step is launched as one captured graph. Issued operation by operation from an eager framework, the same step spends about 8.6 ms on the host submitting 576 kernels, roughly 10× the weight read, which is why every decode floor in this chapter assumes the CUDA Graph capture that Graph capture and JIT compilation developed.

Batch-one decode with FP16 weights runs at the weight-only arithmetic intensity of about 1 FLOP/byte that Autoregressive decoding derived. The H100’s ridge point sits near 295 FLOP/byte, so the step is memory-bandwidth bound by more than two orders of magnitude (Pope et al. 2023). Equation 14 gives its floor per token, where \(\DataVolume\) is the bytes the step reads and \(\AcceleratorMemoryBandwidth\) is the accelerator’s memory bandwidth: \[ \DecodeTokenTimeFloor\approx \frac{\DataVolume}{\AcceleratorMemoryBandwidth} \tag{14}\] While decode remains bandwidth bound, every step pays to move every weight, in energy as well as in time, and adding compute cores yields little latency improvement. Faster memory (Machine), fewer bytes per parameter or a smaller model (Algorithm), or better batching and cache management change the bound.

Batching changes which bytes dominate. Each sequence also reads its own KV cache on every step. GPT-2 XL’s attention heads each keep their own keys and values, with no grouped-query sharing, so its cache holds 307.2 KB per token of context, or 314.6 MB for a full 1,024-token context. Batching shares the weight read across sequences but not the cache reads. At batch one with a full context, a step moves 3.3 GB and takes 1.0 ms. At batch 32 it moves 13.1 GB, 77 percent of it cache, and takes 3.9 ms. Aggregate throughput rises from about 1,010 tokens/s to 8,200 tokens/s, while each sequence’s TPOT stretches by 3.9×. The same trade between per-sequence cadence and aggregate throughput governs every scheduling technique that follows.

13.9.3 Decoding strategies

Switching a chat endpoint from greedy decoding to four-way beam search quadruples both the arithmetic per step and the KV-cache memory each active user holds. The sampling algorithm executed at each step therefore shapes serving cost directly. Greedy decoding takes the highest-scoring token and needs one forward pass per token, though it can fall into repetitive output because it never revisits an early choice. Beam search keeps several candidate sequences and multiplies both computational work and KV-cache footprint by the beam width. Stochastic sampling scales the logits by a temperature and truncates the vocabulary with top-\(\TopTokenSampleCount\) or top-\(\NucleusProbabilityMass\) nucleus sampling before drawing a token (Holtzman et al. 2020). For the serving system, beam width multiplies the state each request holds, while the sampling variants add only a small per-step reduction whose cost depends on its kernel implementation and on the sequence-length distribution that continuous batching must absorb.

Holtzman, Ari, Jan Buys, Li Du, Maxwell Forbes, and Yejin Choi. 2020. “The Curious Case of Neural Text Degeneration.” Proceedings of the 8th International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR 2020).

Interactive LLM services commonly return tokens as they are produced rather than waiting for complete generation. This streaming response transforms the user experience. A two-second total generation feels responsive when tokens stream continuously, but feels broken when users stare at a blank screen for two seconds. Streaming requires infrastructure support for incremental responses and client-side rendering. Once generation streams token by token, the serving unit changes from one fixed-output prediction to a stateful sequence whose length is unknown when it is admitted and whose memory footprint grows on every step. The unknown length breaks the fixed-batch scheduling developed for fixed-output models, and the growing state turns accelerator memory into the next constraint.

13.9.4 Continuous batching

An autoregressive request finishes only when it emits an end-of-sequence token, so its length is revealed one step at a time. Extending the request-level dynamic batching of section 13.5 to an entire generation cycle keeps batch membership fixed until every sequence finishes. When sequence lengths vary, this static commitment degrades accelerator efficiency. If one sequence in a batch of eight finishes after ten tokens while others require 100 tokens, 90 percent of the compute and memory bandwidth allocated to that sequence slot is wasted on padding tokens or masked dummy operations while awaiting the slowest sequence (Yu et al. 2022).

Yu, Gyeong-In, Joo Seong Jeong, Geon-Woo Kim, Soojeong Kim, and Byung-Gon Chun. 2022. “Orca: A Distributed Serving System for Transformer-Based Generative Models.” 16th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation (OSDI 22), 521–38.

22 Continuous batching: NVIDIA TensorRT-LLM calls the same technique “in-flight batching” (NVIDIA 2026f). Request-level batching commits to a fixed batch for an entire generation sequence (potentially hundreds of iterations), while continuous batching reschedules at every token-generation step, a contrast that parallels preemptive OS process scheduling versus run-to-completion.

NVIDIA. 2026f. NVIDIA TensorRT-LLM.

Continuous batching22 (also called iteration-level batching) eliminates this idle-slot waste by rescheduling batch composition at each decoding iteration (Yu et al. 2022; Kwon et al. 2023). Rather than committing to a static batch that persists across hundreds of forward passes, the serving engine re-evaluates batch membership at every token-generation step. When a sequence emits its end-of-sequence token, the scheduler immediately reclaims that execution slot, admitting an arriving request into the batch for the subsequent forward pass. Active sequences continue decoding without interruption, maintaining high accelerator utilization across variable-length workloads.

Serving runtimes such as vLLM23 and TensorRT-LLM improve throughput via two mechanisms: reclaiming wasted compute from completed sequences and shortening queue wait times for new arrivals (Kwon et al. 2023; NVIDIA 2026f). However, admitting new requests dynamically creates a systems collision between prompt prefill and token decoding. Long-prompt prefill often exposes high arithmetic intensity and can occupy the GPU for tens of milliseconds. Low-batch decoding often has less weight reuse and is memory-bandwidth bound, advancing one token per active sequence at a time. When an engine admits an entire long prompt before advancing active decodes, those streams wait for that prefill, stretching their inter-token gaps. Sarathi-Serve resolves this interference through chunked prefill and stall-free batching (Agrawal et al. 2024). By splitting long prompts into fixed-size chunks (e.g., 512 tokens) and co-scheduling a single chunk alongside active decode requests per step, the scheduler limits the prefill work admitted per iteration, reducing interference with active decodes. Measured iteration times determine the resulting token cadence.

23 vLLM (virtual large language model (LLM)): An open-source serving system that enables continuous batching via its PagedAttention algorithm (Kwon et al. 2023). Keeping KV-cache waste low lets it serve larger effective batches on the same hardware.

Agrawal, Amey, Nitin Kedia, Ashish Panwar, Jayashree Mohan, Nipun Kwatra, Bhargav S. Gulavani, Alexey Tumanov, and Ramachandran Ramjee. 2024. “Taming Throughput-Latency Tradeoff in LLM Inference with Sarathi-Serve.” 18th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation (OSDI 24), 117–34.

The scheduling boundary determines when an existing stream can emit its next token. Figure 8 compares admission of one complete prompt with admission of the same prompt in chunks, while keeping the active stream visible in both schedules.

Chunking creates more opportunities to advance the active streams, but each chunk still consumes accelerator time. The scheduler must choose a chunk budget from measured iteration latency; an iteration boundary alone does not guarantee a token deadline.

Speculative decoding

Continuous batching and chunked prefill keep decode batches full and their cadence steady, but at small batch each generated token still pays for one full weight stream. Speculative decoding24 attacks that bound directly by exploiting the arithmetic units that sit idle during bandwidth-bound batch-one decode. A small, fast draft model runs \(\DraftTokenCount\) sequential decode steps to propose a block of \(\DraftTokenCount\) candidate tokens, and the large target model then evaluates all \(\DraftTokenCount\) candidates at once in a single forward pass, much like a short prefill step. Because the target model computes next-token probability distributions at the last confirmed position and after each of the \(\DraftTokenCount\) draft tokens simultaneously, it can check each draft choice in order. At the first position where a draft token is rejected, the target model substitutes a token sampled from its own distribution at that position and discards the remaining draft tokens. If all \(\DraftTokenCount\) draft tokens are accepted, the target model’s distribution at the final position yields one additional \((\DraftTokenCount+1)\)-th token at no extra cost. A single target weight stream therefore always produces at least one valid token and up to \(\DraftTokenCount+1\) tokens, while preserving the exact output distribution of the target model alone. Evaluated T5-XXL workloads achieved roughly 2–3\(\times\) speedups (Leviathan et al. 2023), though the gain depends on how often the target model accepts the draft.

24 Speculative decoding: Parallel verification is possible because the draft has already committed to all \(\DraftTokenCount\) candidate tokens, so the target can score the whole block in one causal-attention forward pass, as it scores a prompt during prefill, rather than in \(\DraftTokenCount\) sequential steps (Leviathan et al. 2023). The technique removes serial steps at the runtime layer without changing the model architecture.

Leviathan, Yaniv, Matan Kalman, and Yossi Matias. 2023. “Fast Inference from Transformers via Speculative Decoding.” International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), 19274–86.

If the draft proposes \(\DraftTokenCount\) tokens and the target accepts each with probability \(\DraftAcceptanceRate\), independently of the others (Leviathan et al. 2023), the number of tokens emitted in one verification round is the accepted prefix of length \(0\) to \(\DraftTokenCount\) plus the one guaranteed token from the target model’s own distribution. Summing that geometric series gives the expected tokens per target pass in equation 15: \[\mathbb{E}[\text{tokens per target pass}] = \frac{1-\DraftAcceptanceRate^{\DraftTokenCount+1}}{1-\DraftAcceptanceRate} \tag{15}\]

Verifying \(\DraftTokenCount+1\) positions in parallel multiplies the target pass’s arithmetic by \(\DraftTokenCount+1\), but at batch one its arithmetic intensity starts two orders of magnitude below the H100’s ridge point, so the verification pass remains memory-bandwidth bound and still costs about one weight stream. For the GPT-2 XL service, a GPT-2 Small draft with 248 MB of FP16 weights proposes 4 tokens per round, spending 0.30 ms on draft reads against 0.90 ms for the target’s verification pass. At an assumed acceptance rate of 70 percent, a round yields 2.8 tokens on average, so the time per generated token falls from 0.90 ms to 0.43 ms, a 2.1× speedup. The gain shrinks as acceptance falls or as the batch grows. A full batch already shares each weight stream across many sequences, and verifying several positions per sequence then consumes arithmetic that a batch-one step left idle but a full batch does not.

PagedAttention

Keeping decode batches full, however, shifts the primary serving bottleneck to memory management. Because transformer generation appends attention states at every step, the KV cache for each active request grows dynamically in accelerator HBM. When sequences of varying lengths share memory, contiguous allocation produces severe external fragmentation. If a completed 100-token sequence releases its cache, an arriving 150-token request cannot occupy that freed memory because it requires a larger contiguous address range. Over time, physical memory fractures into small, noncontiguous gaps, preventing new sequences from launching even when total unallocated capacity appears sufficient. Together with the over-reservation of contiguous allocators, this memory fragmentation can waste 60 to 80 percent of KV-cache memory in naive implementations (Kwon et al. 2023), severely limiting the concurrent batch size that determines serving throughput.

PagedAttention recovers the KV-cache waste of contiguous allocation.
