25 PagedAttention: The name directly references OS virtual memory paging, first implemented on the Atlas computer at Manchester (1962) to solve the same class of allocation problem, in which programs needed more memory than physically available, and contiguous allocation wasted space (Kilburn et al. 1962). Introduced at SOSP 2023, PagedAttention applies this six-decade-old abstraction to GPU KV-cache memory; in its evaluation, the recovered memory enabled 2–4\(\times\) higher throughput on the same hardware (Kwon et al. 2023).

Kilburn, Tom, David B. G. Edwards, Michael J. Lanigan, and Frank H. Sumner. 1962. “One-Level Storage System.” IRE Transactions on Electronic Computers EC-11 (2): 223–35. https://doi.org/10.1109/TEC.1962.5219356.
Kwon, Woosuk, Zhuohan Li, Siyuan Zhuang, Ying Sheng, Lianmin Zheng, Cody Hao Yu, Joseph Gonzalez, Hao Zhang, and Ion Stoica. 2023. “Efficient Memory Management for Large Language Model Serving with PagedAttention.” Proceedings of the 29th Symposium on Operating Systems Principles, 611–26. https://doi.org/10.1145/3600006.3613165.

PagedAttention,25 introduced in vLLM, resolves this fragmentation by applying operating system virtual memory paging to accelerator memory (Kwon et al. 2023). In static contiguous allocation, reserving maximum sequence length memory up front causes internal waste, while freed regions may be too small or poorly placed to satisfy a new contiguous reservation. Instead of allocating one contiguous region per sequence, PagedAttention divides the KV cache into fixed-size blocks mapped through a block table. A sequence’s cache can therefore occupy noncontiguous physical blocks. When a sequence completes, its blocks return to a free pool for reuse. Rather than copying noncontiguous blocks into a temporary contiguous buffer before computing attention, a specialized PagedAttention CUDA kernel traverses the block table directly, staging noncontiguous blocks into on-chip SRAM during query-key multiplication. By eliminating external fragmentation and bounding internal waste to each sequence’s last, partially filled block, PagedAttention reduced KV-cache waste below 4 percent in the reported evaluation and enabled substantially higher batch concurrency on the same accelerator hardware (Kwon et al. 2023). Paging decides where cache blocks live, however. It does not reduce the KV bytes stored per token; a sequence needs another block only when its current allocation fills.

A block table separates a sequence’s token order from its physical placement. Figure 9 uses an eight-block pool: requests C and D occupy the middle pairs, leaving four free blocks in two separated runs. A request E that needs four blocks can use those scattered blocks without moving C or D.

The free capacity becomes usable without compacting the pool into one contiguous region. The table lookup adds indirection, and the last block can still contain unused token slots; allocation efficiency does not eliminate either cost.

13.9.5 Memory and KV cache

Even after PagedAttention eliminates fragmentation waste, thirty-two long-context conversations can exhaust an 80 GB GPU’s memory when the quantized model weights themselves occupy less than half the device. The KV cache, the per-sequence store of attention keys and values defined in Autoregressive decoding, grows linearly with sequence length. Prefill creates cache entries for every prompt token, and each decoded token appends another entry, so a request’s footprint is set by its prompt length plus the tokens it has generated so far. Summed across concurrent variable-length sequences, this dynamic state can exceed the model weights and dominate available accelerator memory, which is why the remaining memory levers either reuse cache state across requests or move it off the accelerator.

KV budget

Once contexts grow, the cache rather than the weights sets how many conversations one accelerator can hold, and Llama 3 8B makes the budget concrete. Each token stores a key vector and a value vector in every layer for every key-value head. Storing 16-bit Key and Value tensors across \(\LayerCount=32\) layers with \(\KvHeadCount=8\) grouped-query attention heads of dimension \(\AttentionHeadDimension=128\) requires \(2 \times 32 \times 8 \times 128 \times 2\text{ bytes} = 131{,}072\text{ bytes} \approx 131\text{ KB}\) per token per sequence. Grouped-query attention (Dubey et al. 2024) lets the model’s 32 query heads share those key-value heads, which cuts the cache by 4× relative to full multi-head attention. An 8,192-token conversation therefore holds 1.07 GB. With 16.1 GB of FP16 weights resident on an H100 whose 80 GB datasheet capacity is 85.9 GB in the decimal units used for tensor footprints, about 69.8 GB remains for caches before runtime workspace, room for about 65 such conversations at once. The weights are a fixed cost; every additional conversation, and every additional thousand tokens of context, draws on the same remainder.

Larger models make the cache the binding constraint sooner. For a 70-billion-parameter-class model with grouped-query attention, 4-bit weights consume 35.3 GB of the same H100, leaving 50.6 GB for FP16 cache before runtime workspace. A batch of 32 conversations exhausts that budget at roughly 4,800 tokens of context, so serving engines trade concurrent batch size against context length to avoid out-of-memory failures (figure 10).

A model whose weights and cache do not fit one accelerator, even at reduced precision, can still be served from one machine by splitting it across the node’s accelerators with the model parallelism of Model parallelism. Serving usually favors tensor parallelism. Pipeline stages also fit, but every decoded token must pass through each stage in turn, which adds latency to every step. Tensor parallelism instead puts every accelerator to work on every layer, and each accelerator holds a slice of the weights and of the KV cache. A 70-billion-parameter-class model at 16-bit precision, whose weights alone exceed one H100’s memory, therefore fits across the accelerators of one node with room left for cache. The cost is a collective operation after each layer’s matrix multiplications. On NVLink that exchange is small next to the layer’s compute; over PCIe or a network it would dominate each decode step. A serving engine therefore keeps one model’s tensor-parallel group inside a single node and meets higher volume by replicating whole groups across machines rather than stretching one group across them.

Prefix caching and memory offloading

Many requests carry identical prefixes, and recomputing their cache entries wastes both prefill compute and memory. Prefix caching stores the KV states of common instruction prefixes (such as a 2,000-token system prompt or a shared retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) context), allowing independent requests to reuse the prefill result for an identical prefix. For \(\SharedPrefixRequestCount\) requests sharing a prefix of \(\SharedPrefixLength\) tokens, the avoided prefill work is roughly \((\SharedPrefixRequestCount-1)\SharedPrefixLength\) token steps, subject to cache hits, eviction, and implementation overhead. For multi-turn conversations, the same principle lets a subsequent turn process only the new suffix after reusing compatible prior state.

When the aggregate KV cache exceeds GPU VRAM, systems can use KV cache offloading. This strategy spills inactive or low-priority context windows to host CPU RAM or NVMe SSD, freeing VRAM for active generation. The reload cost is bounded below by bytes moved divided by PCIe or NVMe bandwidth, before software overhead and queueing are added. Offloading therefore prevents OOM failures and enables larger context windows, but it also creates affinity, invalidation, and hot-decode latency risks that must be budgeted explicitly.

13.9.6 Session affinity constraints

Prefix reuse and offloading both assume that the next turn of a conversation reaches the replica that holds its cache. Session affinity, routing successive requests from a client to the same physical replica, arises whenever serving state resides in local accelerator memory rather than being reconstructed on every invocation. In conversational models, this resident state is dominated by the KV cache. When turn \(\ConversationTurnIndex\) executes on a replica, that replica’s HBM retains the projected keys and values across all prior prompt and generation tokens. Routing turn \(\ConversationTurnIndex+1\) to a different replica discards that local memory footprint, forcing the new node to recompute the entire prefix sequence from scratch. This redundant prefill consumes memory bandwidth and tensor-core cycles that would otherwise advance active decode iterations, directly inflating time-to-first-token.

A parallel physical constraint governs personalized serving using fine-tuned Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) modules: small per-user adapters, each a pair of low-rank matrices whose product is added to a frozen base weight matrix. While base-model weights remain pinned in device HBM, individual user adapters must be dynamically staged into memory. If incoming requests for a user scatter across multiple replicas, each node must repeatedly transfer adapter tensors over the host-to-device PCIe bus or fetch them across the network, displacing other active working sets in memory. Stateful preprocessing pipelines, such as tokenizer vocabularies and per-session normalization state, impose the same penalty, requiring redundant CPU-side allocations and cache rebuilds whenever execution moves across node boundaries.

↩︎ Products of two low-rank matrices, and the storage and memory traffic they save relative to a full matrix, are established in Matrix factorization.

Strict affinity creates a direct conflict with dynamic batching. Continuous batch schedulers maximize accelerator throughput by packing concurrent requests into unified matrix multiplications, raising operational arithmetic intensity in proportion to batch size (\(\ArithmeticIntensity\propto \BatchSize\)). When requests are pinned to designated replicas, the scheduler’s pool of batchable candidates fragments across the cluster. A replica assigned to an idle or low-concurrency user session must either process underfilled batches, collapsing arithmetic intensity back into the memory-bandwidth-bound regime, or stall waiting for affinity-matched tokens. To prevent this collapse in utilization, production routers use soft affinity. Under soft affinity, the ingress gateway prioritizes the cache-holding replica when capacity allows, but sheds requests to alternate nodes when local queue delays exceed the physical latency required to transfer adapter weights or recompute prefix tokens.

Systems Perspective 13.6: LLM serving: Beyond the fundamentals
Each LLM serving technique answers one of two physical limits. The first is the decode bound, in which every generated token pays for a full weight stream at small batch. Continuous batching keeps decode batches full so each stream serves many sequences, chunked prefill keeps prompt processing from stalling that stream, and speculative decoding extracts more than one accepted token per stream. The second is the KV cache, which grows with sequence length and batch size and often exceeds the weights themselves; PagedAttention, prefix caching, offloading, and session affinity decide where it lives and how much of it can be reused. Weight-only quantization (such as INT4 weights with FP16 activations), the precision lever of section 13.6.3 applied to the weight stream, addresses both at once, because it shrinks the bytes streamed per token and frees capacity for cache (Lin et al. 2024).

These LLM-specific optimizations build directly on core serving mechanics: queueing models govern scheduling timeouts, batching dynamics determine throughput-latency curves, and reduced precision bounds memory traffic. Advanced treatments extend these mechanics to multi-tenant prefix sharing across fleets and multi-node tensor-parallel execution.

Every technique above changes how many sequences share each weight stream and how long each one holds its cache. Because an accelerator draws power whether a decode pass serves one sequence or a full batch, that same sharing directly governs the energy each generated token costs. Dividing node power by aggregate token throughput converts batching and prefix-caching gains into joules per token.

Napkin Math 13.5: The energy cost of a chat
Problem: How much energy does an H100-backed chat service spend per generated token and for a response with 500 tokens?

As LLMs scale, joules per token becomes a first-class operational metric alongside latency. Assuming the H100 draws its 700 W thermal design power (TDP), this scenario estimate follows from throughput and power (Choquette 2023):

  1. Throughput: 114 concurrent requests \(\times\) 8 tokens/s per request ≈ 912 tokens/s.
  2. Power: 700 W (GPU) + 300 W (Host/Overhead) = 1000 W.
  3. Energy per token: 1000 W / 912 tokens/s ≈ 1.0965 J/token

Systems insight: Under these assumptions, a response of 500 tokens consumes ≈ 548.2 J. A measured production figure is of the same order. Google reports ≈ 864 J (0.24 Wh) for the median Gemini text prompt, counting host, idle-reserve, and facility energy, although the study does not state how long that prompt was (Elsworth et al. 2025).

  • For comparison, charging a smartphone consumes ≈ 40000 J.
  • Boiling a cup of water consumes ≈ 100000 J.

The primary way to reduce J/token is to increase hardware utilization and eliminate redundant compute. If the GPU sits at 10 percent utilization due to poor batching, it still draws ~300 W and the host adds 300 W, causing energy per token to rise to 6.6 J/token (approximately 6× the baseline). Architectural optimizations like prefix caching also skip the energy-intensive prefill phase for shared context, directly reducing the energy footprint of retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) and chat applications. The serving lesson is that efficiency is not only a latency or cost metric; it also determines how much energy each useful token consumes.

Elsworth, Cooper, Keguo Huang, David Patterson, Ian Schneider, Robert Sedivy, Savannah Goodman, Ben Townsend, et al. 2025. “Measuring the Environmental Impact of Delivering AI at Google Scale.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2508.15734.

Systems Perspective 13.7: Looking ahead: Spending more compute per query
Every lever so far takes the number of tokens a request generates as given, and fixed-output serving minimizes latency (\(\EndToEndRequestLatency \to 0\)) for a fixed amount of work per request. Reasoning models and other inference-time-compute systems make that token count a choice: they deliberately spend more computation per query to improve answer quality, generating intermediate reasoning or search tokens before the visible answer. The optimization target then expands to include answer quality, and every extra reasoning token pays the decode costs this section derived. It adds one more step of decode work at the batch’s cadence, holds one more KV-cache entry until the response ends, and takes its share of the joules per token. Answer quality now trades directly against TPOT, cache capacity, and energy per query.

Checkpoint 13.5: LLM serving fundamentals

LLM serving introduces constraints absent from traditional model serving.

Every LLM serving optimization ultimately decides how many sequences share each weight stream and how much accelerator memory each sequence holds. The chat-energy calculation showed that the same sharing sets the joules spent per useful token, because each decode step streams the weights once for the whole active batch, so a well-filled batch divides that energy across sequences. Unlike fixed-output inference, LLM energy also grows with response length. Carbon accounting additionally requires an emissions factor and a defined boundary. These per-token bounds describe one accelerator. Deciding how many accelerators a service needs, and what each generated token costs, requires carrying them, together with the fixed-output results of the earlier sections, into capacity planning and unit economics.

Self-Check: Question

  1. In Large Language Model (LLM) serving, how do the computational and hardware characteristics of the Prefill phase fundamentally differ from the Decode phase?

    1. Prefill processes tokens autoregressively one by one and is memory-bandwidth bound, whereas Decode processes all output tokens simultaneously and is compute-bound.
    2. Prefill processes all prompt tokens concurrently in parallel matrix multiplications and is often more compute-intensive, whereas Decode generates one token per step autoregressively and at low batch sizes is often memory-bandwidth bound due to repeated weight and KV-state movement.
    3. Prefill operates exclusively on host CPU DRAM, whereas Decode runs on GPU Tensor Cores.
    4. Prefill latency scales quadratically with batch size, whereas Decode latency is strictly independent of sequence length and KV-cache size.

  2. Order the step-by-step execution cycle of Speculative Decoding for accelerating LLM inference:

  1. Target model runs a single parallel forward pass over the draft candidate tokens to compute verification logits
  2. Draft model (a lightweight autoregressive model) generates \(K\) candidate tokens sequentially
  3. Engine appends the accepted tokens to the KV cache and advances the generation context
  4. Verification logic compares the draft tokens with the target model’s verification results and accepts a prefix of them

  1. True or False: Speculative decoding speeds up serving regardless of draft-model quality, because the target model always verifies multiple tokens in parallel.

  2. In a multi-turn conversational AI system with extensive system prompts (e.g., 2,000 tokens of instructions and few-shot examples shared across thousands of user sessions), what serving optimization eliminates redundant prefill compute across requests?

    1. Linear Attention Approximation, which drops system prompt tokens entirely from the attention window.
    2. Static Graph Compilation, which hardcodes user inputs directly into the weight tensors.
    3. Prefix Caching (e.g., RadixAttention), which stores the KV-cache states of common prompt prefixes in memory and reuses them across queries matching the prefix.
    4. Speculative Quantization, which quantizes prompt tokens into 1-bit binary representations during the decode phase.

  3. Contrast traditional request-level dynamic batching with iteration-level continuous batching (as introduced in Orca and vLLM) for Large Language Model (LLM) serving. Why is request-level batching severely inefficient for autoregressive generation?

  4. True or False: PagedAttention resolves the primary memory waste in LLM KV-caching by allocating key and value states in non-contiguous, fixed-size physical memory blocks managed via a virtual page table, eliminating both internal fragmentation from over-allocation and external memory fragmentation.

See Answers →

13.10 Economics and Planning

A team that has raised an H100’s ResNet-50 throughput several-fold through batching still faces a fleet-level question, namely whether serving a 5,000 QPS target within the same p99 latency SLO costs less on a few H100s or on several times as many lower-cost T4s. Batching, precision tuning, kernel fusion, graph compilation, and the LLM levers that share each weight stream across sequences all improve single-node performance, but production deployment requires choosing how many machines to rent, of what type, and at what total bill. Serving infrastructure cost grows continuously with request volume (Zhang et al. 2019), while training cost follows a one-time dataset, model, and optimization budget. The bitter lesson sharpens this asymmetry. The methods that win are the ones that scale, so successive models grow larger, and while training pays for that scale once, serving pays for it on every request.

Zhang, Chengliang, Minchen Yu, Wei Wang, and Feng Yan. 2019. “MArk: Exploiting Cloud Services for Cost-Effective, SLO-Aware Machine Learning Inference Serving.” 2019 USENIX Annual Technical Conference (USENIX ATC 19), 1049–62.

↩︎ The bitter lesson, that general methods which scale with computation keep outperforming hand-engineered ones, is established in The Bitter Lesson.

26 Jevons paradox: William Stanley Jevons observed in 1865 that efficiency improvements in coal-powered steam engines increased total coal consumption by making steam power economically viable for applications previously too costly (Jevons 1865); the same dynamic can apply to AI inference, where each 10\(\times\) cost reduction opens application classes that were economically infeasible at the previous price point, expanding aggregate demand by more than the efficiency gain.

Jevons, William Stanley. 1865. The Coal Question: An Inquiry Concerning the Progress of the Nation, and the Probable Exhaustion of Our Coal Mines. Macmillan; Co.

Tail latency dictates the technical design of a serving system, but economics dictates its operational scale. As specialized accelerators and runtime optimizations reduce the compute cycles required per forward pass, the marginal cost per inference declines.26 Counterintuitively, reduced unit cost does not shrink aggregate serving expenditures. In accordance with Jevons paradox, lower marginal costs stimulate disproportionately higher query volumes, integrating model evaluation deeper into user-facing product loops. At hyperscale, recurring operational inference costs quickly eclipse one-time offline training investments, shifting the primary systems bottleneck toward sustained per-query efficiency.

Example 13.12: Inference cost at Facebook
Scenario: Production ML serving systems at Meta execute tens of trillions of inferences daily across global user-facing services (Hazelwood et al. 2018).

Diagnosis: Recurring inference serving compute and energy costs dwarf initial offline model training expenses. Live request arrival patterns and strict latency bounds constrain GPU batching.

Systems lesson: Model architectures cheap to train can prove unviable to serve at fleet scale. Production serving infrastructure requires hardware-software co-design to balance tail latency, batch size, and recurring operational total cost of ownership.

Hazelwood, Kim, Sarah Bird, David Brooks, Soumith Chintala, Utku Diril, Dmytro Dzhulgakov, Mohamed Fawzy, et al. 2018. “Applied Machine Learning at Facebook: A Datacenter Infrastructure Perspective.” 2018 IEEE International Symposium on High Performance Computer Architecture (HPCA), 620–29. https://doi.org/10.1109/hpca.2018.00059.
OpenAI. 2023b. Introducing ChatGPT and Whisper APIs.
OpenAI. 2023a. GPT-4.
OpenAI. 2024. GPT-4o Mini: Advancing Cost-Efficient Intelligence.
OpenAI Developer Community. 2024. Announcing GPT-4o in the API.
Anthropic. 2024. Introducing the Next Generation of Claude.
Google Developers Blog. 2024. Gemini 1.5 Flash Price Drop with Tuning Rollout Complete, and More.
DeepSeek. 2024. Introducing DeepSeek-V3.

The same serving-economics pressure appears in public API prices. Figure 11 tracks this cost collapse across representative public API list-price snapshots from 2023 through 2025 on a logarithmic scale. The steep downward trend line isolates how prompt token pricing fell by multiple orders of magnitude, from thirty dollars per million tokens for original GPT-4 down to several cents for Gemini 1.5 Flash and DeepSeek-V3. Vendor prices change frequently, so these points should be read as historical provenance for the trend rather than as current purchasing guidance (OpenAI 2023b, 2023a, 2024; OpenAI Developer Community 2024; Anthropic 2024; Google Developers Blog 2024; DeepSeek 2024). Each order-of-magnitude drop changes which applications are economically viable, and a provider can sustain such prices only if its serving stack extracts more completed requests from each accelerator-hour. Converting engineering efficiency into unit cost draws on the latency, batching, and memory mechanisms of the preceding sections.

13.10.1 Cost per inference across hardware

Choosing serving instances by hourly rental price alone, or by peak throughput alone, misleads whenever traffic fluctuates. An H100 that costs several times as much per hour as a T4 delivers the lowest cost per million images when traffic keeps it full, but becomes the more expensive option when overnight traffic drops and the GPU sits mostly idle. The cost per inference divides the allocated serving cost by the number of completed inferences that satisfy the service objective. The numerator can include provisioned CPU or accelerator time, memory capacity, data transfer, storage, and orchestration. At high utilization, useful throughput spreads that provisioned cost across many requests. At low utilization, paid capacity sits idle, so the same hourly cost is divided among fewer successful inferences.

Hardware selection for model serving trades hourly rental price against parallel throughput. Although GPU instances cost several times as much per hour as commodity CPU instances, their matrix units can process enough requests per second to cut the cost per completed query by an order of magnitude. Whether CPUs or dedicated accelerators win economically depends on arithmetic intensity, batchability, and the latency deadline.

Host CPU execution is cheaper for small models, services with relaxed latency deadlines (hundreds of milliseconds), and sparse or unpredictable traffic that leaves dedicated accelerators idle. Conversely, GPU acceleration wins when models perform dense matrix operations, when service-level objectives require response times of tens of milliseconds or less, and when sustained traffic lets dynamic batching amortize kernel launch and memory transfer costs.

Beyond steady-state costs, startup time affects scaling economics. Instance launch, image pull, driver initialization, model loading, compilation, and warmup can make accelerator replicas slower to become ready than CPU replicas, although exact times depend on the platform and deployment artifact. If traffic rises faster than useful capacity can come online, latency SLOs can fail despite sufficient eventual capacity.

When accelerator replicas have a long readiness delay, predictive scaling or a warm pool may be more reliable than purely reactive scaling. For a model that runs acceptably on both device types, a hybrid design can use always-on accelerator capacity for baseline load and CPU overflow for spikes, trading a different unit cost and latency profile for faster reserve capacity. Whether this GPU+CPU hybrid works depends on model compatibility and identical output semantics across runtimes.

Example 13.13: ResNet-50: Cost analysis
Table 18 gives an illustrative scenario for hourly cost, throughput, and per-million-image cost when serving ResNet-50; the H100 row uses the batch-32 rate of the chapter’s batching sweep:

Systems insight: At full utilization the H100 has the lowest unit cost, but its hourly rate is 7.5× the T4’s, so it wins only while traffic keeps it busy. Below about 3,000 img/s of sustained traffic, T4 capacity sized to the load costs less per image than one partly idle H100. Cloud pricing varies by region and changes over time; consult current pricing for production planning.

13.10.2 Capacity planning

The unit-cost comparison in table 18 evaluates each instance at its saturated throughput, but a live fleet cannot run at 100 percent utilization without pushing every arrival burst past the knee of the latency curve (figure 5). Determining how many replicas to provision therefore combines those per-device service rates with the queueing bounds from section 13.4. Capacity planning translates three inputs into replica counts: traffic patterns (peak request rate, daily and weekly cycles, growth projections), latency SLOs (p50, p95, p99 targets), and model service times across batch sizes (Harchol-Balter 2013).

Harchol-Balter, Mor. 2013. Performance Modeling and Design of Computer Systems: Queueing Theory in Action. Cambridge University Press. https://doi.org/10.1017/cbo9781139226424.

The ResNet-50 capacity notebook in section 13.4.3 ran this workflow for batch-one service, deriving an M/M/1 utilization threshold of 68 percent from equation 5 and arriving at 36 H100s with headroom for a 5,000 QPS target. The plan extends to batched service by the same arithmetic. Applying the same utilization factor to the saturated batched rate of 7,040 img/s in table 18 gives 2 H100 before headroom and 3 after; the batch-1 plan needed 27 and 36.

This arithmetic compares assumed capacities under different service models; it does not establish equal p99 latency. Batching changes service times, realized batch sizes, and waiting distributions, so the factor derived for unbatched M/M/1 service cannot by itself certify the batched plan. The throughput opportunity is the batching gain of section 13.5; its usable capacity requires a load test including formation delay and worker backlog. This scenario provisions peak load plus 30 percent headroom; production margins depend on the workload and policy. Autoscaling can reduce costs during low-traffic periods while meeting latency objectives during peaks; Infrastructure as code develops the operational policy layer around these serving calculations. Capacity must be sized for requests that meet the SLO rather than for raw request volume, and that sizing becomes multi-dimensional when each request is an autoregressive token stream constrained by TTFT, TPOT, and KV-cache capacity at once.

13.10.3 Production case study: Serving 8-billion-parameter Llama 3

A team that deploys an 8-billion-parameter Llama 3 chat service on a single H100 under a 200 ms TTFT and 20 ms TPOT budget must determine whether GPU memory capacity, prefill compute, or decode bandwidth caps the number of requests per hour. It must also determine what each million tokens cost. The serving bounds of section 13.9 become a dollar figure only when they meet a concrete workload, an SLO, and an hourly rental rate. This case study costs out that single-H100 deployment, reusing the decode floor and the cache budget derived earlier rather than re-deriving them. Unlike the 70-billion-parameter scale-up in figure 10, the 8-billion-parameter model fits on one GPU with substantial memory headroom, so batching, prompt prefill, and autoregressive decoding can be balanced on a single device without multi-node sharding.

Workload profile

Four fixed parameters anchor the latency, memory, and cost calculations for this single-GPU deployment:

  • Model: 8-billion-parameter Llama 3 (quantized to 4-bit using activation-aware weight quantization (AWQ); see Numerical Precision: Changing How Values Are Stored for quantization techniques) (Dubey et al. 2024; Lin et al. 2024).
  • Hardware: 1\(\times\) NVIDIA H100 SXM5 GPU (80 GB HBM3 device capacity, equivalent to 85.9 GB on the decimal basis used for tensor footprints, 3.35 TB/s bandwidth) (Choquette 2023).
  • Request characteristics: 1,000-token input prompt (Prefill), 256-token generated response (Decode).
  • Target SLOs: TTFT \(<\) 200 ms, TPOT \(<\) 20 ms.
Dubey, Abhimanyu, Abhinav Jauhri, Abhinav Pandey, Abhishek Kadian, et al. 2024. The Llama 3 Herd of Models. arXiv preprint arXiv:2407.21783.
Lin, Ji, Jiaming Tang, Haotian Tang, Shang Yang, Wei-Ming Chen, Wei-Chen Wang, Guangxuan Xiao, Xingyu Dang, Chuang Gan, and Song Han. 2024. “AWQ: Activation-Aware Weight Quantization for on-Device LLM Compression and Acceleration.” Proceedings of Machine Learning and Systems 6: 87–100.
Choquette, Jack. 2023. “NVIDIA Hopper H100 GPU: Scaling Performance.” IEEE Micro 43 (3): 9–17. https://doi.org/10.1109/mm.2023.3256796.

Latency deconstruction

The end-to-end request latency is governed by the two-phase execution model of autoregressive transformers, applying the TTFT and TPOT metrics defined in section 13.9.1. Prefill determines whether the user sees a prompt response quickly; decode determines whether the generated stream keeps moving after it starts.

Prefill phase (time to first token)

The model processes the 1,000-token prompt in parallel. Assuming 40 percent of peak compute efficiency during prefill, the model-side TTFT estimate is 41.9 ms, comfortably within the 200 ms SLO and leaving the remaining budget for network ingress, tokenization, and queueing.

Decode phase (time per output token)

The model generates 256 tokens sequentially. At batch one, this phase is memory-bandwidth bound: each step streams the 4 GB weight tensor from VRAM. A batched step amortizes that stream across its active sequences.

Dividing the 4 GB weight tensor by the 3.35 TB/s bandwidth yields the theoretical floor: \(\DecodeTokenTimeFloor\approx\) 1.2 ms. Adding an assumed residual per-layer dispatch cost after graph capture gives a modeled TPOT of 1.57 ms. This dispatch overhead reflects the cumulative cost of launching 8–10 discrete GPU kernels (RMSNorm, RoPE, projections, attention, multilayer perceptron (MLP)) across each of the model’s 32 layers. At batch size one, launching roughly 250–300 small kernels adds several milliseconds of CPU-GPU submission and driver overhead, which serving engines mitigate by capturing the decode graph into a single CUDA Graph. Generating all 256 tokens therefore takes 256 tokens \(\times\) 1.57 ms = 0.40 s.

Memory and throughput

With 4-bit weights occupying 4 GB, the remaining ~81.9 GB is available for the KV cache and runtime workspace, and the case study sets aside the cache reserve below while leaving the rest as workspace headroom. AWQ compresses the weights to 4 bits but leaves the KV cache in 16-bit precision, so each token still costs the approximately 0.131 MB of FP16 cache derived for this model in section 13.9.5.1. Dividing the 77.3 GB reserve by that per-token cost yields capacity for ≈ 0.6 million tokens, so at 1,256 tokens the GPU can hold a concurrent batch size of ~469 requests.

Unit economics

Consider a representative H100 SXM5 rental cost of approximately $3/hour and an assumed isolated prefill rate of 10,000 prompt tokens/s, deliberately below the rate implied by the model-side TTFT estimate above to allow for scheduling and kernel inefficiency. Steady-state request throughput cannot exceed either the rate at which the GPU prefills incoming prompts or the rate at which resident KV-cache slots turn over completed responses. Dividing the 10,000 prompt tokens/s prefill rate by the 1,000-token prompt bounds admissions to 10 requests/s. For this calculation, each completed request counts both prompt and generated output tokens, totaling 1,256 tokens. Multiplying a 10 requests/s limiting rate by this token count and the 3,600 seconds in an hour gives 45.2 million tokens/hour, and dividing the hourly rental cost by that throughput gives $0.066/million tokens.

To check whether KV-cache capacity imposes a tighter bound, Little’s Law converts the resident KV-cache slot count into a maximum request turnover rate by dividing the resident request count by the modeled time to complete prefill and decode. That calculation assumes that all resident requests can advance at the modeled batch-one pace, which is an optimistic concurrency ceiling rather than a measured decode capacity. Even under that optimistic assumption, the KV-residency turnover rate is much higher than 10 requests/s, so isolated prefill supplies the smaller rate and sets the displayed cost floor. Shared prefill and decode contention, batched decode timing, runtime workspace, and SLO-qualified throughput determine whether the service realizes that rate, because memory fit alone does not establish throughput. The resulting cost floor is a bound for this workload, and a load experiment must supply the missing service capacity before the same arithmetic can price an accepted deployment configuration.

Checkpoint 13.6: Complete the LLM capacity decision

For large language models, memory capacity (the size of the unallocated VRAM dedicated to the KV cache) determines maximum concurrent residency, while prefill compute and decode bandwidth determine realized token throughput and cost under a specific traffic mix. Memory bandwidth bounds low-batch decode latency; larger batches amortize parameter movement and shift the limit toward compute or KV-cache traffic. Physical constraints govern serving economics across the entire stack: latency budgets partition into prefill and decode phases, queueing theory dictates batch sizing and capacity headroom, and memory bandwidth bounds decode throughput. Misinterpreting these interactions leads directly to the operational fallacies and pitfalls that follow.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A team evaluates deploying a small classification model with an average arrival rate of 5 QPS and a 200 ms latency SLA. Measurements at 5 QPS show that both a single CPU server instance and a single GPU server instance meet the matched quality target and the p99 latency target of 200 ms. The CPU instance costs $0.20/hour and sustains up to 20 QPS under the target latency; the GPU instance costs $2.00/hour and sustains up to 200 QPS under the target latency. Which deployment option is more cost-effective for this specific workload, and why?

    1. The GPU server is more cost-effective because its 10x greater SLO-qualified peak throughput more than offsets its 10x higher hourly cost, making it the lower-cost choice even at 5 QPS.
    2. The GPU server is more cost-effective because GPUs always have lower total cost of ownership regardless of arrival traffic.
    3. The CPU server is more cost-effective because at 5 QPS, it already meets the SLA with adequate headroom at $0.20/hour, whereas the GPU server would use only 2.5% of its SLO-qualified capacity and cost about \(10\times\) more per served query at that traffic level.
    4. Neither option is viable because 5 QPS requires at least a 10-node cluster for high availability.

  2. In capacity planning for a mission-critical model serving cluster with a peak expected traffic of \(\lambda_{\text{peak}} = 1\text{,}000\text{ QPS}\) and an individual replica capacity of \(R = 100\text{ QPS}\) at its target latency SLO, why would a capacity engineer provision 16 to 20 replicas (\(1.6\times\text{--}2.0\times\) headroom multiplier) rather than exactly 10 replicas?

  3. True or False: For an 8-billion-parameter LLM with 32 layers, 8 KV heads, and head dimension 128, deployed in FP16 precision with a 16 GB weight footprint on an 80 GB GPU, serving long-context requests with batch size \(B = 8\) and context length \(S = 32\text{,}768\) tokens causes the KV cache to exceed the static weight footprint of the model itself.

  4. An engineer serves an 8-billion parameter LLM on a GPU with 2 TB/s of High-Bandwidth Memory (HBM) bandwidth. Assume the model weights are 16 GB in FP16, the decode step effectively streams those 16 GB of weights from HBM each token at batch size \(B = 1\), and ignore KV-cache traffic and compute time. What is the theoretical hardware-bandwidth lower bound on Time Per Output Token (TPOT)?

    1. \(8.0\text{ ms}\) (\(\text{TPOT} = 16\text{ GB} / 2\text{,}000\text{ GB/s}\))
    2. \(0.8\text{ ms}\) (\(\text{TPOT} = 1.6\text{ GB} / 2\text{,}000\text{ GB/s}\))
    3. \(80.0\text{ ms}\) (\(\text{TPOT} = 160\text{ GB} / 2\text{,}000\text{ GB/s}\))
    4. \(0.08\text{ ms}\) (\(\text{TPOT} = 16\text{ MB} / 2\text{,}000\text{ GB/s}\))

  5. When LLM decode at small batch sizes is dominated by moving model weights rather than by arithmetic throughput, why can INT4 weight-only quantization (e.g., AWQ or GPTQ) reduce Time Per Output Token (TPOT), and what limits the gain even if INT4 compute kernels provide no higher FLOP/s than FP16 Tensor Cores?

  6. Calculate the total memory footprint (in bytes) required to store the KV cache for a batch of \(B = 16\) requests, each with an active context length of \(S = 2\text{,}048\) tokens, for a model with \(L = 32\) transformer layers, \(H_{\text{kv}} = 8\) key-value attention heads (Grouped-Query Attention), head dimension \(D_{\text{head}} = 128\), stored in FP16 precision (\(P = 2\text{ bytes}\)).

13.11 Fallacies and Pitfalls

Serving inverts training priorities in ways that violate intuitions from batch processing. The nonlinear relationship between utilization and latency, the hidden costs of preprocessing, and the silent failure modes of training-serving skew cause violated SLOs, wasted optimization effort, and accuracy degradation invisible to standard monitoring.

Fallacy: Reducing model inference latency proportionally reduces user-perceived latency.

Engineers who optimize model inference expect proportional improvement in user-perceived latency, but serving systems introduce latency sources absent from offline benchmarks. Under load, queuing delay dominates: equation 4 shows that at 80 percent utilization with 3.5 ms service time, average wait time is 14.1 ms before inference even begins. Reducing inference from 3.5 ms to 1.8 ms changes service time but also shifts utilization from 80 percent to 41 percent, reducing queuing wait from 14.1 ms to 1.2 ms, a 12× queuing improvement that dwarfs the 1.7 ms inference gain.

This nonlinear interaction between inference speed and queuing behavior produces an apparent paradox. The system-level speedup (17.6 ms → 3.0 ms, or 6×) far exceeds the model-level speedup (3.5 ms → 1.8 ms, or 2×). At first glance, this superlinear improvement appears to contradict Amdahl’s law, which states that total system speedup is strictly bounded by the enhanced execution fraction. However, the fixed-fraction Amdahl calculation holds other stage costs unchanged; serving is a dynamic queueing system. Average queue waiting time scales nonlinearly with server utilization: \(\QueueWaitTime \propto \frac{\ServingUtilization}{1 - \ServingUtilization}\), where \(\ServingUtilization = \ArrivalRate \cdot \BatchServiceTime(1)\) for unbatched service. When kernel optimization cuts service time \(\BatchServiceTime(1)\), it both accelerates execution and lowers server utilization \(\ServingUtilization\). Pulling utilization away from saturation collapses queue waiting times nonlinearly. A kernel optimization therefore shortens the request twice, once in the service time and again in the queue that the lower utilization drains. Conversely, profiling inference latency in isolation without modeling arrival load conceals the primary driver of user-perceived performance.

Pitfall: Running serving infrastructure at high utilization to maximize cost efficiency.

Teams target 90 percent utilization to minimize idle capacity. In production, latency degrades nonlinearly as utilization approaches capacity. Equation 4 shows that at 90 percent utilization, average time in system reaches 10× service time. Moving from 70 percent to 90 percent utilization cuts modeled infrastructure cost by 22.2 percent but triples average latency. For a 3.5 ms inference service, modeled p99 latency rises from ~54.1 ms to ~162 ms under the M/M/1 assumptions. In this scenario, operating at 60 to 70 percent utilization preserves substantially more latency headroom than 90 percent. A production target must instead be selected from the measured arrival process, service-time distribution, SLO, failure reserve, and cost objective.

Fallacy: Training accuracy guarantees serving accuracy.

Engineers assume identical model weights preserve validation-set performance. In production, preprocessing differences can shift inputs outside the training distribution. Section 13.8.5 explains how resize interpolation, numerical precision, and feature timing can create skew despite identical weights. Validation accuracy holds only over the population it sampled (principle 9), and a serving path that transforms inputs differently presents the model with a different population. The illustrative scenario here drops from 95 percent to 90 percent, a 5 percentage-point loss; these values are not a reported production incident. Production systems require consistent preprocessing and task-quality monitoring because latency and exception checks cannot detect this failure.

Pitfall: Using average latency to evaluate serving system performance.

Engineers monitor average latency because it trends smoothly and is simple to compute. An average, however, cannot show whether the percentile named by an SLO is failing. As section 13.4.3 demonstrates, at 70 percent utilization with 3.5 ms service time, average latency is 11.8 ms but the modeled p99 reaches 54.1 ms, a 4.6× gap invisible to mean-based monitoring. Services therefore report the relevant latency distribution and SLO percentile rather than the mean alone.

Fallacy: Larger serving batches always improve throughput without affecting latency SLOs.

Engineers maximize batch size assuming GPU saturation improves cost efficiency under production load. In serving systems, however, batching introduces a latency-throughput trade-off governed by queuing dynamics absent from offline benchmarks. Accumulating requests into larger batches increases wait time for early arrivals. An oldest-request window of 10 ms can add up to 10 ms of formation wait if the batch-size trigger does not fire first. Waiting for an available worker can add further delay; the combined distribution determines p99 latency. For the chapter’s ResNet-50 profile on the H100 (section 13.5.5), increasing batch size from 16 to 32 raises saturated throughput by 77 percent and per-batch inference time only from 4.0 ms to 4.5 ms. At 500 QPS, however, filling the larger batch raises mean formation wait from 15 ms to 31 ms. Variable input sizes within a batch can also create padding overhead that wastes compute on padding tokens. Section 13.5.4 shows why, for tight p99 targets, larger batch sizes can violate SLOs when batch formation delay plus increased per-batch inference time exceeds the latency budget. Serving batch optimization requires jointly tuning batch size, batch timeout, and concurrency against latency SLOs under realistic traffic patterns, not maximizing throughput in isolation.

Pitfall: Calibrating quantization ranges with unrepresentative data.

Teams may calibrate with a convenient sample even when it does not represent the supported deployment distribution. Post-training quantization determines INT8 scale factors from activation ranges in calibration data, so representative coverage matters. The illustrative scenario here drops from 76.1 percent to 72.9 percent, a 3.2 percentage-point loss; it is not a reported production incident. Effective quantization requires calibration and validation on data representative of the inputs the service is expected to handle.

Fallacy: Cold start latency only matters for the first request.

Engineers optimize steady-state latency assuming most requests hit warm instances. Cold starts compound during traffic spikes, deployments, and recovery, which is exactly when capacity is most needed. In this illustrative scenario, 10 instances each require 30 s of compilation, totaling 300 instance-seconds. Parallel warming makes capacity useful after about 30 s, and a request that reaches a replica before warmup completes waits about 500 ms instead of 5 ms. Autoscaling policies that count only steady-state replicas therefore underestimate the capacity a spike requires. The scale-out budget must include compilation, weight loading, cache initialization, and health-check time (section 13.8.1). Warm pools of pre-initialized spare replicas, precompiled engines, staged model loading, or admission control keep new replicas from becoming another source of tail latency.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A team attempts to maximize hardware cost efficiency by targeting 95% steady-state utilization \(\rho\) across their production serving queue. Why is this strategy a dangerous engineering pitfall?

    1. High utilization permanently erases model weights from GPU memory registers.
    2. In stochastic serving systems, queue waiting times grow asymptotically toward infinity as utilization approaches 100%, causing minor traffic fluctuations to trigger massive tail-latency spikes and widespread SLO breaches.
    3. Running above 90% utilization forces the operating system to switch from 64-bit to 32-bit execution mode.
    4. Modern GPUs automatically shut down power when utilization exceeds 80% for more than 10 seconds.

  2. True or False: Evaluating an online serving system using mean (average) latency is sufficient for capacity planning, because if the average latency is well below the SLA target, user-facing requests are guaranteed to experience good performance.

  3. Why is the assumption that ‘cold-start latency only affects the very first request’ a critical fallacy in autoscaling serverless or Kubernetes-based serving environments with bursty traffic?

  4. An engineering team increases the dynamic batch size on their vision model serving nodes from 8 to 64. Offline peak throughput doubles, and production traces show batch-formation wait time grew sharply after the change. User-facing p99 latency violations then increase by 40%. What fallacy explains this outcome?

    1. Model weights become corrupted when executed with batch sizes greater than 16.
    2. GPU Tensor Cores disable INT8 acceleration when batch size exceeds 32.
    3. High batch sizes reduce arithmetic intensity on the GPU Roofline model.
    4. The fallacy that larger serving batches always improve throughput without affecting latency; early-arriving requests can spend too much of the latency budget waiting in the batcher before execution.

  5. Describe the pitfall of ‘Calibrating quantization ranges with unrepresentative data,’ and explain how it can lead to silent production failure despite passing offline validation checks.

See Answers →

13.12 Summary

Serving moves a validated model into an operating workload. Online requests add a latency distribution, queueing, traffic variability, and failure reserve to the throughput concerns familiar from training. Little’s Law relates stable long-run population, arrival rate, and response time; M/M/1 supplies an intentionally simple model of nonlinear latency growth near saturation. Neither replaces measurement, but together they turn headroom and capacity into explicit hypotheses that a load test can validate.

Effective serving optimization measures the complete request path rather than model inference alone. Protocol and serialization choices matter when transport is a visible fraction of the budget; preprocessing matters when it feeds an accelerator too slowly; batching matters only when arrivals and the SLO permit waiting. Training-serving skew adds a different failure mode. The service can remain fast and error-free while inconsistent transformations silently change its predictions.

The chosen deployment paradigm changes the feasible request path. A cloud API may use dynamic batching under a measured arrival process, synchronized sensors impose a frame deadline, and a single-stream mobile workload may choose batch one to protect responsiveness and energy. The MLPerf scenarios provide standardized approximations of these shapes; production design still begins with the service’s own traffic, hardware, and objective.

Node-level optimization connects the model’s operations to the machine that executes them. Profiling reveals whether compilation, kernel fusion, precision, locality, serialization, or loading actually binds the request path. Gains matter only when they survive production input shapes, accuracy checks, and latency variation. Token-streaming language models stretch the whole toolkit at once. On the chapter’s GPT-2 XL service, a batch-one decode step can be no faster than the 0.9 ms an H100 needs to read the model’s weights. Latency splits into time to first token and time per output token, batching must reschedule at every generated token, and the KV cache, not arithmetic, bounds how many requests can share an accelerator. Capacity planning converts that valid throughput into replica count and unit cost, while the Llama 3 case study in section 13.10.3 separates calculable capacity bounds from the load experiment needed to establish valid throughput. A faster kernel that neither reduces provisioned capacity nor improves the SLO has not produced an economic gain.

Every row of the chapter map (table 1) reduces to a few counting rules, collected in table 19. Each formula links an observable workload parameter—arrival rate, service time, batch window, weight volume, or hourly node rate—to the operational metric it governs. Together they let an engineer estimate a service’s latency budget, headroom, batch size, decode floor, and cache budget before a load test confirms them.

Key Takeaways: Inverting every training priority
  • Serving is latency economics: Training rewards throughput over long runs, but serving spends a fixed per-request budget across serialization, preprocessing, queuing, inference, postprocessing, and the network. Optimizing only model latency misses the stages users actually wait on.
  • Utilization turns into waiting: In the chapter’s M/M/1 approximation, average time in system rises from 5\(\times\) service time at 80 percent utilization to 10\(\times\) at 90 percent. Headroom keeps modest traffic surges from becoming SLO failures.
  • Fast models expose the rest of the pipeline: Once inference is fast, image decode, tokenization, and other preprocessing can dominate total latency. The binding optimization becomes the request path, not the neural network kernel.
  • Batching follows traffic, not habit: Poisson-modeled web arrivals can use dynamic batching, synchronized sensors need aligned batches, and single-stream mobile workloads may have little batching opportunity. A useful window converts available latency slack into throughput without violating the SLO.
  • Skew breaks accuracy without errors: Resize methods, color-channel order, normalization constants, or feature definitions that differ between training and serving can change the model’s effective input distribution. Shared transformations, parity tests, and production-slice monitoring reduce this risk.
  • Runtime choices become infrastructure cost: Precision, graph compilation, kernel fusion, and serving runtime translate directly into replica count and cost per inference. Quantization and specialized runtimes can materially reduce required serving capacity when they preserve accuracy and fit the target hardware.
  • LLM serving is memory management: Decode often reads weights from VRAM for every generated token, so token latency is bandwidth-bound unless batching changes the constraint. KV-cache layout, PagedAttention, continuous batching, precision, and runtime choice determine both concurrency and cost per token.

Training is judged by useful work completed; an online service is judged by whether requests meet their latency objective. Each stage draws on the same latency envelope: serialization, preprocessing, the queue, the model, and the response. Data determines input size and arrival shape, the algorithm determines compute and state, and the machine determines service rate and movement costs. Queueing couples all three nonlinearly, so a system within budget at moderate traffic can breach it after a small surge. Serving engineering therefore allocates the budget across the request path, preserves headroom for variability and failure, and verifies that the required fraction of requests finishes before the deadline, which is the latency budget principle (12) in operational form.

What’s Next: From fast to still correct
This chapter evaluates the request path under a specified workload: latency within budget, headroom preserved, and useful throughput converted into cost. Those performance checks do not establish whether the answers are still right. Training-serving skew showed that a service can stay fast and error-free while its predictions change, and drift produces the same silence when the world moves rather than the pipeline. ML Operations widens the unit of concern from request-path performance to the model’s operating lifetime. Monitoring tracks prediction quality alongside system health, feature stores and model registries keep serving aligned with training, and staged rollout with rollback lets a replacement model reach users without turning a bad release into an outage.

13.13 Self-Check Answers

Self-Check: Answer

  1. When transitioning a deep learning model from training to an online serving environment, how does the system constraint in the D·A·M (Data-Algorithm-Machine) taxonomy fundamentally shift regarding machine utilization and algorithm state?

    1. Machine utilization must be maximized at 100% to amortize capital costs, while the algorithm continues parameter backpropagation during request serving.
    2. Machine capacity must maintain operational headroom below saturation to prevent tail-latency queueing collapse, while the algorithm’s weights remain fixed for a deployed version.
    3. Machine allocation switches from accelerators to general-purpose CPUs exclusively, while the algorithm dynamically modifies its architecture per incoming user payload.
    4. Machine throughput replaces latency as the primary operational constraint, while data volume shifts from live request streams to historical offline batches.

    Answer: The correct answer is B. In online serving, the machine-side priority shifts from maximizing average utilization to preserving headroom, because queueing delay grows nonlinearly as utilization approaches saturation and can break tail-latency SLOs. At the same time, a deployed serving path runs forward-only inference on fixed model weights rather than updating them with backpropagation. The 100 percent utilization view fits training better than latency-sensitive serving, and serving does not imply abandoning accelerators or making throughput the primary constraint.

    Learning Objective: Analyze the fundamental architectural and operational shifts in the D·A·M taxonomy when transitioning machine learning models from offline training to online serving.

  2. True or False: In online model serving, the latency term (\(L_{\text{lat}}\)) in the iron law of ML systems must encompass external factors such as network round-trip time, request serialization, and system orchestration, rather than solely the accelerator kernel execution time.

    Answer: True. In an operational online serving system, user-perceived response time includes client-server network transport, ingress routing, payload serialization/deserialization, queue waiting time, and CPU preprocessing/postprocessing in addition to accelerator forward-pass execution. Consequently, optimizing only the raw accelerator kernel latency often yields diminishing returns if orchestration and transport overheads dominate the latency envelope.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate how end-to-end serving latency budgets extend beyond isolated accelerator kernel execution times.

  3. Explain why achieving high average throughput during offline benchmarking does not guarantee that an online serving system will satisfy its tail-latency Service-Level Objective (SLO) during production traffic spikes.

    Answer: A high offline throughput number shows what the model and runtime can sustain under the benchmarked conditions, but it does not by itself prove that live requests will meet a tail-latency SLO. In production, arrival timing, queueing, preprocessing, and network overhead all vary over time; a system that looks strong in a saturated or controlled benchmark can still miss p99 targets when bursts consume its headroom. The practical implication is that serving capacity must be validated under representative traffic and percentile-latency measurements, not inferred from throughput alone.

    Learning Objective: Explain why steady-state throughput benchmarks fail to predict tail-latency degradation under stochastic online arrival workloads.

  4. An e-commerce platform evaluates precomputing product recommendations offline (static inference) versus computing them on demand when a user visits the homepage (dynamic inference). Which trade-off correctly characterizes static inference compared to dynamic inference?

    1. Static inference eliminates database storage costs but increases real-time p99 latency by executing large batched matrix multiplications during user page loads.
    2. Static inference guarantees real-time contextual adaptation to intra-session user clicks but requires dedicated high-end GPUs on the critical request path.
    3. Static inference trades storage capacity and potential prediction staleness for retrieval latency that avoids per-request model execution, whereas dynamic inference provides fresh predictions from live context at the expense of computational latency and variable serving capacity.
    4. Static inference requires running unbatched single-sample inferences across microcontrollers, whereas dynamic inference processes large offline shards across cloud clusters.

    Answer: The correct answer is C. Static inference precomputes predictions offline and serves the request from stored results, so it trades extra storage and possible staleness for lookup-based serving that avoids live model execution on the critical path. Dynamic inference instead uses fresh live context, but each request must pay the compute and capacity cost of on-demand serving. The claim that static inference removes storage gets the trade-off backward, and precomputed outputs cannot react immediately to intra-session changes.

    Learning Objective: Compare the trade-offs between static precomputation and dynamic on-demand inference regarding latency, freshness, storage, and compute provisioning.

  5. Why does bare-metal TinyML serving on microcontrollers (e.g., ARM Cortex-M) preclude the use of standard cloud serving frameworks (like Triton or vLLM), and what architectural adaptations are required?

    Answer: Microcontrollers operate under a very different runtime envelope from cloud servers: they have tightly constrained SRAM working memory, weights often live in flash, and deployment may run bare-metal or on a small RTOS rather than a full server OS. A Cortex-M target therefore uses a compact embedded runtime with preplanned buffers and a firmware-style execution path instead of a cloud serving stack built for networked processes, dynamic scheduling, and large accelerator memory. In practice, this often pushes engineers toward ahead-of-time compilation, static allocation, and compact quantized models, even though the exact numeric format and hardware features vary by device.

    Learning Objective: Analyze the hardware and runtime constraints that distinguish bare-metal TinyML deployment from networked cloud serving architectures.

← Back to Questions

Self-Check: Answer
  1. **Order the sequential stages of a request passing through a high-performance decoupled inference serving pipeline from initial arrival to client response:
  1. Hardware Accelerator Execution (tensor kernel execution on GPU/NPU)
  2. Dynamic Batcher (aggregating incoming items up to max batch size or timeout)
  3. Network Ingress & Gateway (TLS termination, authentication, protocol deserialization)
  4. Postprocessing & Response Dispatch (logit normalization, top-\(k\) filtering, response serialization)
  5. Request Queue (absorbing traffic bursts and buffering pending requests)
  6. Inference Runner / Engine (tensor memory management, CUDA stream scheduling)**

Answer: The correct order is: 1. (3) Network Ingress & Gateway (TLS termination, authentication, protocol deserialization) 2. (5) Request Queue (absorbing traffic bursts and buffering pending requests) 3. (2) Dynamic Batcher (aggregating incoming items up to max batch size or timeout) 4. (6) Inference Runner / Engine (tensor memory management, CUDA stream scheduling) 5. (1) Hardware Accelerator Execution (tensor kernel execution on GPU/NPU) 6. (4) Postprocessing & Response Dispatch (logit normalization, top-\(k\) filtering, response serialization)

Explanation: A request arrives at the Network Ingress for TLS termination and protocol handling, buffers in the Request Queue during bursts, gets grouped with other concurrent requests by the Dynamic Batcher, is prepared and scheduled by the Inference Runner onto GPU streams, executes on the Hardware Accelerator, and finally undergoes Postprocessing and response serialization before returning to the client.

Learning Objective: Design the end-to-end request pipeline of a modern inference server by ordering its architectural stages.

  1. In a microservices architecture where measurements show JSON serialization and parsing are a visible bottleneck in an internal fraud detection service processing \(10^5\) queries per second under a 5 ms SLA, why is gRPC with Protocol Buffers preferred over HTTP/1.1 with JSON?

    1. JSON text parsing is performed directly inside GPU Tensor Cores, whereas Protocol Buffers require host CPU preprocessing.
    2. HTTP/1.1 provides automatic iteration-level continuous batching, whereas gRPC only supports static batching.
    3. Protocol Buffers eliminate the need for schema definitions, enabling zero-copy dynamic typing across heterogeneous languages.
    4. Protocol Buffers use compact binary encoding with pre-compiled schema parsers that can reduce CPU serialization overhead, and gRPC multiplexes requests over persistent HTTP/2 connections.

    Answer: The correct answer is D. When serialization is a visible part of the latency budget, compact binary messages and generated parsers can cut host CPU overhead relative to JSON, and gRPC’s HTTP/2 transport supports multiplexing over long-lived connections. HTTP/1.1 can also use persistent connections, so the point is reduced host-side overhead and better transport behavior under this workload, not an inherent handshake on every request. JSON parsing happens on host CPUs, not Tensor Cores, and batching behavior is a serving-engine decision rather than a transport feature.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate interface protocols and serialization formats (REST/JSON vs. gRPC/Protobuf) for latency-critical serving pipelines.

  2. What is the ‘serialization bottleneck’ in model serving, and under what operational conditions (model size, request payload, query rate) does it become the dominant factor in the end-to-end latency budget?

    Answer: The serialization bottleneck appears when packing or unpacking request payloads on the host CPU takes enough time that it rivals or exceeds the model’s own execution time. This is most likely with lightweight or highly optimized models, large tensor payloads or feature vectors, and high query rates where repeated parsing and allocation consume a noticeable share of CPU time. The practical implication is that teams should confirm the bottleneck with per-stage timing before changing protocols or formats, rather than assuming serialization dominates every serving path.

    Learning Objective: Analyze the conditions under which serialization overheads dominate model inference execution in serving architectures.

  3. True or False: Replacing HTTP/1.1 REST endpoints with gRPC automatically accelerates the GPU kernel execution time of deep learning models by \(2\times\) to \(4\times\).

    Answer: False. Changing the transport protocol does not automatically make the model’s GPU kernels run faster; with the same tensors and backend, the underlying math kernels are unchanged. Protocol changes can still improve end-to-end behavior by reducing CPU and transport overhead or by easing contention elsewhere in the serving stack.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate transport/serialization layer optimizations and distinguish them from accelerator computation in model serving pipelines.

  4. In an inference server architecture, what is the primary role of decoupling the request queue from the dynamic batcher?

    1. It allows the server to permanently cache all intermediate GPU activations across independent client requests.
    2. It allows the system to buffer irregular arrivals and present the batcher with pending work so it can form batches within a timeout budget.
    3. It eliminates the need for GPU kernel compilation by converting dynamic input shapes into fixed static tensors in the queue.
    4. It bypasses host RAM by streaming network packets directly from the NIC into the accelerator’s L2 cache via PCIe peer-to-peer transfers.

    Answer: The correct answer is B. Separating the request queue from the batcher gives the system a bounded buffer between arrival and execution, so short bursts can be absorbed and the batcher can choose when to dispatch based on queue depth and timeout. That does not guarantee no drops under overload, and it does not guarantee an optimal batch every time. Queueing also does not eliminate compilation work or imply a specific zero-copy network path to the GPU.

    Learning Objective: Explain the function of queue decoupling and dynamic batching in inference server design.

  5. A high-throughput model serving cluster sits behind a Layer 7 load balancer. Which routing approach is most appropriate for reducing p99 tail latency when inference times vary significantly across requests due to variable input sequence lengths?

    1. A load-aware policy such as least-connections or least-loaded routing, which steers new requests away from replicas that already appear busy or backlogged.
    2. Static round-robin routing, which deterministically rotates incoming connections across all healthy replicas regardless of current queue depths.
    3. Random routing with hash-based sticky IP assignment, which pins client IP addresses permanently to specific backends without monitoring load.
    4. Maximum-throughput greedy allocation, which concentrates all incoming traffic onto a single primary replica until its memory is completely saturated before spilling over.

    Answer: The correct answer is A. When request service times vary, routing should account for current load or backlog so new work is less likely to land behind long-running requests. A load-aware policy can reduce tail latency if its load and health signals are accurate. Round-robin ignores current queue depth, sticky hashing can trap traffic on a slow replica, and intentionally saturating one node drives the exact queue growth that serving tries to avoid.

    Learning Objective: Design load balancing and request routing strategies to mitigate tail latency under variable service-time distributions.

Self-Check: Answer

  1. An image classification service has an end-to-end SLA of 30 ms. Assume the request path is a serial pipeline with unchanged non-GPU stages, no introduced overhead from the optimization itself, and no queueing feedback effects. The request path consists of: network ingress/egress (8 ms), CPU image decoding and normalization (10 ms), GPU forward pass (8 ms), and postprocessing/top-\(k\) filtering (2 ms). If an engineer optimizes the GPU model forward pass to run in 4 ms (a \(2\times\) speedup), what is the new total latency and what architectural principle explains the resulting overall speedup?

    1. Total latency decreases from 28 ms to 24 ms (a 14.3% latency reduction, about a \(1.17\times\) speedup), demonstrating Amdahl’s law where unchanged serial stages bound the system gains.
    2. Total latency decreases from 28 ms to 14 ms (a 50% overall improvement), because GPU acceleration propagates linearly across all pipeline stages.
    3. Total latency remains 28 ms because dynamic batching automatically inserts artificial delay to fill GPU occupancy.
    4. Total latency increases to 32 ms due to host-device synchronization overhead incurred by faster kernel launches.

    Answer: The correct answer is A. The original serial path is \(8 + 10 + 8 + 2 = 28\text{ ms}\). Cutting only the GPU stage from 8 ms to 4 ms yields \(8 + 10 + 4 + 2 = 24\text{ ms}\), which is a \(14.3\%\) latency reduction and about a \(1.17\times\) end-to-end speedup. This is the serving form of Amdahl’s law: if the other stages are unchanged and no new queueing or overhead is introduced, they bound the total gain.

    Learning Objective: Calculate end-to-end latency improvements using Amdahl’s Law across multi-stage serving pipelines.

  2. What is the ‘killer microseconds’ problem in low-latency ML serving systems, and why do standard operating system scheduling and hardware primitives struggle to handle it efficiently?

    Answer: The ‘killer microseconds’ problem refers to operations that take between \(1\,\mu\text{s}\) and \(100\,\mu\text{s}\) (such as fast tensor copies, micro-batch inference, or inter-process IPC). This timescale falls into an architectural gap: it is too long to waste CPU cycles in busy-wait polling/spinlocks, but too short to amortize the overhead of operating system thread context switches, interrupts, and kernel transitions (which cost several microseconds), resulting in high CPU overhead or significant latency jitter.

    Learning Objective: Analyze the system trade-offs underlying the killer microseconds problem in low-latency inference runtimes.

  3. True or False: For computer vision models utilizing standard 2D convolutions or self-attention mechanisms, increasing the input image resolution from \(224 \times 224\) to \(448 \times 448\) quadruples the number of input pixels (\(4\times\)), which results in an approximately \(4\times\) increase in FLOPs for standard convolutional layers and up to a \(16\times\) increase for unwindowed full self-attention layers, assuming fixed channels, patch size, and architecture.

    Answer: True. Doubling height and width multiplies the pixel or token grid by \(4\times\). With fixed channels, kernel sizes, patching, and no architecture changes, standard convolution arithmetic scales roughly with the number of spatial positions, giving about \(4\times\) more work, while the pairwise attention term in full unwindowed self-attention scales with the square of token count, giving up to \(16\times\) more work. That statement is about arithmetic scaling, not a guarantee that total latency rises by exactly the same factor.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate computational complexity and latency scaling across varying input resolutions in vision serving systems.

  4. A classification serving pipeline returns only the top-5 predictions to the client. Why is it often better to run top-\(k\) selection on the GPU before copying results back to the host?

    1. It retrains the model online so fewer logits must be stored in GPU memory.
    2. It guarantees lower latency in every deployment because GPUs always execute small reductions faster than CPUs.
    3. It reduces the payload that crosses PCIe from the full logit vector to just a few scores and indices, though the host must still budget the synchronization and copy needed to materialize those results.
    4. It avoids the need for any host-side postprocessing or response formatting after inference finishes.

    Answer: The correct answer is C. Performing top-\(k\) on the device can shrink the result that crosses PCIe from a large logit tensor to a small set of scores and class indices, which often helps when transfer size matters. The trade-off is that materializing those results on the host still requires synchronization and a device-to-host copy, so the optimization reduces one serving cost rather than removing postprocessing entirely. A claim that GPUs are always faster for every such reduction ignores workload and runtime details, and top-\(k\) does not retrain the model.

    Learning Objective: Analyze how GPU-side postprocessing can reduce transfer overhead while still requiring host-side synchronization in the serving path.

  5. In a high-throughput vision serving pipeline, engineers overlap CPU image preprocessing of request \(N+1\) with GPU inference of request \(N\) using multiple CUDA streams and pinned host memory buffers, thereby increasing accelerator ____ without modifying the underlying model architecture.

    Answer: duty cycle. Overlapping host preprocessing with device execution can raise the fraction of wall-clock time during which the accelerator has active kernels running, improving throughput without changing the model itself. That is a statement about active time, not achieved FLOP/s rate, and it does not guarantee the GPU stays fully busy all the time because the benefit still depends on whether upstream stages can keep feeding it.

    Learning Objective: Apply request pipelining and double-buffering techniques to maximize accelerator duty cycle.
